Relative Cluster Categories and Higgs categories
Yilin Wu

Abstract
. Cluster categories were introduced in 2006 by Buan-Marsh-Reineke-Reiten-Todorov in order to categorify acyclic cluster algebras without coefficients. Their construction was generalized by Amiot (2009) and Plamondon (2011) to arbitrary cluster algebras associated with quivers. A higher dimensional generalization is due to Guo (2011). Cluster algebras with coefficients are important since they appear in nature as coordinate algebras of varieties like Grassmannians, double Bruhat cells, unipotent cells, etc. The work of Geiss-Leclerc-Schröer often yields Frobenius exact categories which allow us to categorify such cluster algebras. In this paper, we generalize the construction of (higher) cluster categories by Claire Amiot and by Lingyan Guo to the relative context. We prove the existence of an n n -cluster tilting object in a Frobenius extriangulated category, namely the Higgs category (generalizing the Frobenius categories of Geiss-Leclerc-Schröer), which is stably n n -Calabi–Yau and Hom-finite, arising from a left ( n + 1 ) (n+1) -Calabi–Yau morphism. Our results apply in particular to relative Ginzburg dg algebras coming from ice quivers with potential and higher Auslander algebras associated to n n -representation-finite algebras.



1.
Introduction

Almost 20 years ago, Fomin and Zelevinsky
[
13
]
invented cluster algebras in order to create a combinatorial framework for the study of canonical bases
[
31
,
48
]
in quantum groups and the study of total positivity in algebraic groups. Since the combinatorics of cluster algebras are very complicated, it is useful to model them categorically, so that more conceptual tools become available.

Consider a cluster algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
without frozen variables and such that one of the clusters has an acyclic quiver
Q
. Buan–Marsh–Reineke–Reiten–Todorov
[
6
]
introduced the cluster category
{\mathcal{C}}_{Q}
, given by the orbit category

{\mathcal{C}}_{Q}={\mathcal{D}}^{b}(kQ)/\tau\Sigma^{-1},

where
\tau
denotes the Auslander-Reiten translation of the derived category
{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(kQ)
and
\Sigma
the shift functor on
{\mathcal{D}}^{b}(kQ)
. It was shown to be triangulated by a result of Keller
[
37
]
.

Claire Amiot
[
3
]
generalized the construction of the cluster category to finite-dimensional algebras
A_{0}
of global dimension
\leqslant 2
. In her approach, in order to show that there is a triangle equivalence between
\mathcal{C}_{A_{0}}
, constructed as a triangulated hull
[
37
]
, and the quotient category
\mathrm{per}\,\bm{\Pi}_{3}(A_{0})/\mathrm{pvd}\,\bm{\Pi}_{3}(A_{0})
, where
\bm{\Pi}_{3}(A_{0})
is the 3-Calabi–Yau completion
[
40
]
of
A_{0}
, she first studied the category
\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}=\mathrm{per}\,\Pi/\mathrm{pvd}\,(\Pi)
associated to a dg algebra
\Pi
with the following four properties:

1)

\Pi
is homologically smooth,

2)

\Pi
is connective, i.e. the cohomology of
\Pi
vanishes in degrees
>0
,

3)

\Pi
is 3-Calabi–Yau as a bimodule,

4)

H^{0}(\Pi)
is finite-dimensional.

She proved that the category
\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}
is Hom-finite and
2
-Calabi–Yau. Moreover, the image of the free dg module
\Pi
is a cluster tilting object in
\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}
whose
endomorphism algebra is the zeroth homology of
\Pi
. Later, Lingyan Guo
[
18
]
generalized Amiot’s construction to finite-dimensional algebras
A
of global dimension
\leqslant n
and to dg algebras satisfying
1)
,
2)
,
4)
and
n
-Calabi–Yau as a bimodule.

For cluster algebras with non invertible frozen variables, there is a natural model for this purpose, a Frobenius category
{\mathcal{E}}
, i.e. an exact category with enough projective and injective objects, and such that the projective and injective objects coincide. Then by definition, each projective-injective object
I
satisfies

\mathrm{Ext}^{i}_{{\mathcal{E}}}(?,I)=\mathrm{Ext}^{i}_{{\mathcal{E}}}(I,?)=0\,\,\text{for}\,\,i>0.

Thus, each projective-injective object
I
is in
\mathrm{add}T
for any cluster-tilting object
T\in{\mathcal{E}}
. By a result of Happel
[
19
]
, the stable category
\underline{{\mathcal{E}}}
, formed by taking the quotient by the ideal of morphisms factoring through a projective-injective object, is a triangulated category. The corresponding stable category
{\mathcal{E}}
is 2-Calabi–Yau if there is a bifunctorial duality

\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{{\mathcal{E}}}(X,Y)\cong D\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{{\mathcal{E}}}(Y,X)

for all
X,Y\in{\mathcal{E}}
.

Relative right Calabi–Yau structures were invented by Bertrand Toën in
[
60
, pp. 227-228]
. Then, relative right and left Calabi–Yau structures were studied by Chris Brav and Tobias Dyckerhoff in
[
8
]
. A relative left
n
-Calabi–Yau structure on a morphism
f\colon B\rightarrow A
between smooth dg algebras is the datum of a class
[\xi]
in negative cyclic homology
H\!N_{n}(f)
inducing certain dualities in
\mathcal{D}(B^{e})
and
\mathcal{D}(A^{e})
(see Section
3
). In particular, if the dg algebra
B
is zero, then
A
is
n
-Calabi–Yau as a bimodule. A canonical way to produce relative left Calabi–Yau structures is the deformed relative Calabi–Yau completion which was introduced by Yeung (
[
63
]
). This generalized Keller’s construction
[
40
]
of deformed
n
-Calabi–Yau completions to the relative context.

The main aim of this paper is to generalize the construction of Claire Amiot and Lingyan Guo to the relative context. We change the above properties of the dg algebra
A
to the following properties on a dg algebra morphism
f\colon B\rightarrow A
(not necessarily preserving the unit)

1)

A
and
B
are homologically smooth,

2)

A
is connective, i.e. the cohomology of
A
vanishes in degrees
>0
,

3)

the morphism
f\colon B\to A
has a left
(n+1)
-Calabi–Yau structure,

4)

H^{0}(A)
is finite-dimensional.

Then we introduce the relative cluster category
\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
associated with
f\colon B\rightarrow A
and show that it is Hom-finite under the above assumptions. We prove the existence of an
n
-cluster tilting object in the Higgs category
\mathcal{H}
which is an extension closed subcategory of
\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
and is stably
n
-Calabi–Yau as a Frobenius extriangulated category in the sense of
[
50
]
. However, there are many cases where
H^{0}(A)
is infinite-dimensional. Then the corresponding relative cluster category is Hom-infinite. Hom-infinite cluster categories were studied by Plamondon in
[
52
]
. Similarly, it would be interesting to explore Hom-infinite relative cluster categories. As this is not needed for the results in this paper, we leave it for future work.

The structure of the paper is as follows. In Section
2
, we give some background material on dg categories and their invariants. Section
3
is devoted to giving the definitions of relative Calabi–Yau structures and relative Calabi–Yau completions, and proving Proposition
3.18
, where we obtain a reduced version of the deformed relative Calabi–Yau completion for a dg functor between finitely cellular type dg categories. We also discuss the relation between relative Calabi–Yau completions and absolute Calabi–Yau completions, see Proposition
3.19
. Let
f\colon B\to A
be a morphism (not necessarily preserving the unit element) between dg
k
-algebras and let
e=f(\boldmath{1}_{B})
. In Section
4
, under the above assumptions on
f
, we define the
relative
n
-cluster category

\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
as the Verdier quotient of the perfect derived category
\mathrm{per}A
by the full subcategory
\mathrm{pvd}_{B}(A)
of the perfectly valued derived category
\mathrm{pvd}(A)
formed by the dg modules whose restriction to
B
is acyclic (see Definition
4.1
).
The relation between the triangulated categories involved can be summarized by the following commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathrm{per}(eAe)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{per}(eAe)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{pvd}_{B}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\mathrm{per}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{rel}}
\scriptstyle{p^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{pvd}(\overline{A})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{per}(\overline{A})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}_{n}(\overline{A}),}

where
\overline{A}
is the homotopy cofiber of
f\colon B\rightarrow A
, and the rows and columns are exact sequences of triangulated categories.

In Section
5
, we define the
relative fundamental domain

\mathcal{F}^{rel}
as a certain extension closed full subcategory of
\mathrm{per}A
(see Definition
5.12
). As in
[
3
]
and
[
18
]
, the canonical quotient functor
\pi^{rel}\colon\mathrm{per}A\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
induces a fully faithful embedding
\pi^{rel}\colon\mathcal{F}^{rel}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
(see Proposition
5.20
). Then the
Higgs category

\mathcal{H}
is defined as the image of
\mathcal{F}^{rel}
in
\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
(see Definition
5.22
). We show that it is closed under extensions in
\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
(see Proposition
5.39
) and thus becomes an extriangulated category in the sense of
[
50
]
. More precisely, we prove the following theorem.

Theorem 1.1
.

\mathrm{(Theorem~\ref{Higgs is Frobenius extrianglated category}\ and\ Proposition~\ref{addA is an n-cluster tiliting})}

The Higgs category
\mathcal{H}
is a Frobenius extriangulated category with projective-injective objects
\mathcal{P}=\mathrm{add}(eA)
and
\mathrm{add}A
is an
n
-cluster-tilting subcategory of
\mathcal{H}
with
\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{H}}(A)=H^{0}(A)
. Moreover, the quotient functor
p^{*}\colon\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{n}(\overline{A})
induces an equivalence of triangulated categories

\mathcal{H}/[\mathcal{P}]\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathcal{C}_{n}(\overline{A}),

where
[\mathcal{P}]
denotes the ideal of morphisms of
\mathcal{H}
which factor through objects in
{\mathcal{P}}
.

In
[
43
]
, for
n=3
, we will define and study cluster characters on the Higgs category and the relative cluster category.

We have the following results related to
n
-angulated categories.

Theorem 1.2
.

\mathrm{(Theorem~\ref{addA is Frobenius n-exangulated category})}

Suppose that the
n
-cluster tilting category
\mathrm{add}\overline{A}
of
{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\overline{A})
satisfies

\Sigma^{n}\mathrm{add}\overline{A}=\mathrm{add}\overline{A}.

Then
\mathrm{add}\overline{A}
carries a canonical
(n+2)
-angulated structure. Moreover, the
n
-cluster-tilting subcategory
\mathrm{add}A
of
{\mathcal{H}}
carries a canonical structure of Frobenius
n
-exangulated category with projective-injective objects
\mathcal{P}=\mathrm{add}(eA)
. The quotient functor
p^{*}\colon\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{n}(\overline{A})
induces an equivalence of
(n+2)
-angulated categories

\mathrm{add}A/[\mathcal{P}]\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathrm{add}(\overline{A}).

In Section
6
, under the hypotheses
1)
-
4)
, when the dg algebra
A
is concentrated in degree 0, we show that
H^{0}(A)
is of global dimension at most
n+1
so that we have the equivalence

\mathcal{D}^{b}(\mathrm{mod}H^{0}A)\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathrm{per}A.

Moreover,
H^{0}A
is internally bimodule
(n+1)
-Calabi–Yau with respect to the idempotent
e=f(\boldmath{1}_{B})
in the sense of Pressland (see
[
54
]
) and restriction induces an equivalence from the Higgs category
\mathcal{H}
to the category of Gorenstein projective modules over
B^{\prime}=eH^{0}(A)e
. More precisely, we have the following theorem.

Theorem 1.3
.

\mathrm{(Theorem~\ref{Higgs of stalk})}

Let
f\colon B\rightarrow A
be a dg algebra morphism which satisfies the hypotheses
1)
-
4)
. Let
e=f(\boldmath{1}_{B})
. Moreover, we assume that
A
is concentrated in degree
0
. Then we have

a)

The algebra
B^{\prime}=eH^{0}(A)e
is Iwanaga-Gorenstein of injective dimension at most
g\leqslant n+1
as a
B^{\prime}
-module.

b)

Under the equivalence
\mathcal{D}^{b}(\mathrm{mod}H^{0}A)\simeq\mathrm{per}A
, the subcategory
\mathcal{F}^{rel}
corresponds to the subcategory
\mathrm{mod}_{n-1}(H^{0}A)
of
H^{0}A
-modules of projective dimension at most
n-1
.

c)

Via the equivalence
\mathrm{res}\colon\mathcal{D}^{b}(\mathrm{mod}H^{0}A)\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathrm{per}A
, the localization
\pi^{rel}\colon\mathrm{per}A\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
identifies with the restriction functor
\mathcal{D}^{b}(\mathrm{mod}H^{0}A)\rightarrow\mathcal{D}^{b}(\mathrm{mod}B^{\prime})
, i.e. we have a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}^{b}(\mathrm{mod}H^{0}A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}^{b}(\mathrm{mod}B^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{per}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B).}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle\sim

d)

Under the equivalence
\mathcal{D}^{b}(\mathrm{mod}B^{\prime})\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
, the Higgs category
\mathcal{H}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
corresponds to the subcategory
\mathrm{gpr}B^{\prime}
of Gorenstein projective modules over
B^{\prime}=eH^{0}(A)e
. In particular, when
B^{\prime}
is self injective, we have
\mathcal{H}\cong\mathrm{mod}B^{\prime}
.

e)

Let
{\mathcal{M}}=\mathrm{add}A\subset{\mathcal{H}}
. Then the exact sequence of triangulated categories

0\rightarrow\mathrm{pvd}_{B}(A)\rightarrow\mathrm{per}A\rightarrow{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(A,B)\rightarrow 0

is equivalent to

0\rightarrow{\mathcal{K}}^{b}_{{\mathcal{H}}-ac}({\mathcal{M}})\rightarrow{\mathcal{K}}^{b}({\mathcal{M}})\rightarrow{\mathcal{D}}^{b}({\mathcal{H}})\rightarrow 0.

In particular, the relative cluster category
{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(A,B)
is equivalent to the bounded derived category
{\mathcal{D}}^{b}({\mathcal{H}})
of
{\mathcal{H}}
.

We summarize the situation in the following commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathrm{per}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{rel}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}^{b}(\mathrm{mod}H^{0}A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{res}
\textstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}^{b}(\mathrm{mod}B^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\mathrm{mod}H^{0}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{res}
\textstyle{\mathrm{mod}B^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{mod}_{n-1}(H^{0}A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\mathrm{gpr}B^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\mathcal{F}^{rel}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\mathcal{H}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\,.}

The paradigmatic example for
A
is the relative 3-Calabi–Yau completion of the Auslander algebra of a Dynkin quiver
Q
(cf. below). Then
B^{\prime}
is the preprojective algebra of
Q
and
\mathcal{H}
is equivalent to the module category of
B^{\prime}
. This motivates the terminology “Higgs category” because Higgs bundles
[
22
,
57
]
are the geometric version of modules over preprojective algebras.

In Section
7
, we apply this general approach to Jacobi-finite ice quivers with potential. In this way, for each Jacobi-finite ice quiver with potential
(Q,F,W)
, we associate a Frobenius extriangulated category
\mathcal{H}
endowed with a canonical cluster-tilting object (see Theorem
7.10
).

In the last section, we apply our main result to higher Auslander-Reiten theory. Let
B_{0}
be an
n
-representation-finite algebra in the sense of Iyama-Oppermann
[
27
]
. Let
\tau_{n}^{-1}
be the higher inverse Auslander-Reiten translation of
B_{0}
and let
A_{0}\coloneqq\mathrm{End}_{B_{0}}(\oplus_{i\geqslant 0}\tau_{n}^{-i}B_{0})
be the higher Auslander algebra of
B_{0}
.

Then there is a natural fully faithful morphism

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 17.29341pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-17.29341pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{f_{0}\colon B_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 17.29341pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@hook{1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 41.29341pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 41.29341pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A_{0}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

The relative
(n+2)
-Calabi–Yau completion of
f_{0}

f\colon B=\bm{\Pi}_{n+1}(B_{0})\longrightarrow A=\bm{\Pi}_{n+2}(A_{0},B_{0})

satisfies the assumptions 1)-4) and
A
is concentrated in degree 0. Moreover,
H^{0}(f)
is fully faithful (see Proposition
8.14
). Let
\widetilde{B_{0}}
denote the higher preprojective algebra of
B_{0}
in the sense of Iyama-Oppermann
[
27
]
. We give a new proof (see Lemma
8.16
) of the fact, first proved in
[
27
]
, that

\widetilde{B_{0}}
is a self-injective algebra. By our main results in Section
5
and Section
6
, we have the following theorem.

Theorem 1.4
.

\mathrm{(Theorem~\ref{CTO in n-finite case})}

Consider the relative cluster category
\mathcal{C}_{n+1}(A,B)
associated with
f\colon B\rightarrow A
.

a)

The Higgs category
\mathcal{H}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{n+1}(A,B)
is equivalent to
\mathrm{mod}(\widetilde{B_{0}})
and the image of
A
in
\mathcal{H}
is an
(n+1)
-cluster-tilting object.

b)

We have a triangle equivalence
\underline{\mathrm{mod}}(\widetilde{B_{0}})\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathcal{C}_{n+1}(A_{0}/A_{0}eA_{0})
, where
e=f_{0}(\boldmath{1}_{B_{0}})
. In particular,
\underline{\mathrm{mod}}(\widetilde{B_{0}})
contains a canonical
(n+1)
-cluster-tilting object.

Notice that
b)
is the main result of
[
27
]
. We deduce it from
a)
thereby giving a new proof which is fundamentally different from that of
[
27
]
.

Notation and conventions

Throughout this paper,
k
will denote an algebraically closed field. We denote by
D=\mathrm{Hom}_{k}(-,k)
the
k
-dual. All modules are right modules unless stated otherwise. We say an algebra
A
is Noetherian if it is Noetherian as both a left and right module over itself. We denote by
gf
the composition of morphisms (or arrows)
f\colon X\rightarrow Y
and
g\colon Y\rightarrow Z
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be an additive category. We say that a morphism
f\colon X\rightarrow Y
is
right minimal
if any morphism
g\colon X\rightarrow X
satisfying
fg=f
is an isomorphism. Dually we define a
left minimal
morphism. For a collection
\mathcal{X}
of objects in
\mathcal{T}
, we denote by
\mathrm{add}\,\mathcal{X}
the smallest full subcategory of
\mathcal{T}
which is closed under finite coproducts, summands and isomorphisms and contains
\mathcal{X}
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a subcategory of
\mathcal{T}
. We say that a morphism
f\colon X\rightarrow Y
is a
right
\mathcal{X}
-approximation
of
Y
if
X\in\mathcal{X}
and
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{T}}(X^{\prime},f)
is surjective for any
X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}
. We say that
\mathcal{X}
is
contravariantly finite
if any object in
\mathcal{T}
has a right
\mathcal{X}
-approximation. Dually, we define a
left
\mathcal{X}
-approximation
and a
covariantly finite
subcategory. We say that
\mathcal{X}
is
functorially finite
if it is contravariantly and covariantly finite.

When
\mathcal{T}
is a triangulated category, we denote by
\Sigma
the shift functor and by
\mathrm{thick}\,\mathcal{X}
the smallest thick subcategory of
\mathcal{T}
containing
\mathcal{X}
. For collections
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
of objects in
\mathcal{T}
, we denote by
\mathcal{X}*\mathcal{Y}
the collection of objects
Z\in\mathcal{T}
appearing in a triangle
X\rightarrow Z\rightarrow Y\rightarrow\Sigma X
with
X\in\mathcal{X}
and
Y\in\mathcal{Y}
. We set

\mathcal{X}^{\perp_{\mathcal{T}}}\coloneqq\{T\in\mathcal{T}\,|\,\mathrm{Hom}(\mathcal{X},T)=0\},

{}^{\perp_{\mathcal{T}}}\mathcal{X}\coloneqq\{T\in\mathcal{T}\,|\,\mathrm{Hom}(T,\mathcal{X})=0\}.

When it does not cause confusion, we will simply write
\mathcal{X}^{\perp}
and
{}^{\perp}\mathcal{X}
.

Let
{\mathcal{T}}
be a triangulated category. For two objects
X
and
Y
of
{\mathcal{T}}
and an integer
n
, by
\mathrm{Hom}_{{\mathcal{T}}}(X,\Sigma^{>n}Y)=0
(respectively,
\mathrm{Hom}_{{\mathcal{T}}}(X,\Sigma^{\geqslant n}Y)=0
,
\mathrm{Hom}_{{\mathcal{T}}}(X,\Sigma^{<n}Y)=0
,
\mathrm{Hom}_{{\mathcal{T}}}(X,\Sigma^{\leqslant n}Y)=0
), we mean

\mathrm{Hom}_{{\mathcal{T}}}(X,\Sigma^{i}Y)=0

for all
i>n
(respectively, for all
i\geqslant n
,
i<n
,
i\leqslant n
).

Let
{\mathcal{X}}
be a full subcategory of
{\mathcal{T}}
. We say that
{\mathcal{X}}
is a thick subcategory of
{\mathcal{T}}
if it is a triangulated subcategory of
{\mathcal{T}}
which is closed under taking direct summands. In this case we denote by
{\mathcal{T}}/{\mathcal{X}}
the triangle quotient of
{\mathcal{T}}
by
{\mathcal{X}}
. In general, we denote by
\mathrm{thick}\,_{{\mathcal{T}}}{\mathcal{X}}
(or simply
\mathrm{thick}\,{\mathcal{X}}
) the smallest thick subcategory of
{\mathcal{T}}
which contains
{\mathcal{X}}
.

2.
Preliminaries

We recall some basic definitions related to dg categories and their invariants. We refer to Keller’s ICM address
[
38
]
for the details.

A
differential graded or dg category
is a
k
-category
\mathcal{A}
whose morphism spaces are dg
k
-modules and whose compositions
\mathcal{A}(y,z)\otimes\mathcal{A}(x,y)\to\mathcal{A}(x,z)
are morphisms of dg
k
-modules. We denote the category of all (small) dg categories over
k
by
\mathrm{dgcat}_{k}
. In particular, dg categories with one object can be identified with dg algebras
A
, i.e. graded
k
-algebras endowed with a differential
d
such that the Leibniz rule holds

\textstyle{d(f\circ g)=d(f)\circ g+(-1)^{p}f\circ d(g)}

for all
f\in A^{p}
and all
g
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a dg category. The
opposite dg category

\mathcal{A}^{op}
has the same objects as
\mathcal{A}
and its morphisms are defined by

\mathcal{A}^{op}(X,Y)=\mathcal{A}(Y,X);

the composition of
f\in\mathcal{A}^{op}(Y,X)^{p}
with
g\in\mathcal{A}^{op}(Z,Y)^{q}
is given by
(-1)^{pq}gf
.
The category
Z^{0}(\mathcal{A})
has the same objects as
\mathcal{A}
and its morphisms are defined by

(Z^{0}\mathcal{A})(X,Y)=Z^{0}(\mathcal{A}(X,Y)),

where
Z^{0}
is the kernel of
d\colon\mathcal{A}(X,Y)^{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}(X,Y)^{1}
. The category
H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
has the same objects as
\mathcal{A}
and its morphisms are defined by

(H^{0}(\mathcal{A}))(X,Y)=H^{0}(\mathcal{A}(X,Y)),

where
H^{0}
denotes the 0-th homology of the complex
\mathcal{A}(X,Y)
. We say that a morphism
f\colon x\to y
in
Z^{0}({\mathcal{A}})
is a
homotopy equivalence
if it becomes invertible in
H^{0}({\mathcal{A}})
.

2.1.
The category of dg categories

Let
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
be dg categories. A
dg functor

G\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}

is given by a map
G\colon\mathrm{obj}(\mathcal{B})\to\mathrm{obj}(\mathcal{A})
and by morphisms of dg
k
-modules
G(x,y)\colon\mathcal{B}(x,y)\to\mathcal{A}(Gx,Gy),x,y\in\mathrm{obj}(\mathcal{B})
,
compatible with the composition and the units. The category of small dg categories
\mathrm{dgcat}_{k}
has the small dg categories as objects and the dg functors as morphisms.

The
tensor product

\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}
has the class of objects
\mathrm{obj}(\mathcal{A})\times\mathrm{obj}(\mathcal{B})
and the morphism spaces

\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B}((x,y),(x^{\prime},y^{\prime}))=\mathcal{A}(x,x^{\prime})\otimes\mathcal{B}(y,y^{\prime})

with the natural compositions and units. The
enveloping dg category
of
\mathcal{A}
is defined as
\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A}^{op}
and we denote it by
\mathcal{A}^{e}
.

Let
G,G^{\prime}\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
be two dg functors. We define
\mathcal{H}om(G,G^{\prime})^{n}
to be the
k
-module formed by the families of morphisms

\displaystyle\phi_{x}\in\mathcal{A}(Gx,G^{\prime}x)^{n}

such that
G^{\prime}(f)\circ\phi_{x}=(-1)^{pn}\phi_{y}\circ G(f)
for all
f\in\mathcal{B}^{p}(x,y)
. We define
\mathcal{H}om(G,G^{\prime})
to be the graded
k
-module with components
\mathcal{H}om(G,G^{\prime})^{n}
and whose differential is induced by the differential of
\mathcal{A}(Gx,G^{\prime}x)
. The set of morphisms
G\to G^{\prime}
is by definition in bijection with
Z^{0}(\mathcal{H}om(G,G^{\prime}))
. Thus, we can form a dg category
\mathcal{H}om(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A})
, which has the dg functors as objects and the morphism space
\mathcal{H}om(G,G^{\prime})
for two dg functors
G
and
G^{\prime}
.

Endowed with the tensor product, the category
\mathrm{dgcat}_{k}
becomes a symmetric tensor category which admits an internal Hom-functor, i.e.

\displaystyle\mathrm{Hom}(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C})=\mathrm{Hom}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H}om(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C})),

for
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{dgcat}_{k}
.

Definition 2.1
.

A
quasi-equivalence
is a dg functor
G\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
such that

(1)

G(x,y)\colon\mathcal{B}(x,y)\to\mathcal{A}(G(x),G(y))
is a quasi-isomorphism for all objects
x,y
of
\mathcal{A}
;

(2)

the induced functor
H^{0}(G)\colon H^{0}(\mathcal{B})\to H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
is an equivalence.

By
[
58
]
, there is a model structure on
\mathrm{dgcat}_{k}
with weak equivalences being quasi-equivalences. We denote by
\rm Ho(\mathrm{dgcat}_{k})
the corresponding homotopy category.

Theorem 2.2
.

[
58
, Theorem 0.1]

Let
k
be a commutative ring. There is a cofibrantly generated model structure (Dwyer-Kan model structure) on
\mathrm{dgcat}_{k}
where a dg functor
G\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
is

•

a weak equivalence if
G
is quasi-equivalence;

•

a fibration if

1. for all objects
x,y\in\mathcal{B}
the component
G(x,y)
is a degreewise surjection of chain complexes;

2. for each isomorphism
G(x)\to z
in
H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
there is a lift to an isomorphism in
H^{0}(\mathcal{B})
.

2.2.
Drinfeld dg quotients

Let
{\mathcal{B}}\subseteq{\mathcal{A}}
be a full dg subcategory. Denote by
j\colon{\mathcal{B}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}
the inclusion.

Definition 2.3
.

[
12
, Section 3]

The dg quotient category
{\mathcal{A}}/{\mathcal{B}}
is defined as follows:

•

\mathrm{obj}({\mathcal{A}}/{\mathcal{B}})=\mathrm{obj}({\mathcal{A}})
;

•

freely add new morphisms
\epsilon_{U}\colon U\rightarrow U
of degree
-1
for each
U\in\mathrm{obj}({\mathcal{B}})
, and set
d(\epsilon_{U})=\boldmath{1}_{U}
.

We denote by
p\colon{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}/{\mathcal{B}}
the canonical functor. For any objects
x
and
y
, we have a decomposition of graded
k
-modules

{\mathcal{A}}/{\mathcal{B}}(x,y)=\bigoplus_{n\geqslant 0}\bigoplus_{U_{i}\in\mathrm{obj}({\mathcal{B}})}{\mathcal{A}}(U_{n},y)\otimes_{k}k\epsilon_{U_{n}}\otimes_{k}\cdots\otimes_{k}k\epsilon_{U_{2}}\otimes_{k}{\mathcal{A}}(U_{1},U_{2})\otimes_{k}k\epsilon_{U_{1}}\otimes_{k}{\mathcal{A}}(x,U_{1}).

Using the formula
d(\epsilon_{U})=\boldmath{1}_{U}
, one can easily get the differential on
{\mathcal{A}}/{\mathcal{B}}(x,y)
.

Let
G\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
be a dg functor. The
homotopy cofiber

\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{B}

[
59
, Remark 4.0.2]
of
G
is defined by the following homotopy push-out diagram in
\mathrm{dgcat}_{k}
with respect to the Dwyer-Kan model structure

\textstyle{\mathcal{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{G}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{B},}

where
0
is the dg category of one object with endomorphism space being
0
.
We will call
{\mathcal{B}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}/{\mathcal{B}}
a
homotopy cofiber sequence
in
\mathrm{dgcat}_{k}
.

The homotopy cofiber
\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{B}
can be computed as the Drinfeld dg quotient of
\mathcal{A}
by its full dg subcategory
\mathrm{Im}(G)
, where
\mathrm{Im}(G)
is the full dg subcategory of
\mathcal{A}
whose objects are the
y\in\mathcal{A}
such that there exist an object
x
in
\mathcal{B}
and an isomorphism
G(x)\cong y
in
\rm H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
.

2.3.
Homotopy between dg functors

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a small dg category. The
dg category

P(\mathcal{B})
is defined as follows: its
objects

are the homotopy equivalences
f\colon x\to y
. The complexes of
morphisms
are defined (as
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
k
-modules) by:

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 95.1609pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-95.1609pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{P(\mathcal{B})(f,g)=\mathcal{B}(x,w)\oplus\mathcal{B}(y,z)\oplus\mathcal{B}(x,z)[-1]}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

where
f\colon x\to y
,
g\colon w\to z
are in
P(\mathcal{B})
.

A homogeneous element of degree
r
of this graded
k
-module can be represented by a matrix

\left[\begin{matrix}m_{1}&0\\
h&m_{2}\end{matrix}\right],

where
m_{1}\in\mathcal{B}(x,w)^{r}
,
m_{2}\in\mathcal{B}(y,z)^{r}
and
h\in\mathcal{B}(x,z)^{r-1}
.

The differential is given by

\displaystyle d\left\{\left[\begin{matrix}m_{1}&0\\
h&m_{2}\end{matrix}\right]\right\}=\left[\begin{matrix}d(m_{1})&0\\
d(h)+gm_{1}-(-1)^{r}(m_{2}f)&d(m_{2})\end{matrix}\right].

The Composition in
P(\mathcal{B})
corresponds to matrix multiplication and the units to the identity matrices.

Then we have a dg inclusion functor

\textstyle{I\colon\mathcal{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{P(\mathcal{B})}

which sends an object
x
in
\mathcal{B}
to
(x=x)
and
I(f)=\left[\begin{matrix}f&0\\
0&f\end{matrix}\right]
.

Moreover we have two projection functors

\displaystyle P_{0},P_{1}\colon P(\mathcal{B})\to\mathcal{B}

which are defined as follows

\displaystyle P_{0}(f\colon x\to y)=x

\displaystyle P_{0}\left\{\left[\begin{matrix}m_{1}&0\\
h&m_{2}\end{matrix}\right]\right\}=m_{1};

\displaystyle P_{1}(f\colon x\to y)=y

\displaystyle P_{1}\left\{\left[\begin{matrix}m_{1}&0\\
h&m_{2}\end{matrix}\right]\right\}=m_{2}.

Then we obtain the following commutative diagram in
\mathrm{dgcat}_{k}
(
[
59
, Proposition 2.0.11]
)

\textstyle{\mathcal{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bigtriangleup=(id_{\mathcal{B}},id_{\mathcal{B}})}
\scriptstyle{I}
\textstyle{\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{B}}
\textstyle{P(\mathcal{B})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{P_{0}\times P_{1}}
,

where
I
is a quasi-equivalence and
P_{0}\times P_{1}
is a trivial fibration, with respect to the Dwyer-Kan model structure on
\mathrm{dgcat}_{k}
.
This means that the dg category
P(\mathcal{B})
is a path object for
\mathcal{B}
.

Definition 2.4
.

[
59
, Remark 2.0.12]
Let
G,G^{\prime}\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
be two dg functors, where
\mathcal{B}
is a cofibrant dg category. Two dg functors
G
and
G^{\prime}
are homotopic if there exists a dg functor
H\colon\mathcal{B}\to P(\mathcal{A})
that makes the following diagram commute

\textstyle{\mathcal{A}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{G}
\scriptstyle{G^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{H}
\textstyle{P(\mathcal{A})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{P_{0}}
\scriptstyle{P_{1}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}.}

The dg functor
H
corresponds exactly to

•

a homotopy equivalence
\alpha(x)\colon G(x)\to G^{\prime}(x)
in
\mathcal{A}
for every object
x
in
\mathcal{B}
, and

•

a degree
-1
morphism

h=h(x,y)\colon\mathcal{B}(x,y)\to\mathcal{A}(G(x),G^{\prime}(y))
, for all objects
x
and
y
in
\mathcal{B}
, such that

\alpha(y)G(f)-G^{\prime}(f)\alpha(x)=d(h(f))+h(d(f))

and

h(fg)=h(f)G(g)+(-1)^{n}G^{\prime}(f)h(g),

where
f
and
g
are composable morphisms in
\mathcal{B}
with
f
of degree
n
.

2.4.
The derived category of a dg category

Let
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
be small dg categories. Let
{\mathcal{C}}_{dg}(k)
be the dg category of
k
-complexes
[
38
, Section 2.2]
.
A
left dg
\mathcal{A}
-module
is a dg functor
L\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{C}_{dg}(k)
. A
right dg
\mathcal{A}
-module
is a dg functor
M\colon\mathcal{A}^{op}\to\mathcal{C}_{dg}(k)
. A
dg
\mathcal{A}
-
\mathcal{B}
-bimodule
is a dg functor
N\colon\mathcal{B}^{op}\otimes\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{C}_{dg}(k)
. For each object
X
of
{\mathcal{A}}
, we have the right module represented by
X

X^{\wedge}={\mathcal{A}}(?,X).

The
category of right dg modules

\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A})
has as objects the right dg
\mathcal{A}
-modules and as morphisms
L\rightarrow M
the morphisms of dg functors.

We identify
\mathcal{A}
-bimodules with right
\mathcal{A}^{e}
-modules via the morphism

\displaystyle M\otimes\mathcal{A}^{e}=M\otimes\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A}^{op}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{A}^{op}\otimes M\otimes\mathcal{A}

taking
m\otimes a\otimes b
to
(-1)^{|b|(|m|+|a|)}b\otimes m\otimes a
, and we denote by
{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{A}^{e})
the category of
\mathcal{A}
-bimodules. There is a distinguished
\mathcal{A}
-bimodule
\mathcal{A}_{\triangle}
given by morphisms in the category
\mathcal{A}
, i.e.
\mathcal{A}_{\triangle}(x,y)=\mathcal{A}(x,y)
. We call it the
diagonal bimodule of
\mathcal{A}
and still denote it by
\mathcal{A}
.

A bimodule
M\in{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{A}}^{e})
is said to be
semi-free
if there is a set of homogeneous elements
{\xi_{i}\in M(x_{i},y_{i})},\,i\in S
, called a basis of
M
, such that, for any pair
(x,y)\in\mathrm{obj}({\mathcal{A}})\times\mathrm{obj}({\mathcal{A}})
, every object
\eta\in M(x,y)
can be written uniquely as a finite sum

\eta=\Sigma_{i\in S}f_{i}\circ\xi_{i}\circ g_{i},

where
g_{i}\in{\mathcal{A}}(x,x_{i})
and
f_{i}\in{\mathcal{A}}(y_{i},y)
and only finitely many of them are nonzero. When the basis set is finite, its cardinality is called the
rank
of the semi-free module
M
.

The dg category
{\mathcal{C}}_{dg}({\mathcal{A}})
is defined by
{\mathcal{C}}_{dg}({\mathcal{A}})={\mathcal{H}}om({\mathcal{A}}^{op},{\mathcal{C}}_{dg}(k))
. We write
{\mathcal{H}}om(L,M)
for the complex of morphisms from
L
to
M
in
{\mathcal{C}}_{dg}({\mathcal{A}})
. For each
X\in{\mathcal{A}}
, we have a natural isomorphism

{\mathcal{H}}om(X^{\wedge},M)\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}M(X).

A morphism
f\colon L\rightarrow M
is a
quasi-isomorphism
if it induces an isomorphism in homology. Then the
derived category

\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})
is the localization of the category
\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A})
with respect to the class of quasi-isomorphisms. The
category of perfect objects
per
(\mathcal{A})
associated with
\mathcal{A}
is the closure in
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})
of the set of representable functors
X^{\wedge}=\mathcal{A}(?,X)
,
X\in\mathcal{A}
, under shifts in both directions, extensions and taking direct factors. The
category of perfectly valued modules

\mathrm{pvd}(\mathcal{A})
is the full subcategory of
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})
formed by the dg modules
M
such that each dg
k
-module
M(X)
,
X\in\mathcal{A}
, is perfect, i.e.
\sum_{p}\dim_{k}H^{p}(M(X))
is finite.

Let
f\colon\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
be a dg functor. Then
f
induces the restriction functor
f_{*}\colon\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{B})
which is given by
f_{*}(M)=M\circ f
. It fits into the usual triple of adjoint functors
(f^{*},f_{*},f^{!})
between
\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A})
and
\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{B})
. We denote the corresponding adjoint functors between
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})
and
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{B})
by
(\mathbf{L}f^{*},f_{*},\mathbf{R}f^{!})
.

The functor
f
also induces a dg functor
f^{e}\colon{\mathcal{B}}^{e}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}^{e}
between their enveloping dg categories. By abuse of notation, we also denote the corresponding adjoint functors between
\mathcal{D}({\mathcal{A}}^{e})
and
\mathcal{D}({\mathcal{B}}^{e})
by
(\mathbf{L}f^{*},f_{*},\mathbf{R}f^{!})
. For a dg
{\mathcal{B}}
-bimodule
M
, we have the following useful formula

\begin{split}\mathbf{L}f^{*}(M)=&M\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}{\mathcal{A}}^{e}\\
\cong&{\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}}M\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathcal{A}}.\end{split}

In particular, if we take
M={\mathcal{B}}
, then
\mathbf{L}f^{*}({\mathcal{B}})\cong{\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathcal{A}}
.

Definition 2.5
.

A dg category
\mathcal{A}
is said to be
(homologically) smooth
if the diagonal bimodule
\mathcal{A}
is perfect as a module over
\mathcal{A}^{e}
, i.e.
\mathcal{A}
is in per
(\mathcal{A}^{e})
.

Definition 2.6
.

A dg category
\mathcal{A}
is said to be
proper
if
{\mathcal{A}}(X,Y)\in\mathrm{per}k
for all objects
(X,Y)\in{\mathcal{A}}^{e}
.

Definition 2.7
.

For any right
\mathcal{A}^{e}
-module
M
, we define its
derived dual

M^{\vee}
in
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}^{e})
as

\displaystyle M^{\vee}=\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}^{e}}(M,\mathcal{A}^{e}).

In particular, the
\emph{inversedualizingbimodule}
of
\mathcal{A}
is defined as
\mathcal{A}^{\vee}
.

Definition 2.8
.

Let
G\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
be dg functor between dg categories. The
inverse dualizing bimodule of
G
in
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}^{e})
is defined as

\displaystyle\Theta_{G}=\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}^{e}}(\mathrm{Cone}(\mathcal{A}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}}\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}),\mathcal{A}^{e}).

Definition 2.9
.

[
38
, Section 4.5]

A dg category
{\mathcal{A}}
is called
strictly pretriangulated (=spt)
if it satisfies the following:

•

each object has a suspension, and
\Sigma\colon{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}
is dg dense (i.e. every object in the target category is dg isomorphic to some object in the image);

•

each closed morphism of degree zero has a cone.

Proposition 2.10
.

[
36
, Lemma 2.3]

Let
{\mathcal{A}}
be a spt dg category. Then
Z^{0}({\mathcal{A}})
has a canonical Frobenius exact structure, whose stable category coincides with
H^{0}({\mathcal{A}})
. Therefore,
H^{0}({\mathcal{A}})
is canonically triangulated.

Definition 2.11
.

The
pretriangulated hull

{\mathcal{A}}^{pretr}
is the smallest dg subcategory of
{\mathcal{C}}_{dg}({\mathcal{A}})
containing
{\mathcal{A}}
, closed under
\Sigma^{\pm}
and cones. As
{\mathcal{A}}^{pretr}
is spt, the
triangulated hull

{\mathcal{A}}^{tr}
of
{\mathcal{A}}
is defined to be
H^{0}({\mathcal{A}}^{pretr})
.

Theorem 2.12
.

[
12
, Theorem 3.4]

Let
{\mathcal{A}}
be a dg category and
{\mathcal{B}}\subseteq{\mathcal{A}}
a full dg subcategory. Then the canonical functor

{\mathcal{A}}^{tr}/{\mathcal{B}}^{tr}\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}({\mathcal{A}}/{\mathcal{B}})^{tr}

is a triangle equivalence.

2.5.
Relative Hochschild homology

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a dg category. The
\emph{barresolution}

(C^{bar}(\mathcal{A}),b^{\prime})
of the diagonal bimodule
\mathcal{A}
is the dg
\mathcal{A}
-bimodule whose value at
(x,y)
is given by the total complex of the bicomplex whose
(n,j)
-th entry is

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 185.31764pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-185.31764pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C_{n}^{bar}(x,y)^{(j)}\coloneqq\oplus_{x_{0},\cdots,x_{n-1}}\{\mathcal{A}(x_{n-1},y)\otimes_{k}\mathcal{A}(x_{n-2},x_{n-1})\otimes_{k}\cdots\otimes_{k}\mathcal{A}(x_{0},x_{1})\otimes_{k}\mathcal{A}(x,x_{0})\}^{(j)}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

where the horizontal arrows are given by the Hochschild differential

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 112.36246pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-112.36246pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{d_{0}(a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n})\coloneqq\Sigma_{i=0}^{n-1}\pm a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{i}a_{i+1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

and the vertical arrows are the differentials of the tensor products.

The augmentation is the morphism of bimodules

\textstyle{\epsilon_{\mathcal{A}}\colon C^{bar}(\mathcal{A})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}}

which is

\textstyle{\epsilon_{x,y}\colon\oplus_{z\in Obj(\mathcal{A})}\mathcal{A}(z,y)\otimes_{k}\mathcal{A}(x,z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}(x,y)\colon f\otimes f^{{}^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{f\circ f^{{}^{\prime}}}

and 0 everywhere else.
C^{bar}(\mathcal{A})
is a cofibrant replacement of
\mathcal{A}
in the category of
\mathcal{A}
-bimodules.

Definition 2.13
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a small dg category. Then the
Hochschild complex
of
\mathcal{A}
is defined as

\textstyle{H\!H(\mathcal{A})=\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}^{e}}C^{bar}(\mathcal{A})}

and the
Hochschild homology

H\!H_{\bullet}({\mathcal{A}})
of
\mathcal{A}
is the homology of this complex. More precisely,

\textstyle{H\!H(\mathcal{A})=\bigoplus_{m\geqslant 0}\{\bigoplus_{(x_{0},x_{1},\cdots,x_{m})\in Obj(\mathcal{A})}\mathcal{A}(x_{m},x_{0})\otimes(\Sigma\mathcal{A}(x_{m-1},x_{m}))\otimes\cdots\otimes(\Sigma\mathcal{A}(x_{0},x_{1}))\}}

We denote by
b
the differential of
H\!H(\mathcal{A})
.

Let
G\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
be dg functor. Then
G
induces a canonical morphism of
\mathcal{B}
-bimodules
G_{\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A}}\colon C^{bar}(\mathcal{B})\to C^{bar}(\mathcal{A})
and we have the following commutative diagram of
\mathcal{B}
-bimodules

\textstyle{\mathcal{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{G}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}}
\textstyle{C^{bar}(\mathcal{B})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{G_{\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A}}}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon_{\mathcal{B}}}
\textstyle{C^{bar}(\mathcal{A})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces.}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon_{\mathcal{A}}}

Thus, we have a canonical morphism of Hochschild complexes

\textstyle{\gamma_{G}\colon H\!H(\mathcal{B})=\mathcal{B}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}^{e}}C^{bar}(\mathcal{B})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{G\otimes G_{\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}^{e}}C^{bar}(\mathcal{A})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{can}
\textstyle{H\!H(\mathcal{A})=\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}^{e}}C^{bar}(\mathcal{A})}

Definition 2.14
.

The
Hochschild homology

H\!H_{\bullet}(G)
of the dg functor
G\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
is the homology of the
relative Hochschild complex
which is defined as follows

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 85.0495pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-85.0495pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{H\!H(G)=\mathrm{Cone}(\gamma_{G}\colon H\!H(\mathcal{B})\to H\!H(\mathcal{A}))}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

2.6.
Mixed complexes

Let
\Lambda
be the dg algebra generated by an indeterminate
\epsilon
of chain degree
-1
with
\epsilon^{2}=0
and
d\epsilon=0
. The underlying complex of
\Lambda
is

\cdots 0\rightarrow 0\rightarrow k\epsilon\rightarrow k\rightarrow 0\cdots.

It carries the structure of graded commutative Hopf
algebra with coproduct given by
\Delta(\epsilon)=1\otimes\epsilon+\epsilon\otimes 1
.

Then a
mixed complex
over
k
is a dg right
\Lambda
-module whose underlying dg
k
-module is
(M,b)
and where
\epsilon
acts by
B
. Suppose that
M=(M,b,B)
is a mixed complex. Then the
shifted mixed complex

\Sigma M
is the mixed complex such that
(\Sigma M)_{p}=M_{p-1}
for all
p
,
b_{\Sigma M}=-b
and
B_{\Sigma M}=-B
. Let
f\colon M\rightarrow M^{\prime}
be a morphism of mixed complexes. Then the
mapping cone
over
f
is the mixed complex

\bigg(M^{\prime}\oplus M,\begin{bmatrix}b_{M^{\prime}}&f\\
0&-b_{M}\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}B_{M^{\prime}}&0\\
0&-B_{M}\end{bmatrix}\bigg).

We denote by
{\mathcal{M}}ix
the category of mixed complexes and by
{\mathcal{D}}{\mathcal{M}}ix
the derived category of the dg algebra
\Lambda
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a dg category. We associate a precyclic chain complex
C({\mathcal{A}})
(see
[
47
]
) with
{\mathcal{A}}
as follows: For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, its
n
-th term is

\coprod{\mathcal{A}}(x_{n},x_{0})\otimes{\mathcal{A}}(x_{n-1},x_{n})\otimes{\mathcal{A}}(x_{n-2},x_{n-1})\otimes\cdots\otimes{\mathcal{A}}(x_{0},x_{1}),

where the sum runs over all sequences
x_{0},\ldots,x_{n}
of objects of
{\mathcal{A}}
. The degeneracy maps are given by

d_{i}(f_{n},\ldots,f_{i},f_{i-1},\ldots,f_{0})=\left\{\begin{aligned} (f_{n},\ldots,f_{i}f_{i-1},\ldots,f_{0})&&\text{if $i>0$,}\\
(-1)^{n+\sigma}(f_{0}f_{n},\ldots,f_{1})&&\text{if $i=0$},\end{aligned}\right.

where
\sigma=(\mathrm{deg}f_{0})(\mathrm{deg}f_{1}+\cdots+\mathrm{deg}f_{n-1})
. The cyclic operator is given by

t(f_{n-1},\ldots,f_{0})=(-1)^{n+\sigma}(f_{0},f_{n-1},f_{n-2},\cdots,f_{1}).

Then we associate a mixed complex
(M(\mathcal{A}),b,B)
with this precyclic chain complex as follows: The underlying dg module of
M({\mathcal{A}})
is the mapping cone over
(1-t)
viewed as a morphism of complexes

1-t\colon(C({\mathcal{A}}),b^{\prime})\rightarrow(C({\mathcal{A}}),b),

where
b=\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{i}d_{i}
and
b^{\prime}=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}(-1)^{i}d_{i}
.
Its underlying module is
C({\mathcal{A}})\oplus C({\mathcal{A}})
; it is endowed with the grading whose
n
-th component is
C({\mathcal{A}})_{n}\oplus C({\mathcal{A}})_{n-1}
and the differential is

\begin{bmatrix}b&1-t\\
0&-b^{\prime}\end{bmatrix}.

The operator
B\colon M({\mathcal{A}})\rightarrow M({\mathcal{A}})
is

\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
N&0\end{bmatrix},

where
N=\sum_{i=0}^{n}t^{i}
.

Definition 2.15
.

The
cyclic homology

H\!C_{\bullet}({\mathcal{A}})
of
\mathcal{A}
is defined to be the homology of the
cyclic chain complex
of
{\mathcal{A}}

H\!C({\mathcal{A}})=M({\mathcal{A}})\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{\Lambda}k.

The
negative cyclic homology

H\!N_{\bullet}({\mathcal{A}})
of
\mathcal{A}
is defined to be the homology of the
negative cyclic chain complex
of
{\mathcal{A}}

H\!N({\mathcal{A}})=\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{\Lambda}(k,M({\mathcal{A}})).

Remark 2.16
.

The dg algebra
\Lambda
is the singular homology with coefficients in
k
of the circle
S^{1}
. The circle action is captured algebraically in terms of the structure of a mixed complex so that the above constructions can be explained as homotopy orbit and homotopy fixed points of the Hochschild complex
C_{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})
with the algebraic circle action (see
[
32
,
47
,
23
]
).

The augmentation morphism
\Lambda\to k
induces natural morphisms in
{\mathcal{D}}(k)

H\!N({\mathcal{A}})\rightarrow H\!H({\mathcal{A}})\rightarrow H\!C({\mathcal{A}}).

Let
G\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
be a dg functor. It induces a canonical morphism
\gamma_{G}\colon M(\mathcal{B})\to M(\mathcal{A})
between their mixed complexes. We denote by
M(G)
the mapping cone over
\gamma_{G}
.

Definition 2.17
.

The
cyclic homology

H\!C_{\bullet}(G)
of
G\colon{\mathcal{B}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}
is defined to be the homology of the
cyclic chain complex group
of
G

H\!C(G)=M(G)\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{\Lambda}k.

The
negative cyclic homology

H\!N_{\bullet}(G)
of
G\colon{\mathcal{B}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}
is defined to be the homology of the
negative cyclic chain complex
of
G

H\!N(G)=\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{\Lambda}(k,M(G)).

Similarly, the augmentation morphism
\Lambda\to k
induces natural morphisms in
{\mathcal{D}}(k)

H\!N(G)\rightarrow H\!H(G)\rightarrow H\!C(G).

Theorem 2.18
.

[
36
, Theorem 1.5]

Let
{\mathcal{A}}
and
{\mathcal{B}}
be dg categories. Let
G\colon{\mathcal{B}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}
be a
Morita functor
, i.e. a dg functor such that
G_{*}\colon{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{B}})\rightarrow{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{A}})
is an equivalence. Then
\gamma_{G}\colon M({\mathcal{B}})\rightarrow M({\mathcal{A}})
is an isomorphism in
{\mathcal{D}}(\Lambda)
.

3.
Relative Calabi–Yau structures

3.1.
Reminder on the derived category of morphisms

Let
I
be the path
k
-category of
the quiver
1\to 2
. The letter
I
stands for ‘interval’. Let
{\mathcal{A}}
be a dg
k
-category. The objects of the derived category

{\mathcal{D}}(I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{A}})
identify with morphisms
f\colon M_{1}\to M_{2}
of dg
{\mathcal{A}}
-modules. Each such object gives
rise to a triangle

\textstyle{M_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{M_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{cof}(f)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma M_{1}}

of
{\mathcal{D}}{\mathcal{A}}
which is
functorial
in the object
f
of
{\mathcal{D}}(I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{A}})
. Here, we write
\mathrm{cof}
for the homotopy cofiber, i.e. the cone of a morphism.

For two objects
f\colon M_{1}\to M_{2}
and
f^{\prime}\colon M_{1}^{\prime}\to M_{2}^{\prime}
, consider a morphism of triangles

\textstyle{M_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{M_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b}
\textstyle{\mathrm{cof}(f)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c}
\textstyle{\Sigma M_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma a}
\textstyle{M_{1}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\textstyle{M^{\prime}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{cof}(f^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma M_{1}^{\prime}}

in the derived category
{\mathcal{D}}{\mathcal{A}}
. It is well-known that a given morphism
b\colon M_{2}\to M_{2}^{\prime}

extends to such a morphism of triangles
(a,b,c)
if and only if we have
g^{\prime}\circ b\circ f=0
and
that in this case, the pair
(a,b)
lifts to a morphism of
{\mathcal{D}}(I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{A}})
. The following easy
lemma makes this more precise. Here, we write
\mathrm{fib}
for the homotopy fiber, i.e. the
desuspension of the cone of a morphism.

Lemma 3.1
.

We have a canonical isomorphism bifunctorial in the objects

f
and
f^{\prime}
of
{\mathcal{D}}(I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{A}})

\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}}(f,f^{\prime})\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathrm{fib}(\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(M_{2},M_{2}^{\prime})\to\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(M_{1},\mathrm{cof}(f^{\prime})).

More precisely, let
g\colon N_{1}\rightarrow N_{2}
be an object in
{\mathcal{D}}(I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{A}})
and
\beta\colon f^{\prime}\rightarrow g
a morphism in
{\mathcal{D}}(I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{A}})
, we have the following commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}}(f,f^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{*}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{fib}(\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(M_{2},M_{2}^{\prime})\to\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(M_{1},\mathrm{cof}(f^{\prime}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{*}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}}(f,g)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\mathrm{fib}(\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(M_{2},N_{2})\to\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(M_{1},\mathrm{cof}(g)).}

Proof
.
We have isomorphisms of dg categories

\begin{split}{\mathcal{C}}_{dg}(I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{A}})=&{\mathcal{H}}om(I\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{op},{\mathcal{C}}_{dg}(k))\\
\simeq&{\mathcal{H}}om(I,{\mathcal{H}}om({\mathcal{A}}^{op},{\mathcal{C}}_{dg}(k)))\\
=&{\mathcal{H}}om(I,{\mathcal{C}}_{dg}({\mathcal{A}})).\end{split}

In this way,
{\mathcal{C}}_{dg}({\mathcal{A}})
identifies with the category of morphisms
M_{1}\rightarrow M_{2}
of dg
{\mathcal{A}}
-modules with the dg enhancement given by

\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\mathcal{H}}om(f,f^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\ulcorner
\textstyle{{\mathcal{H}}om_{{\mathcal{A}}}(M_{2},M^{\prime}_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{H}}om_{{\mathcal{A}}}(M_{1},M^{\prime}_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{H}}om_{{\mathcal{A}}}(M_{1},M^{\prime}_{2}).}

The model structure on
{\mathcal{C}}(I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{A}})
translates into a model structure on
{\mathcal{H}}om(I,{\mathcal{C}}_{dg}({\mathcal{A}}))
whose weak equivalences are the componentwise quasi-isomorphisms and whose cofibrant objects are the graded split monomorphisms
M_{1}\rightarrowtail M_{2}
with cofibrant
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
. Therefore, we can assume that
f
and
f^{\prime}
are graded split injective morphisms between cofibrant dg
{\mathcal{A}}
-modules. Then we have the following commutative diagram in
{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{A}})

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{coker}(f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\prime}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\prime}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{coker}(f^{\prime}),}

where the first row and second row are graded split exact sequences. It induces the the following commutative diagram of complexes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{H}}om(\mathrm{coker}(f),M^{\prime}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{H}}om(f,f^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{H}}om(M_{1},M^{\prime}_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{H}}om(\mathrm{coker}(f),M^{\prime}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{H}}om(M_{2},M^{\prime}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{H}}om(M_{1},M^{\prime}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{H}}om(M_{1},\mathrm{coker}(f^{\prime}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{H}}om(M_{1},\mathrm{coker}(f^{\prime})),}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\square}

where the upper right square is a bicartesian. Thus we have the exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{H}}om(f,f^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{H}}om(M_{2},M^{\prime}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{H}}om(M_{1},\mathrm{coker}(f^{\prime}))}

and the canonical isomorphism

\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}}(f,f^{\prime})\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathrm{fib}(\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(M_{2},M_{2}^{\prime})\to\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(M_{1},\mathrm{cof}(f^{\prime})).

∎

Let
G\colon{\mathcal{B}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}
be a dg functor. It induces the dg functor

\mathbf{1}\otimes G\colon I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}\longrightarrow I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{A}},

which we still denote by
G
. It yields the adjunction

\mathrm{\textbf{L}}G^{*}\colon{\mathcal{D}}(I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{B}})\leftrightarrows{\mathcal{D}}(I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{A}})\colon G_{*}.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
f\colon M_{1}\rightarrow M_{2}
and
f^{\prime}\colon M^{\prime}_{1}\rightarrow M^{\prime}_{2}
be objects in
{\mathcal{D}}(I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{B}})
. We have the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}}(f,f^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{fib}(\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{{\mathcal{B}}}(M_{2},M^{\prime}_{2})\rightarrow\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{{\mathcal{A}}}(M_{1},\mathrm{cof}(f^{\prime}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}}(\mathrm{\textbf{L}}G^{*}(f),\mathrm{\textbf{L}}G^{*}(f^{\prime}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{fib}(\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{{\mathcal{A}}}(\mathrm{\textbf{L}}G^{*}(M_{2}),\mathrm{\textbf{L}}G^{*}(M^{\prime}_{2}))\rightarrow\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{{\mathcal{A}}}(\mathrm{\textbf{L}}G^{*}(M_{1}),\mathrm{cof}(\mathrm{\textbf{L}}G^{*}(f^{\prime})).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\textbf{L}}G^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\textbf{L}}G^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

Similarly, let
g\colon N_{1}\rightarrow N_{2}
and
g^{\prime}\colon N^{\prime}_{1}\rightarrow N^{\prime}_{2}
be objects in
{\mathcal{D}}(I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{A}})
. We have the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}}(g,g^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{fib}(\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{{\mathcal{B}}}(N_{2},N^{\prime}_{2})\rightarrow\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{{\mathcal{A}}}(N_{1},\mathrm{cof}(g^{\prime}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}}(G_{*}(g),G_{*}(g^{\prime}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{fib}(\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{{\mathcal{A}}}(G_{*}(N_{2}),G_{*}(N^{\prime}_{2}))\rightarrow\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{{\mathcal{A}}}(G_{*}(N_{1}),\mathrm{cof}(G_{*}(g^{\prime})).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

Proof
.
We only show the first statement since the second one can be shown similarly. We can assume that
f,f^{\prime}
are graded split injective morphisms between cofibrant dg
{\mathcal{A}}
-modules. Then it is easy to see that the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{H}}om_{I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}}(f,f^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{H}}om_{{\mathcal{B}}}(M_{2},M^{\prime}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{H}}om_{{\mathcal{B}}}(M_{1},\mathrm{coker}(f^{\prime}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{H}}om_{I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}}(G^{*}(f),G^{*}(f^{\prime}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{H}}om_{{\mathcal{A}}}(G^{*}(M_{2}),G^{*}(M^{\prime}_{2}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{H}}om_{{\mathcal{A}}}(G^{*}(M_{1}),\mathrm{coker}(G^{*}(f^{\prime}))).}

Thus we get the first commutative diagram.

∎

Relative right Calabi–Yau structures were invented by Bertrand Toën in
[
60
, pp. 227-228]
.
Later, the theory of relative right and left Calabi–Yau structures was developed by Chris Brav and Tobias Dyckerhoff in
[
8
]
.

3.2.
Relative right Calabi–Yau structures

Let
G\colon{\mathcal{B}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}
be a dg functor
1
1

1

The definition we will give actually makes sense even if we do not assume
{\mathcal{A}}
and
{\mathcal{B}}
to be proper.
. We denote by
D{\mathcal{A}}^{op}
the dg
{\mathcal{A}}
-bimodule defined as follows:

D{\mathcal{A}}^{op}(X,Y)=D{\mathcal{A}}(Y,X),\quad\forall(X,Y)\in{\mathcal{A}}^{e},

where
D
is the
k
-linear dual
\mathrm{Hom}_{k}(?,k)
. We call it the
linear dual bimodule
of
{\mathcal{A}}
.
Similarly, we define the dg
{\mathcal{B}}
-bimodule
D{\mathcal{B}}^{op}
. The natural
{\mathcal{B}}
-bimodule morphism
u_{G}\colon{\mathcal{B}}\rightarrow G_{*}{\mathcal{A}}
induces a morphism between their linear dual bimodules

G_{*}(D{\mathcal{A}}^{op})\rightarrow D{\mathcal{B}}^{op}.

It canonically lifts to an object
u_{G}^{*}
of
{\mathcal{D}}(I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}^{e})
. Similarly, its homotopy fiber

\mathrm{fib}(u_{G}^{*})\rightarrow G_{*}(D{\mathcal{A}}^{op})

lifts to an object
\delta_{G}
of
{\mathcal{D}}(I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}^{e})
. Each morphism
\Sigma^{n-1}(u_{G})\rightarrow\delta_{G}
gives rise to a morphism of triangles in
{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{B}}^{e})

(1)

\textstyle{\Sigma^{n-1}{\mathcal{B}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma^{n-1}u_{G}}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{n-1}G_{*}{\mathcal{A}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{cof}(\Sigma^{n-1}u_{G})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{n}{\mathcal{B}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{fib}(u_{G}^{*})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{G}}
\textstyle{G_{*}(D{\mathcal{A}}^{op})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u_{G}^{*}}
\textstyle{D{\mathcal{B}}^{op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma\mathrm{fib}(u_{G}^{*})}

We are therefore interested in morphisms
\Sigma^{n-1}u_{G}\to\delta_{G}
in
{\mathcal{D}}(I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}^{e})
.

Lemma 3.3
.

We have a canonical isomorphism

\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}(u_{G},\delta_{G})\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathrm{fib}(\mathrm{Hom}_{k}({\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}{\mathcal{A}},k)\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{k}({\mathcal{B}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}{\mathcal{B}},k)).

Moreover this isomorphism is compatible with the composition of dg functors, i.e. if
Q\colon{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{C}}
is another dg functor, then we have the following commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}(u_{Q\circ G},\delta_{Q\circ G})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\mathrm{fib}(\mathrm{Hom}_{k}({\mathcal{C}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}{\mathcal{C}},k)\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{k}({\mathcal{B}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}{\mathcal{B}},k))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}(u_{G},\delta_{G})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\mathrm{fib}(\mathrm{Hom}_{k}({\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}{\mathcal{A}},k)\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{k}({\mathcal{B}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}{\mathcal{B}},k))}

Proof
.
Using the standard adjunctions, we get

\textstyle{\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}({\mathcal{B}},D{\mathcal{B}}^{op})\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{k}({\mathcal{B}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}{\mathcal{B}},k)}

and

\begin{split}\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}(G_{*}{\mathcal{A}},G_{*}(D{\mathcal{A}}^{op}))\simeq&\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{e}}(\mathrm{\textbf{L}}G^{*}(G_{*}{\mathcal{A}}),D({\mathcal{A}}^{op}))\\
\simeq&\mathrm{Hom}_{k}(G_{*}({\mathcal{A}})\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}{\mathcal{A}}^{e},k)\\
\simeq&\mathrm{Hom}_{k}({\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}{\mathcal{A}},k).\end{split}

We know that the composition
Q\circ G\colon{\mathcal{B}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{C}}
induces the following morphisms in
{\mathcal{D}}(I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}^{e})

u_{G}\rightarrow u_{Q\circ G},\quad\delta_{Q\circ G}\rightarrow\delta_{G}.

Then the claim follows by Lemma
3.1
.

∎

We therefore obtain the following chain of morphisms

\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}(H\!C(G),k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}(H\!H(G),k)\cong\mathrm{fib}(\mathrm{Hom}({\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{e}}{\mathcal{A}},k)\to\mathrm{Hom}({\mathcal{B}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}{\mathcal{B}},k))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{fib}(\mathrm{Hom}({\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}{\mathcal{A}},k)\to\mathrm{Hom}({\mathcal{B}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}{\mathcal{B}},k))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}(u_{G},\delta_{G})}

Definition 3.4
.

[
8
, Definition 4.7]

A
right
n
-Calabi–Yau structure
on the dg functor
G\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}

is a class
[\omega]
in
\mathrm{Hom}(H\!C_{n-1}(G),k)
such that the associated morphism
\Sigma^{n-1}u_{G}\to\delta_{G}
is invertible, i.e. its associated morphism of triangles (
1
) is invertible.

3.3.
Relative left Calabi–Yau structures

Let
G\colon{\mathcal{B}}\to{\mathcal{A}}
be a dg functor. We assume that
{\mathcal{B}}
is smooth. This ensures that the canonical morphism

{\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathcal{B}}^{\vee}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow({\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathcal{B}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathcal{A}})^{\vee}

is invertible in
{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{A}}^{e})
. The composition of
{\mathcal{A}}
induces the morphism

{\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{\mathcal{B}}{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{A}}

of
{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{A}}^{e})
. It canonically lifts to an object
\mu_{G}
of
{\mathcal{D}}(I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{e})
. Similarly, its homotopy fiber

\mathrm{fib}(\mu_{G})\to{\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{\mathcal{B}}{\mathcal{A}}

lifts to an object
\nu_{G}
of
{\mathcal{D}}(I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{e})
. Notice that each morphism
\Sigma^{n-1}\mu_{G}^{\vee}\to\nu_{G}

gives rise to a morphism of triangles in
{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{A}}^{e})

(8)

\textstyle{\Sigma^{n-1}{\mathcal{A}}^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma^{n-1}\mu_{G}^{\vee}}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{n-1}({\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{\mathcal{B}}{\mathcal{A}})^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{n-1}\mathrm{cof}(\mu_{G}^{\vee})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{n}{\mathcal{A}}^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{fib}(\mu_{G})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nu_{G}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{\mathcal{B}}{\mathcal{A}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{G}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{A}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{cof}(\mu_{G}).}

We are therefore interested in morphisms
\Sigma^{n-1}\mu_{G}^{\vee}\to\nu_{G}
in
{\mathcal{D}}(I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{e})
.

Lemma 3.5
.

We have a canonical morphism

\mathrm{fib}({\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{e}}{\mathcal{A}})\rightarrow\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{e}}(\mu_{G}^{\vee},\nu_{G}).

It is invertible if
{\mathcal{A}}
is smooth. Moreover this canonical morphism is compatible with the composition of dg functors, i.e. if
Q\colon{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{C}}
is another dg functor between smooth dg categories, then we have the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{fib}({\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{e}}{\mathcal{A}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{e}}(\mu_{G}^{\vee},\nu_{G})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{fib}({\mathcal{C}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{C}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{C}}^{e}}{\mathcal{C}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{C}}^{e}}(\mu_{Q\circ G}^{\vee},\nu_{Q\circ G}).}

Proof
.
By Lemma
3.1
, we have

\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{e}}(\mu_{G}^{\vee},\nu_{G})\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathrm{fib}(\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{e}}(({\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathcal{A}})^{\vee},{\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathcal{A}})\to\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{e}}({\mathcal{A}}^{\vee},{\mathcal{A}})).

We have a canonical morphism

{\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{e}}{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{e}}({\mathcal{A}}^{\vee},{\mathcal{A}}),

which is invertible if
{\mathcal{A}}
is smooth. Moreover, we have the isomorphisms

\begin{split}\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{e}}(({\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathcal{A}})^{\vee},{\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathcal{A}})\simeq&\,\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{e}}(\mathrm{\textbf{L}}G^{*}({\mathcal{B}}^{\vee}),{\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathcal{A}})\\
\simeq&\,\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}({\mathcal{B}}^{\vee},G_{*}({\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathcal{A}}))\\
\simeq&\,{\mathcal{B}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}({\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathcal{A}})\\
\simeq&\,{\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}{\mathcal{A}},\end{split}

where we use the smoothness of
{\mathcal{B}}
for the first and the
\boldmath{3}
rd isomorphism.
Thus, we have a canonical morphism

\mathrm{fib}({\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{e}}{\mathcal{A}})\rightarrow\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{e}}(\mu_{G}^{\wedge},\nu_{G}),

which is invertible if
{\mathcal{A}}
is smooth.

By Lemma
3.2
, we have the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{fib}({\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{e}}{\mathcal{A}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{e}}(\mu_{G}^{\vee},\nu_{G})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{fib}(\mathrm{\textbf{L}}Q^{*}({\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}{\mathcal{A}})\to\mathrm{\textbf{L}}Q^{*}({\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{e}}{\mathcal{A}}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{C}}^{e}}(\mathrm{\textbf{L}}Q^{*}(\mu_{G}^{\vee}),\mathrm{\textbf{L}}Q^{*}(\nu_{G})),}

where
\mathrm{\textbf{L}}Q^{*}(\mu^{\vee}_{G})
is given by
{\mathcal{A}}^{\vee}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{e}}{\mathcal{C}}^{e}\rightarrow{\mathcal{B}}^{\vee}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}{\mathcal{C}}^{e}
and
\mathrm{\textbf{L}}Q^{*}(\nu_{G})
is given by
\mathrm{\textbf{L}}G^{*}(\mathrm{fib}(\mu_{G}))\rightarrow{\mathcal{B}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}{\mathcal{C}}^{e}
.

It is easy to see that we have natural morphisms
\mu^{\vee}_{Q\circ G}\rightarrow\mathrm{\textbf{L}}Q^{*}(\mu^{\vee}_{G})
and
\mathrm{\textbf{L}}Q^{*}(\nu_{G})\rightarrow\nu_{Q\circ G}
in
{\mathcal{D}}(I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{C}})
. Then by Lemma
3.1
, we get the following commutative diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{fib}({\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{e}}{\mathcal{A}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{e}}(\mu_{G}^{\vee},\nu_{G})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{fib}(\mathrm{\textbf{L}}Q^{*}({\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}{\mathcal{A}})\to\mathrm{\textbf{L}}Q^{*}({\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{e}}{\mathcal{A}}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{C}}^{e}}(\mathrm{\textbf{L}}Q^{*}(\mu_{G}^{\vee}),\mathrm{\textbf{L}}Q^{*}(\nu_{G}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{fib}({\mathcal{C}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{C}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{e}}{\mathcal{A}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{C}}^{e}}(\mu^{\vee}_{Q\circ G},\mathrm{\textbf{L}}Q^{*}(\nu_{G}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{fib}({\mathcal{C}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{C}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{C}}^{e}}{\mathcal{C}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{C}}^{e}}(\mu^{\vee}_{Q\circ G},\nu_{Q\circ G}).}

∎

We therefore obtain the following chain of morphisms

\textstyle{H\!N(G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H\!H(G)\cong\Sigma\mathrm{fib}({\mathcal{B}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}{\mathcal{B}}\to{\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{e}}{\mathcal{A}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma\mathrm{fib}({\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{e}}{\mathcal{A}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{e}}(\mu_{G}^{\vee},\nu_{G}).}

Definition 3.6
.

[
8
, Definition 4.11]
[
63
, Definition 4.13]
A
left
n
-Calabi–Yau structure
on the dg functor
G\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}

is a relative negative cyclic class
[\xi]
in
H\!N_{n}(G)
such that

a)

the associated morphism
\Sigma^{n-1}\mu_{G}^{\vee}\to\nu_{G}
is invertible and

b)

the morphism
\Sigma^{n-1}{\mathcal{B}}^{\vee}\to{\mathcal{B}}
corresponding to the image of
[\xi]
in

H\!H_{n-1}({\mathcal{B}})
is invertible in
{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{B}}^{e})
.

Notice that the morphism
\mu_{G}^{\vee}\to\Sigma^{n-1}\nu_{G}
is invertible if and only if
its associated morphism of triangles (
8
) is invertible.
We point out that condition b) is not imposed by Brav–Dyckerhoff
[
8
]
but
is imposed by Yeung
[
63
]
.

Remark 3.7
.

If we take the dg category
\mathcal{B}
to be the empty dg category
\emptyset
, which is the initial object in the category of small dg categories
\mathrm{dgcat}_{k}
, then the above definition coincides with the definition of an
absolute left n-Calabi–Yau structure
on
\mathcal{A}

[
40
]
.

Proposition 3.8
.

[
8
, Corollary 7.1]

Let
f\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
be a dg functor between homologically smooth dg categories which carries a left
n
-Calabi–Yau structure. Then there is a canonical left
n
-Calabi–Yau structure on the cofiber
\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{B}
.

Proposition 3.9
.

Let
\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A},{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
be smooth dg categories. Let
G\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
be a dg functor and
Q\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
be a quasi-equivalence. The isomorphism

H\!N_{n}(G)\rightarrow H\!N_{n}(Q\circ G)

induced by
Q
yields a bijection between the left
n
-Calabi–Yau structures on
G
and on
Q\circ G
.

Proof
.
By Theorem
2.18
, the functor
Q
induces the following quasi-isomorphism of triangles in
{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{M}}ix)

\textstyle{M(\mathcal{B})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M(\mathcal{A})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M(G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma M(\mathcal{B})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M(\mathcal{B})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M(\mathcal{C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M(Q\circ G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma M(\mathcal{B}).}

Combining with Lemma
3.5
, the above diagram yields the following commutative diagrams in
{\mathcal{D}}(k)

\textstyle{H\!N(G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{H\!N(Q\circ G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H\!H(G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{H\!H(Q\circ G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma\mathrm{fib}({\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{e}}{\mathcal{A}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\Sigma\mathrm{fib}({\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime e}}{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\Sigma\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{e}}(\mu_{G}^{\vee},\nu_{G})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Theta}
\textstyle{\Sigma\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime e}}(\mu_{Q\circ G}^{\vee},\nu_{Q\circ G}).}

The map
\Theta
admits the following description. The quasi-equivalence
Q
induces a quasi-equivalence

\mathbf{1}\otimes Q^{e}\colon I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{e}\rightarrow I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime e},

which we still denote by
Q
. Then the extension along
Q
yields an equivalence

\mathrm{\textbf{L}}Q^{*}\colon{\mathcal{D}}(I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{e})\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}{\mathcal{D}}(I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{e}).

The functor
\mathrm{\textbf{L}}Q^{*}
maps
\mu_{G}^{\vee}
to
\mu_{Q\circ G}^{\vee}
and
\nu_{G}
to
\nu_{Q\circ G}
. Then the map
\Theta
is the map induced by
\mathrm{\textbf{L}}Q^{*}
on mapping complexes. In particular,
\Theta
preserves equivalences.
Thus each left
n
-Calabi–Yau structure on
G
induces a left
n
-Calabi–Yau structure on
Q\circ G
.
Similarly, we can use the restriction functor
Q_{*}\colon{\mathcal{D}}(I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime e})\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}{\mathcal{D}}(I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime e})
to show that each left
n
