-Calabi–Yau structure on
Q\circ G
induces a left
n
-Calabi–Yau structure on
G
.
∎

Corollary 3.10
.

Let
\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A}
be two homologically smooth dg categories and moreover assume
\mathcal{B}
is cofibrant with respect to the Dwyer-Kan model structure (see Theorem
2.2
).
Let
G,G^{\prime}\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
be two homotopic dg functors. The canonical isomorphism

H\!N_{n}(G)\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}H\!N_{n}(G^{\prime})

induces a bijection between the relative left
n
-Calabi–Yau structures on
G
and on
G^{\prime}
.

Proof
.
Since
G
and
G^{\prime}
are homotopic, there exists a dg functor
H\colon\mathcal{B}\to P(\mathcal{A})
that makes the following diagram commutative (see Definition
2.4
)

\textstyle{\mathcal{A}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{G}
\scriptstyle{G^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{H}
\textstyle{P(\mathcal{A})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{P_{0}}
\scriptstyle{P_{1}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}.}

We know that
P_{0}
and
P_{1}
are quasi-equivalences. They induce isomorphisms
H\!N_{n}(G)\xleftarrow{{}_{\sim}}H\!N_{n}(H)\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}H\!N_{n}(G^{\prime})
. Now the claim follows from the above Proposition
3.9
.
∎

3.4.
From left to right

Let
G\colon{\mathcal{B}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}
be a dg functor between smooth dg categories. Suppose that
G
carries a left
n
-Calabi–Yau structure. We define
\mathrm{per}_{dg}({\mathcal{A}})
to be the dg subcategory of
{\mathcal{C}}_{dg}({\mathcal{A}})
whose objects are the perfect cofibrant dg
{\mathcal{A}}
modules and
\mathrm{pvd}_{dg}({\mathcal{A}})
to be the dg subcategory of
{\mathcal{C}}_{dg}({\mathcal{A}})
whose objects are the perfectly valued cofibrant dg
{\mathcal{A}}
modules. Similarly, we define
\mathrm{per}_{dg}({\mathcal{B}})
and
\mathrm{pvd}_{dg}({\mathcal{B}})
. The restriction along
G\colon{\mathcal{B}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}
induces a dg functor
R\colon{\mathcal{E}}=\mathrm{pvd}_{dg}({\mathcal{A}})\rightarrow{\mathcal{F}}=\mathrm{pvd}_{dg}({\mathcal{B}})
.

Theorem 3.11
.

[
8
, pp. 389]

The functor
R\colon{\mathcal{E}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{F}}
inherits a canonical right
n
-Calabi–Yau structure, i.e. we have a class
[\omega]
in
\mathrm{Hom}_{k}(H\!C_{n-1}(R),k)
which yields an isomorphism of triangles in
{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{E}}^{e})

(15)

\textstyle{\Sigma^{n-1}{\mathcal{E}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma^{n-1}u_{R}}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{n-1}R_{*}{\mathcal{F}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{n-1}\mathrm{cof}(u_{R})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{n}{\mathcal{E}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{fib}(u_{R}^{*})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{R}}
\textstyle{R_{*}(D{\mathcal{F}}^{op})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u_{R}^{*}}
\textstyle{D{\mathcal{E}}^{op}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma\mathrm{fib}(u_{R}^{*}),}

where
R_{*}
is the restriction along
R^{e}\colon{\mathcal{E}}^{e}\rightarrow{\mathcal{F}}^{e}
.

Proof
.
By the definition of
\mathrm{pvd}_{dg}{\mathcal{B}}
, we have a dg functor

(\mathrm{per}_{dg}{\mathcal{B}})^{op}\otimes_{k}{\mathcal{F}}\rightarrow\mathrm{per}_{dg}(k),\quad(P,M)\longmapsto{\mathcal{H}}om_{{\mathcal{B}}}(P,M).

It yields a morphism in
{\mathcal{D}}{\mathcal{M}}ix

M((\mathrm{per}_{dg}{\mathcal{B}})^{op})\otimes_{k}M({\mathcal{F}})\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}M((\mathrm{per}_{dg}{\mathcal{B}})^{op}\otimes_{k}{\mathcal{F}})\rightarrow M(\mathrm{per}_{dg}(k))\xleftarrow{{}_{\sim}}M(k)\simeq k,

where the first quasi-isomorphism of
\Lambda
-modules is due to
[
32
, Theorem 2.4]
.

By the adjunction between
?\otimes_{k}M({\mathcal{F}})
and
\mathrm{Hom}_{k}(M({\mathcal{F}}),?)
, we get a morphism in
{\mathcal{D}}{\mathcal{M}}ix

M({\mathcal{B}})\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}M(\mathrm{per}_{dg}{\mathcal{B}})\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}M((\mathrm{per}_{dg}{\mathcal{B}})^{op})\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{k}(M({\mathcal{F}}),k).

Similarly, we get another morphism in
{\mathcal{D}}{\mathcal{M}}ix

M({\mathcal{A}})\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}M(\mathrm{per}_{dg}{\mathcal{A}})\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{k}(M({\mathcal{E}}),k).

Those two maps fit into the following commutative diagram in
{\mathcal{D}}{\mathcal{M}}ix

\textstyle{M({\mathcal{B}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{G}}
\textstyle{M(\mathrm{per}_{dg}{\mathcal{B}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{G^{*}}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{k}(M({\mathcal{F}}),k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{R}^{*}}
\textstyle{M({\mathcal{A}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{M(\mathrm{per}_{dg}{\mathcal{A}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{k}(M({\mathcal{E}}),k).}

Applying the functor
\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{\Lambda}(k,?)
, it yields the following commutative diagram in
{\mathcal{C}}(k)

\textstyle{H\!N({\mathcal{B}})=\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{\Lambda}(k,M({\mathcal{B}}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{G}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{\Lambda}(k,\mathrm{Hom}_{k}(M({\mathcal{F}}),k))\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{k}(H\!C({\mathcal{F}}),k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta^{\prime}_{G}}
\textstyle{H\!N({\mathcal{A}})=\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{\Lambda}(k,M({\mathcal{A}}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{\Lambda}(k,\mathrm{Hom}_{k}(M({\mathcal{E}}),k))\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{k}(H\!C({\mathcal{E}}),k),}

where the isomorphisms on the right hand are due to the adjunction pair
(k\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{\Lambda}?,\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{k}(k,?))
.

The above commutative square fits into the following commutative cube

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\!N({\mathcal{B}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{\Lambda}(k,\mathrm{Hom}_{k}(M({\mathcal{F}}),k))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\!N({\mathcal{A}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{\Lambda}(k,\mathrm{Hom}_{k}(M({\mathcal{E}}),k))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\!H({\mathcal{B}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{\Lambda}(\Lambda,\mathrm{Hom}_{k}(M({\mathcal{F}}),k))\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{k}({\mathcal{F}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{F}}^{e}}{\mathcal{F}},k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\!H({\mathcal{A}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{k}({\mathcal{E}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{E}}^{e}}{\mathcal{E}},k).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{G}}
induced by
\scriptstyle\Lambda\rightarrow k
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{\prime}_{G}}

Moreover, the bottom of the cube above fits into the following commutative cube

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\!H({\mathcal{B}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{k}({\mathcal{F}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{F}}^{e}}{\mathcal{F}},k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\!H({\mathcal{A}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{k}({\mathcal{E}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{E}}^{e}}{\mathcal{E}},k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}{\mathcal{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{k}({\mathcal{F}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{E}}^{e}}{\mathcal{F}},k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{e}}{\mathcal{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{k}({\mathcal{E}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{E}}^{e}}{\mathcal{E}},k).}

Therefore we get the following commutative diagram in
{\mathcal{C}}(k)

\textstyle{H\!N(G)=\mathrm{Cone}(\beta_{G})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{k}(\Sigma^{-1}H\!C(R),k)=\mathrm{Cone}(\beta^{\prime}_{G})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H\!H(G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{k}(\Sigma^{-1}H\!H(R),k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{cof}({\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{e}}{\mathcal{A}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq\,\text{due to Lemma \ref{Lemma:morphism in left CY}}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{cof}(\mathrm{Hom}_{k}({\mathcal{F}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{E}}^{e}}{\mathcal{F}},k)\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{k}({\mathcal{E}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{E}}^{e}}{\mathcal{E}},k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq\,\text{due to Lemma \ref{Lemma: right CY iso map}}}
\textstyle{\Sigma\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{e}}(\mu_{G}^{\vee},\nu_{G})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Theta}
\textstyle{\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{E}}^{e}}(\Sigma^{-1}u_{R},\delta_{R}).}

The map
\Theta
is described below.

Consider the functor
\Psi
given by the composite

{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{A}}^{e})\rightarrow{\mathcal{C}}(\mathrm{per}_{dg}({\mathcal{A}})^{e})\rightarrow{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{E}}\otimes(\mathrm{per}_{dg}{\mathcal{A}})^{op})\rightarrow{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{E}}^{op}\otimes\mathrm{per}_{dg}{\mathcal{A}})\rightarrow{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{E}}^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{E}})\simeq{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{E}}^{e})^{op},

where the second and last functors are given by restriction along
{\mathcal{E}}\subseteq\mathrm{per}_{dg}{\mathcal{A}}
, the first functor is given by the extension along Yoneda embedding and the third functor is given by

M\mapsto M^{*},\,(a,p)\mapsto\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{{\mathcal{E}}}(M(?,p),\mathrm{per}_{dg}{\mathcal{A}}(?,a)).

Then we obtain an induced functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\mathbf{L}}\Psi\colon{\mathcal{D}}(I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{e})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}(I^{op}\otimes{\mathcal{E}}^{e})^{op}.}

Explicitly, this functor associates to a graded split monomorphism of
{\mathcal{A}}
-bimodules
f\colon M_{1}\rightarrowtail M_{2}
with cofibrant
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
, the morphism of
{\mathcal{E}}
-bimodules given by

\textstyle{\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{{\mathcal{E}}}(M_{2}\otimes_{{\mathcal{A}}^{e}}\mathrm{per}_{dg}({\mathcal{A}})^{e}(?,p^{\prime}),\mathrm{per}_{dg}({\mathcal{A}})(?,p))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(p,p^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{{\mathcal{E}}}(M_{1}\otimes_{{\mathcal{A}}^{e}}\mathrm{per}_{dg}({\mathcal{A}})^{e}(?,p^{\prime}),\mathrm{per}_{dg}({\mathcal{A}})(?,p))}

Therefore, the functor
\mathrm{\mathbf{L}}\Psi
maps
\mu_{G}\colon{\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}
to
{\mathcal{E}}\rightarrow R_{*}({\mathcal{F}})
and
\mu_{G}^{\vee}\colon{\mathcal{A}}^{\vee}\rightarrow({\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathcal{A}})^{\vee}
to
R_{*}(D{\mathcal{F}}^{op})\rightarrow D{\mathcal{E}}^{op}
. An explicit calculation shows that the map
\Theta
in diagram (
3.4
) is the map induced by
\mathrm{\mathbf{L}}\Psi
on mapping complexes.

Suppose that the left
n
-Calabi–Yau structure on
G\colon{\mathcal{B}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}
is induced by
[\xi]\in H\!N_{n}(G)
. Then we have an isomorphism of triangles (
8
) in
{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{A}}^{e})
. After applying the functor
\mathrm{\mathbf{L}}\Psi
to this diagram (
8
), we get an isomorphism of triangles (
15
) in
{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{E}}^{e})
and this isomorphism is induced by the class
\alpha([\xi])
.

∎

Proposition 3.12
.

Let
G\colon{\mathcal{B}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}
satisfy the above assumption. For
L,M\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})
, we put

\mathcal{C}(L,M)=\mathrm{Cone}(\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(L,M))\rightarrow\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{\mathcal{B}}(G_{*}(L),G_{*}(M)).

Suppose that
L\in\mathrm{pvd}(\mathcal{A})
and
M\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})
. Then there is a bifunctorial isomorphism of triangles

\textstyle{D\mathcal{C}(L,M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{D\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{\mathcal{B}}(G_{*}(L),G_{*}(M))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{DG_{*}}
\textstyle{D\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(L,M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma D\mathcal{C}(L,M)}
\textstyle{\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(M,\Sigma^{n-1}L)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{G_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{\mathcal{B}}(G_{*}(M),\Sigma^{n-1}G_{*}(L))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}(M,\Sigma^{n-1}L)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(M,\Sigma^{n}L)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces.}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}

If
G_{*}(L)=0
or
G_{*}(M)=0
, then
D\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(L,M)\cong\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(M,\Sigma^{n}L)
. In particular, the full subcategory
\mathrm{pvd}_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathcal{A})
defined as the kernel of the restriction functor
G_{*}\colon\mathrm{pvd}{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow\mathrm{pvd}{\mathcal{B}}
is
n
-Calabi–Yau as a triangulated category.

Proof
.
Since
G:\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
has a relative left
n
-Calabi–Yau structure, by Definition
3.6
and diagram (
8
), we have an isomorphism in
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}^{e})

\Sigma^{n}{\mathcal{A}}^{\vee}\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathrm{cof}({\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathcal{A}}\xrightarrow{\mu_{G}}{\mathcal{A}})=\mathrm{cof}({\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathcal{B}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathcal{A}}\xrightarrow{\mu_{G}}\mathcal{A})\cong\mathrm{Cone}({\mathcal{B}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}{\mathcal{A}}\xrightarrow{\mu_{G}}\mathcal{A}),

and an isomorphism in
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{B}^{e})

\Sigma^{n-1}{\mathcal{B}}^{\vee}\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathcal{B}.

Let
P_{L}
and
P_{M}
be cofibrant resolutions of
L
and
M
respectively.
By
[
39
, Lemma 4.1]
, we have

\begin{split}D\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{\mathcal{B}}(G_{*}(L),G_{*}(M))&\simeq\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{\mathcal{B}}(G_{*}(M),\Sigma^{n-1}G_{*}(L))\end{split}

and

\begin{split}D\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(L,M)&\simeq D{\mathcal{H}}om_{{\mathcal{A}}}(P_{L},P_{M})\\
&\simeq{\mathcal{H}}om_{{\mathcal{A}}}(P_{M}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathbf{L}}{\mathcal{A}}^{\vee},P_{L})\\
&\simeq\mathrm{fib}({\mathcal{H}}om_{{\mathcal{A}}}(P_{M},\Sigma^{n}P_{L})\to\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(A)}(P_{M}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathbf{L}}\mathbf{L}G^{*}(\mathcal{B}),\Sigma^{n}P_{L}))\\
&\simeq\mathrm{fib}(\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{{\mathcal{A}}}(M,\Sigma^{n}L)\to{\mathcal{H}}om_{{\mathcal{A}}}(P_{M}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathbf{L}}(\mathcal{A}\otimes^{\mathbf{L}}_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{A}),\Sigma^{n}P_{L}))\\
&\simeq\mathrm{fib}(\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{{\mathcal{A}}}(M,\Sigma^{n}L)\to{\mathcal{H}}om_{{\mathcal{A}}}(P_{M}\otimes^{\mathbf{L}}_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{A},\Sigma^{n}P_{L}))\\
&\simeq\mathrm{fib}(\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{{\mathcal{A}}}(M,\Sigma^{n}L)\to\mathcal{H}om_{{\mathcal{B}}}(G_{*}(P_{M}),\Sigma^{n}G_{*}(P_{L}))\\
&\simeq\mathrm{fib}(\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{{\mathcal{A}}}(M,\Sigma^{n}L)\to\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{{\mathcal{B}}}(G_{*}(M),\Sigma^{n}G_{*}(L)))\\
&\simeq\mathcal{C}(M,\Sigma^{n-1}L).\end{split}

Thus, we get the bifunctorial isomorphism of triangles (
3.12
). If
G_{*}(L)=0
or
G_{*}(M)=0
, then we have the following functorial duality

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 84.29182pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-84.29182pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{D\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(L,M)\simeq\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(M,\Sigma^{n}L)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

In particular, the kernel
\mathrm{pvd}_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathcal{A})
of
G_{*}\colon\mathrm{pvd}({\mathcal{A}})\rightarrow\mathrm{pvd}({\mathcal{B}})
is
n
-Calabi–Yau as a triangulated category.

∎

Let
\mathcal{B}\xrightarrow{G}\mathcal{A}\xrightarrow{Q}\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{B}
be a homotopy cofiber sequence of small dg categories. By construction, the dg category
\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{B}
is the Drinfeld dg quotient of
\mathcal{A}
by its full dg subcategory
\mathrm{Im}(G)
, where
\mathrm{Im}(G)
is the full dg subcategory of
\mathcal{A}
whose objects are the
y
in
\mathcal{A}
such that there exists an object
x
in
\mathcal{B}
and an isomorphism
F(x)\cong y
in
\rm H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
. We denote by
i
the dg inclusion
\mathrm{Im}(G)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{A}
.

Corollary 3.13
.

For any dg module
N
and any dg module
M
in
\mathrm{pvd}(\mathcal{A})
whose restriction to
\mathrm{Im}G
is acyclic, there is a canonical isomorphism

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 89.97939pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-89.97939pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{D\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})}(M,N)\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})}(N,\Sigma^{n}M)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Proof
.
Since the restriction of
M
to
\mathrm{Im}G
is acyclic, we have
G_{*}(M)=0
. Then the claim follows from the above Proposition
3.12
.
∎

3.5.
Relative Calabi–Yau completions

Given a dg category
{\mathcal{B}}
, let
(\mathrm{dgcat}_{k})_{{\mathcal{B}}/}
be the category of dg categories under
{\mathcal{B}}
. The forgetful functor
(\mathrm{dgcat}_{k})_{{\mathcal{B}}/}\rightarrow{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{B}}^{e})
, sending a dg functor
G\colon{\mathcal{B}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}
to the
{\mathcal{B}}
-bimodule given by
(a,a^{\prime})\mapsto{\mathcal{A}}(G(a^{\prime}),G(a))
, has a left adjoint
T_{{\mathcal{B}}}
, that can be described as follows:

Given a
{\mathcal{B}}
-bimodule
M
, the
tensor category

T_{\mathcal{B}}(M)
is defined as follows:

\textstyle{T_{\mathcal{B}}(M)=\mathcal{B}\oplus M\oplus(M\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}M)\oplus(M\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}M\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}M)\oplus\cdots}

Thus, the dg category
T_{\mathcal{B}}(M)
has the same objects as
{\mathcal{B}}
and morphism complexes

\begin{split}T_{\mathcal{B}}(M)(x,y)&=\mathcal{B}(x,y)\oplus M(x,y)\oplus\{\oplus_{z\in\mathcal{B}}M(z,y)\otimes_{k}M(x,z)\}\oplus\\
&\{\oplus_{z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathcal{B}}M(z_{2},y)\otimes_{k}M(z_{1},z_{2})\otimes_{k}M(x,z_{1})\}\oplus\cdots\end{split}

The dg structure on
T_{\mathcal{B}}(M)
is given by the differentials of
\mathcal{B}
and
M
and the multiplication is given by the concatenation product. This adjunction is Quillen and thus induces an adjunction between their homotopy categories. We will denote by
\mathbf{L}T_{{\mathcal{B}}}
the left derived functor of
T_{{\mathcal{B}}}\colon{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{B}}^{e})\rightarrow(\mathrm{dgcat}_{k})_{{\mathcal{B}}/}
.

An
{\mathcal{B}}
-bilinear (super-)derivation
D
of degree 1 on
\mathbf{L}T_{{\mathcal{B}}}(M)
is determined by its restriction to the generating bimodule
M
. Then it is easy to see that each morphism
c\colon M\rightarrow\Sigma{\mathcal{B}}
in
{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{B}}^{e})
gives rise to a
‘deformation’

(\mathbf{L}T_{{\mathcal{B}}}(M),d_{c})

of
\mathbf{L}T_{{\mathcal{B}}}(M)
, obtained by adding the
{\mathcal{A}}
-bilinear (super-)derivation
D_{c}
determined by
c
to the differential of
\mathbf{L}T_{{\mathcal{B}}}(M)
.

Let
G\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
be a dg functor between smooth dg categories and let
[\xi]
be an element in
H\!H_{n-2}(G)
. Our objective is to define the
deformed relative
n
-Calabi–Yau completion
of
G\colon{\mathcal{B}}\to{\mathcal{A}}
with respect to the Hochschild homology class
[\xi]\in H\!H_{n-2}(G)
.

The dg functor
G\colon{\mathcal{B}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}
induces a morphism of dg
{\mathcal{A}}
-bimodules
{\mathcal{B}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}{\mathcal{A}}^{e}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}
. Let
\Xi
be the cofiber of its bimodule dual, i.e.
\Xi=\mathrm{Cone}({\mathcal{A}}^{\vee}\rightarrow\mathrm{\textbf{L}}G^{*}({\mathcal{B}})^{\vee})=\mathrm{Cone}({\mathcal{A}}^{\vee}\rightarrow({\mathcal{B}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}{\mathcal{A}}^{e})^{\vee})
. Clearly, the the dualizing bimodule
\Theta_{G}=(\mathrm{Cone}({\mathcal{B}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}{\mathcal{A}}^{e}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}))^{\vee}
of
G
is quasi-isomorphic to
\Sigma^{-1}\Xi
.

By the definition of Hochschild homology of
G
, we have the following long exact sequence

\cdots\rightarrow H\!H_{n-2}({\mathcal{B}})\rightarrow H\!H_{n-2}({\mathcal{A}})\rightarrow H\!H_{n-2}(G)\rightarrow H\!H_{n-3}({\mathcal{B}})\rightarrow\cdots.

Thus, the Hochschild homology class
[\xi]=[(s\xi_{\mathcal{B}},\xi_{\mathcal{A}})]\in H\!H_{n-2}(G)
induces an element
[\xi_{{\mathcal{B}}}]
in
H\!H_{n-3}({\mathcal{B}})
.

Notice that since
{\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{A}}
are smooth, we have the following isomorphisms

\begin{split}H\!H_{n-3}({\mathcal{B}})=&H^{3-n}({\mathcal{B}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}{\mathcal{B}})\\
\simeq&H^{3-n}(\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}({\mathcal{B}}^{\vee},{\mathcal{B}}))\\
\simeq&\mathrm{Hom}_{{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{B}}^{e})}(\Sigma^{n-2}{\mathcal{B}}^{\vee},\Sigma{\mathcal{B}})\\
\mathrm{Hom}_{{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{A}}^{e})}(\Sigma^{n-2}\Xi,\Sigma{\mathcal{A}})\simeq&\mathrm{Hom}_{{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{A}}^{e})}(\mathrm{Cone}({\mathcal{A}}^{\vee}\rightarrow({\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathcal{A}})^{\vee}),\Sigma^{3-n}{\mathcal{A}})\\
\simeq&\mathrm{Hom}_{{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{A}}^{e})}(\mathrm{Cone}({\mathcal{A}}^{\vee}\rightarrow({\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathcal{B}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathcal{A}})^{\vee}),\Sigma^{3-n}{\mathcal{A}})\\
\simeq&\mathrm{Hom}_{{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{A}}^{e})}(\mathrm{Cone}({\mathcal{A}}^{\vee}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathcal{B}}^{\vee}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathcal{A}}),\Sigma^{3-n}{\mathcal{A}})\\
\simeq&H^{3-n}(\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{e}}(\mathrm{Cone}({\mathcal{A}}^{\vee}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathcal{B}}^{\vee}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathcal{A}}),{\mathcal{A}}))\\
\simeq&H^{2-n}(\mathrm{Cone}(\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{e}}({\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathcal{B}}^{\vee}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{A}})\rightarrow\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{e}}({\mathcal{A}}^{\vee},{\mathcal{A}})))\\
\simeq&H^{2-n}(\mathrm{Cone}({\mathcal{B}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{e}}{\mathcal{A}})),\end{split}

where we use the isomorphism
{\mathcal{B}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}{\mathcal{B}}\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}({\mathcal{B}}^{\vee},{\mathcal{B}})
in the first computation and
{\mathcal{B}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}{\mathcal{A}}\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}({\mathcal{B}}^{\vee},{\mathcal{A}})\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{e}}({\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathcal{B}}^{\vee}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{A}})
in the second one.

Thus, via the canonical morphism

H\!H_{n-2}(G)=H^{2-n}(\mathrm{Cone}({\mathcal{B}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}{\mathcal{B}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{e}}{\mathcal{A}}))\rightarrow H^{2-n}(\mathrm{Cone}({\mathcal{B}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{e}}{\mathcal{A}})),

the homology class
[\xi]=[(s\xi_{\mathcal{B}},\xi_{\mathcal{A}})]
induces a morphism in
{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{A}}^{e})

\xi\colon\Sigma^{n-2}\Xi\rightarrow\Sigma{\mathcal{A}}

and the homology class
[\xi_{{\mathcal{B}}}]
induces a morphism in
{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{B}}^{e})

\xi_{{\mathcal{B}}}\colon\Sigma^{n-2}{\mathcal{B}}^{\vee}\rightarrow\Sigma{\mathcal{B}}.

Moreover, we have the following commutative diagram in
{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{A}}^{e})
(
[
63
, Proposition 4.7]
)

\textstyle{\mathrm{\textbf{L}}G^{*}(\Sigma^{n-2}{\mathcal{B}}^{\vee})=\Sigma^{n-2}({\mathcal{B}}^{\vee}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}{\mathcal{A}}^{e})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\xi_{{\mathcal{B}}}}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{n-2}\Xi=\Sigma^{n-2}(\mathrm{Cone}({\mathcal{A}}^{\vee}\rightarrow{\mathcal{B}}^{\vee}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}{\mathcal{A}}^{e}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\xi}
\textstyle{\mathrm{\textbf{L}}G^{*}(\Sigma{\mathcal{B}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma{\mathcal{A}},}

where the first horizontal morphism is the canonical inclusion and the second one is the canonical morphism
\mathrm{\textbf{L}}G^{*}({\mathcal{B}})={\mathcal{B}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}{\mathcal{A}}^{e}\simeq{\mathcal{A}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}
of
{\mathcal{A}}
-bimodules.

Therefore, the morphism
\xi_{{\mathcal{B}}}
gives rise to a
‘deformation’

\bm{\Pi}_{n-1}({\mathcal{B}},\xi_{{\mathcal{B}}})

of
\bm{\Pi}_{n-1}({\mathcal{B}})=\mathbf{L}T_{{\mathcal{B}}}(\Sigma^{n-2}{\mathcal{B}}^{\vee})
, obtained by adding
\xi_{{\mathcal{B}}}
to the differential of
\bm{\Pi}_{n-1}({\mathcal{B}})
; the morphism
\xi
gives rise to a ‘deformation’

\bm{\Pi}_{n}({\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{B}},\xi)

of
\bm{\Pi}_{n}({\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{B}})=\mathbf{L}T_{{\mathcal{A}}}(\Sigma^{n-2}\Xi)
, obtained by adding
\xi
to the differential of
\mathbf{L}T_{{\mathcal{A}}}(\Sigma^{n-2}\Xi)
; and the commutative diagram above gives rise to a dg functor

\textstyle{\widetilde{G}\colon\bm{\Pi}_{n-1}({\mathcal{B}},\xi_{{\mathcal{B}}})\rightarrow\bm{\Pi}_{n}({\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{B}},\xi).}

A standard argument shows that up to quasi-isomorphism, the dg functor
\widetilde{G}
and the deformations
\bm{\Pi}_{n-1}(\mathcal{B},\xi_{\mathcal{B}})
,
\bm{\Pi}_{n}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\xi)
only depend on the class
[\xi]
. When the class
[\xi_{{\mathcal{B}}}]
vanishes (respectively,
[\xi]
vanishes), we will abbreviate
\bm{\Pi}_{n-1}(\mathcal{B},\xi_{\mathcal{B}})
(respectively,
\bm{\Pi}_{n}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\xi)
) to
\bm{\Pi}_{n-1}(\mathcal{B})
(respectively,
\bm{\Pi}_{n}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
).

Definition 3.14
.

[
63
, Definition 5.18]

Let
G\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
be a dg functor between smooth dg categories. The dg functor
\widetilde{G}
(
3.5
) defined above is called the
deformed relative
n
-Calabi–Yau completion
of
G\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
with respect to the Hochschild homology class
[\xi]\in H\!H_{n-2}(G)
. If we take the class
[\xi]
be 0, then we will call it simply the
relative
n
-Calabi–Yau completion
of
G\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
.

Remark 3.15
.

If we take
\mathcal{B}
to be the empty category, then the deformed relative
n
-Calabi–Yau completion is the deformed
n
-Calabi–Yau completion of
[
40
]
.

Theorem 3.16
.

[
63
, Theorem 7.1]
[
7
, Proposition 5.29]

Let
G\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
be a dg functor between smooth dg categories and let
[\xi]
be an element in
H\!H_{n-2}(G)
. If
[\xi]
has a negative cyclic lift, then each choice of such a lift gives rise to a canonical left
n
-Calabi–Yau structure on the dg functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{G}\colon\bm{\Pi}_{n-1}(\mathcal{B},\xi_{\mathcal{B}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\Pi}_{n}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\xi).}

3.6.
Reduced relative Calabi–Yau completions

Recall that a dg category
\mathcal{A}
over
k
is said to be
semi-free
if there is a graded quiver
Q=(Q_{0},Q_{1})
such that
the underlying graded
k
-category of
\mathcal{A}
is freely generated by the arrows of
Q
over the vertex set
Q_{0}
. We write this as
\mathcal{A}=T_{kQ_{0}}(kQ_{1})
.

Definition 3.17
.

[
63
, Section 2]

A dg category
\mathcal{A}
is said to be
cellular
if it is semi-free over some graded quiver
Q=(Q_{0},Q_{1})

that admits a filtration

\displaystyle Q^{(1)}\subset Q^{(2)}\subset\cdots

such that every generating arrow
f\in Q^{(i)}
has differential
d(f)
contained in the graded category
T_{kQ_{0}}(kQ^{(i-1)})
.

We say that
\mathcal{A}
is
finitely cellular
if the graded quiver
(Q_{0},Q_{1})
is finite (i.e. both
Q_{0}
and
Q_{1}
are finite).

We say that
\mathcal{A}
is of
finite cellular type
if it is quasi-equivalent to a finitely cellular dg category.

Let
G\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
be a dg functor between finitely cellular type dg categories. By
[
62
, Remark 24.2.8]
, we can assume that
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{A}
are finitely cellular and
G\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
is a semi-free extension, i.e. there is a finite graded quiver
Q
and a subquiver
F\subseteq Q
such that the underlying graded
k
-category of
{\mathcal{B}}
and
{\mathcal{A}}
are isomorphic to
T_{kF_{0}}(kF_{1})
and
T_{kQ_{0}}(kQ_{1})
, respectively. We abbreviate
R_{2}=kF_{0}
and
R_{1}=kQ_{0}
. Then we have a short exact sequence of
\mathcal{B}
-bimodules

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{B})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{\mathcal{B}\otimes_{R_{2}}\mathcal{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m}
\textstyle{\mathcal{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}

where
the bimodule of differentials

\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{B})
is generated by
\{D(f)|f\in F_{1}\}
, the map
\alpha
is given by
D(f)\mapsto f\otimes 1_{x}-1_{y}\otimes f
where
f\colon x\rightarrow y

and the map
m
is the composition map in
\mathcal{B}
.

We define
\Omega^{1}({\mathcal{A}})
similarly.
We put

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 40.07352pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-40.07352pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{P}_{B}=\mathrm{Cone}(\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{B})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 46.47414pt\raise 4.50694pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\alpha}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 64.07352pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 64.07352pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{B}\otimes_{R_{2}}\mathcal{B}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces)

and

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 40.0973pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-40.0973pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{P}_{A}=\mathrm{Cone}(\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{A})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 46.49792pt\raise 4.50694pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\alpha}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 64.0973pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 64.0973pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{A}\otimes_{R_{1}}\mathcal{A}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces).

Then
P_{\mathcal{B}}
and
P_{\mathcal{A}}
are cofibrant replacements of the bimodules
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{A}
respectively. The
\mathcal{B}
-bimodule
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\vee}
is cellular of finite rank, with basis
\{f^{\vee}_{\mathcal{B}}|f\in F_{1}\}\cup\{c_{x,\mathcal{B}}|x\in F_{0}\}
where the arrow
f^{\vee}_{\mathcal{B}}
has degree
|f^{\vee}_{\mathcal{B}}|=1-|f|
, and points in the opposite direction to
f
; the loop
c_{x,\mathcal{B}}
has degree
|c_{x,\mathcal{B}}|=0
, and is based at
x
. Similarly, the
\mathcal{A}
-bimodule
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\vee}
is also cellular of finite rank, with basis
\{g^{\vee}_{\mathcal{A}}|g\in Q_{1}\}\cup\{c_{y,\mathcal{A}}|y\in Q_{0}\}
where the arrow
g^{\vee}_{\mathcal{A}}
has degree
|g^{\vee}_{\mathcal{A}}|=1-|g|
, and points in the opposite direction to
g
; the loop
c_{y,\mathcal{A}}
has degree
|c_{y,\mathcal{A}}|=0
, and is based at
y
.

The natural map
\alpha_{G}\colon G^{*}(P_{\mathcal{B}})\to P_{\mathcal{A}}
in
\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A}^{e})
induces the dual map
\alpha_{G}^{\vee}\colon P_{\mathcal{A}}^{\vee}\to G^{*}(P_{\mathcal{B}})^{\vee}
in
\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A}^{e})
. This
\alpha_{G}^{\vee}
is given as follows:

•

\alpha_{G}^{\vee}(c_{y,\mathcal{A}})=c_{y,\mathcal{B}}
if
y
belongs to
F_{0}
; otherwise,
\alpha_{G}^{\vee}(c_{y,\mathcal{A}})=0
,

•

\alpha_{G}^{\vee}(g_{\mathcal{A}}^{\vee})=g_{\mathcal{B}}^{\vee}
if
g
belongs to
F_{1}
; otherwise,
\alpha_{G}^{\vee}(g_{\mathcal{A}}^{\vee})=0
.

Clearly, the morphism
\alpha_{G}^{\vee}
is a graded split surjection of
{\mathcal{A}}
-bimodules. Let
\mathcal{K}
be the kernel of
\alpha_{G}^{\vee}
. Then
\mathcal{K}
is cellular of finite rank, with basis
\{g_{\mathcal{A}}^{\vee},c_{y,\mathcal{A}}\ |\ g\in N_{1}=Q_{1}\setminus F_{1},\ y\in N_{0}=Q_{0}\setminus F_{0}\}
. We have a split exact sequence in the category of graded
\mathcal{A}
-bimodules, i.e. there exist two graded bimodule morphisms
s_{G}\colon G^{*}(P_{\mathcal{B}})^{\vee}\to P_{\mathcal{A}}^{\vee}
,
r_{\mathcal{K}}\colon P_{\mathcal{A}}^{\vee}\to\mathcal{K}
such that
\alpha_{G}^{\vee}\circ s_{G}=\mathbf{1}_{G^{*}(P_{\mathcal{B}})^{\vee}}
,
r_{\mathcal{K}}\circ i_{\mathcal{K}}=\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{K}}
,
s_{G}\circ\alpha_{G}^{\vee}+i_{\mathcal{K}}\circ r_{\mathcal{K}}=\mathbf{1}_{P_{\mathcal{A}}^{\vee}}
. We summarize the notations in the diagram

(30)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{\mathcal{A}}^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{*}(P_{\mathcal{B}})^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{{\mathcal{K}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{G}^{\vee}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{{\mathcal{K}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{G}}

We choose the graded morphisms
r_{{\mathcal{K}}}
and
s_{G}
are given as follows:

•

The graded morphism
s_{G}
maps
g^{\vee}_{{\mathcal{B}}}
to
g^{\vee}_{{\mathcal{A}}}
and maps
c_{x,{\mathcal{B}}}
to
c_{x,{\mathcal{A}}}
.

•

The graded morphism
r_{{\mathcal{K}}}
maps
g^{\vee}_{{\mathcal{A}}}
to
g^{\vee}_{{\mathcal{A}}}
if
g
is in
N_{1}
; otherwise, we put
r_{{\mathcal{K}}}(g^{\vee}_{{\mathcal{A}}})=0
. Moreover, it maps
c_{y,{\mathcal{A}}}
to
c_{y,{\mathcal{A}}}
if
y
is in
N_{0}
; otherwise, we put
r_{{\mathcal{K}}}(c_{y,{\mathcal{A}}})=0
.

The above exact sequence yields a triangle in
D(\mathcal{A}^{e})

\textstyle{P_{\mathcal{A}}^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{G}^{\vee}}
\textstyle{G^{*}(P_{\mathcal{B}})^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{\Sigma\mathcal{K}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
,

where
u
is equal to
r_{\mathcal{K}}\circ d_{P_{\mathcal{A}}^{\vee}}\circ s_{G}
. Thus, we get the following isomorphism of triangles in
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}^{e})

\textstyle{P_{\mathcal{A}}^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{G}^{\vee}}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{1}}
\textstyle{G^{*}(P_{\mathcal{B}})^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{1}}
\textstyle{\Sigma\mathcal{K}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v}
\textstyle{P_{\mathcal{A}}^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{G}^{\vee}}
\textstyle{G^{*}(P_{\mathcal{B}})^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{l}
\textstyle{\Xi\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
,

where
\Xi=\mathrm{Cone}(P_{\mathcal{A}}^{\vee}\to G^{*}(P_{\mathcal{B}})^{\vee})
and

is the quasi-isomorphism induced by the inclusion of
{\mathcal{K}}
into
P_{{\mathcal{A}}}^{\vee}
. Here the morphism
P_{\mathcal{A}}^{\vee}\to G^{*}(P_{\mathcal{B}})^{\vee}
of
{\mathcal{A}}
-modules is a cofibrant replacement of
{\mathcal{A}}^{\vee}\rightarrow\mathrm{\textbf{L}}G^{*}({\mathcal{B}})^{\vee}
.

Now we consider the derived tensor category
\mathbf{L}T_{\mathcal{A}}(\Sigma^{n-1}\mathcal{K})
. Let
[\xi]=[(s\xi_{\mathcal{B}},\xi_{\mathcal{A}})]
be an element in
H\!H_{n-2}(G)
. By the above section, the homology class
[\xi]
induces a morphism in
{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{A}}^{e})

\xi\colon\Sigma^{n-2}\Xi\rightarrow\Sigma{\mathcal{A}}

and the homology class
[\xi_{{\mathcal{B}}}]
induces a morphism in
{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{B}}^{e})

\xi_{{\mathcal{B}}}\colon\Sigma^{n-2}{\mathcal{B}}^{\vee}\rightarrow\Sigma{\mathcal{B}}.

Since the
{\mathcal{A}}
-bimodule
{\mathcal{K}}
is cofibrant, we have
\mathbf{L}T_{\mathcal{A}}(\Sigma^{n-1}\mathcal{K})=T_{\mathcal{A}}(\Sigma^{n-1}\mathcal{K})
.

We define
\xi_{\mathcal{K}}
as the following composition

\textstyle{\xi_{\mathcal{K}}\colon\,\Sigma^{n-1}\mathcal{K}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{n-2}\Xi\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\xi}
\textstyle{\Sigma\mathcal{A},}

where

is the conical inclusion.
Then it determines an
{\mathcal{A}}
-bilinear derivation
d^{\prime}_{{\mathcal{K}}}
on
T_{\mathcal{A}}(\Sigma^{n-1}\mathcal{K})
and we get a

of

, obtained by adding

to the differential of
T_{\mathcal{A}}(\Sigma^{n-1}\mathcal{K})
.

Then the canonical inclusion of dg
{\mathcal{A}}
-bimodules

\textstyle{\Sigma^{n-1}\mathcal{K}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{n-2}\Xi}
induces a fully faithful dg functor

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 43.04288pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-43.04288pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Psi\colon T_{{\mathcal{A}}}(\Sigma^{n-1}\mathcal{K},\xi_{\mathcal{K}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 43.04288pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@hook{1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 67.04288pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 67.04288pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\bm{\Pi}_{n}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\xi)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

188.97302pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 158.97302pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 188.97302pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Sigma^{n-3}\Xi\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 222.19096pt\raise 6.1111pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{-\xi}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 244.98584pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 244.98584pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{A}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Then the following diagram commutes strictly

\textstyle{\Sigma^{n-2}G^{*}(P_{\mathcal{B}})^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(-H,u)^{T}}
\scriptstyle{(d_{G^{*}(P_{\mathcal{B}})^{\vee}},G^{*}(\xi_{\mathcal{B}}))^{T}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}\oplus\Sigma^{n-1}\mathcal{K}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(d_{\mathcal{A}},\xi\circ v)}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{n-1}G^{*}(P_{\mathcal{B}})^{\vee}\oplus\Sigma G^{*}(\mathcal{B})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(H,j_{G})}
\textstyle{\Sigma\mathcal{A}.}

Thus, the above commutative diagram induces a dg functor

\textstyle{\bm{G}_{rel}\colon\bm{\Pi}_{n-1}(\mathcal{B},\xi_{\mathcal{B}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\bm{\Pi}_{n}^{red}({\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{B}},\xi)}

where we put
\bm{\Pi}_{n}^{red}({\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{B}},\xi)=T_{{\mathcal{A}}}(\Sigma^{n-1}\mathcal{K},\xi_{\mathcal{K}})
.
A standard argument shows that up to quasi-isomorphism, the dg functor
\bm{G}_{rel}
and the deformed dg category
\bm{\Pi}_{n}^{red}({\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{B}},\xi)
only depend on the class
[\xi]
and the dg functor
G\colon{\mathcal{B}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{A}}
.

We call the dg functor
\bm{G}_{rel}
the
reduced deformed relative n-Calabi–Yau completion
of
G\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
with respect to the Hochschild homology class
[\xi]\in H\!H_{n-2}(G)
.

Proposition 3.18
.

Let
G\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
be a dg functor between dg categories of finitely cellular type and let
[\xi]=[(s\xi_{\mathcal{B}},\xi_{\mathcal{A}})]
be an element in
H\!H_{n-2}(G)
which has a negative cyclic lift. Then we have the following diagram which is commutative up to homotopy and where
\Psi
is a quasi-equivalence.

\textstyle{\bm{\Pi}_{n-1}(\mathcal{B},\xi_{\mathcal{B}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{G}}
\scriptstyle{\bm{G}_{rel}}
\textstyle{\bm{\Pi}_{n}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\xi)}
\textstyle{\bm{\Pi}_{n}^{red}({\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{B}},\xi)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi}

Thus, the dg functor
\bm{G}_{rel}\colon\bm{\Pi}_{n-1}(\mathcal{B},\xi_{\mathcal{B}})\to\bm{\Pi}_{n}^{red}({\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{B}},\xi)
has a canonical left
n
-Calabi–Yau structure.

Proof
.
Since the map
v
in
diagram (
3.6
) is a quasi-isomorphism between cofibrant dg
{\mathcal{A}}^{e}
-modules, the map
v
is a homotopy equivalence. Then we can construct a homotopy inverse of
\Psi
. Thus the dg functor
\Psi
is a quasi-equivalence.

Suppose that
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{A}
are finitely cellular and
G\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
is a semi-free extension, i.e. there is a finite graded quiver
Q
and a subquiver
F\subseteq Q
, cf. above. We know that the bimodules

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 40.07352pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-40.07352pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{P}_{B}=\mathrm{Cone}(\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{B})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 46.47414pt\raise 4.50694pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\alpha}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 64.07352pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 64.07352pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{B}\otimes_{R_{2}}\mathcal{B}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces)

and

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 40.0973pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-40.0973pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{P}_{A}=\mathrm{Cone}(\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{A})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 46.49792pt\raise 4.50694pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\alpha}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 64.0973pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 64.0973pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{A}\otimes_{R_{1}}\mathcal{A}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces)

are cofibrant replacements of the bimodules
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{A}
respectively. Therefore, the
\mathcal{B}
-bimodule
\Sigma^{n-2}\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\vee}
is cellular of finite rank, with basis

\{f^{\vee}_{\mathcal{B}}\,|\,f\in F_{1}\}\cup\{c_{x,\mathcal{B}}\,|\,x\in F_{0}\}
where the arrow
f^{\vee}_{\mathcal{B}}
has degree
|f^{\vee}_{\mathcal{B}}|=3-n-|f|
, and points in the opposite direction to
f
; the loop
c_{x,\mathcal{B}}
has degree
|c_{x,\mathcal{B}}|=2-n
, and points from
x
to
x
.

Similarly, the
\mathcal{A}
-bimodule
\Sigma^{n-1}\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\vee}
is also cellular of finite rank, with basis

\{g^{\vee}_{\mathcal{A}}|g\in Q_{1}\}\cup\{c_{y,\mathcal{A}}|y\in Q_{0}\}
where the arrow
g^{\vee}_{\mathcal{A}}
has degree
|g^{\vee}_{\mathcal{A}}|=2-n-|g|
, and points in the opposite direction to
g
; the loop
c_{y,\mathcal{A}}
has degree
|c_{y,\mathcal{A}}|=1-n
, and points from
y
to
y
.

Then the homotopy (see Definition
2.4
) between

\Psi\circ\bm{G}_{rel}
and
\widetilde{G}
is given as follows:

•

For each object
x
in
R_{1}
, we have
\Psi\circ\bm{G}_{rel}(x)=\widetilde{G}(x)=x
, i.e,
\alpha(x)
is the identity map in
\bm{\Pi}_{n}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\xi)
.

•

For all objects
x
and
y
in
R_{1}
, the degree
-1
map

h=h(x,y)\colon\bm{\Pi}_{n-1}(\mathcal{B},\xi_{\mathcal{B}})(x,y)\to\bm{\Pi}_{n}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\xi)(x,y)

is obtained from the following map of degree
-1
,

\textstyle{h_{2}\colon\Sigma^{n-2}G^{*}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\vee})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{n-1}\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\vee}}

where
h_{2}
is given by
f_{\mathcal{B}}^{\vee}\to f_{\mathcal{A}}^{\vee}
, and
c_{x,\mathcal{B}}\to c_{x,\mathcal{A}}
.

By Proposition
3.9
and Corollary
3.10
, the dg functor
\bm{G}_{rel}\colon\bm{\Pi}_{n-1}(\mathcal{B},\xi_{\mathcal{B}})\to\bm{\Pi}_{n}^{red}({\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{B}},\xi)
has a canonical left
n
-Calabi–Yau structure.

∎

3.7.
Relation with the absolute Calabi–Yau completion

Let
G\colon\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
be a dg functor between smooth dg categories. In
[
7
, Section 5.2.3]
, Bozec–Calaque–Scherotzke defined the following tensor category over
\mathcal{A}

\bm{\Pi}_{n}(G)=T_{\mathcal{A}}(\Sigma^{n-1}{\mathcal{B}}^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}^{e}}^{\mathbf{L}}\mathcal{A}^{e}).

Let
{\mathcal{A}}/{\mathcal{B}}
be the homotopy cofiber of
G
, i.e. we have the following homotopy push-out diagram in
\mathrm{dgcat}_{k}
with Dwyer-Kan model structure
[
58
]

\textstyle{\mathcal{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{B}.}

Proposition 3.19
.

The following sequence is a homotopy cofiber sequence in
dgcat_{k}

\bm{\Pi}_{n-1}(\mathcal{B})\rightarrow\bm{\Pi}_{n}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\rightarrow\bm{\Pi}_{n}(\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{B}),

where
\Pi_{n}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})=\Pi_{n}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\xi=0)
.

Proof
.
By
[
7
, Corollary 5.24]
, the dg functor
\bm{\Pi}_{n-1}(\mathcal{B})\rightarrow\bm{\Pi}_{n}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
is the following composition

\bm{\Pi}_{n-1}(\mathcal{B})\rightarrow\bm{\Pi}_{n-1}(G)\rightarrow\bm{\Pi}_{n}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}).

Consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\Pi}_{n-1}({\mathcal{A}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\Pi}_{n-1}({\mathcal{B}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\Pi}_{n-1}(G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\Pi}_{n-1}({\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{B}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}}/{\mathcal{B}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 3}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2}

The square
1
is a homotopy push-out by
[
7
,
Lemma 5.27]
. Since the rectangle around
1
and
2
is a homotopy push-out, it follows that so is
2
. By
[
7
, Corollary 5.24]
, the square
3
is also a homotopy push-out.

Therefore the homotopy cofiber of
\bm{\Pi}_{n-1}(\mathcal{B})\rightarrow\bm{\Pi}_{n}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
is the homotopy push-out of the following diagram

\textstyle{\bm{\Pi}_{n-1}(\mathcal{A})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}}
\textstyle{\bm{\Pi}_{n-1}(G)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{B}}
.

It is easy to see that the composition
\bm{\Pi}_{n-1}(\mathcal{A})\rightarrow\bm{\Pi}_{n-1}(G)\rightarrow\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{B}
is equal to
\bm{\Pi}_{n-1}(\mathcal{A})\xrightarrow{}\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{B}
. Consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\Pi}_{n-1}(\mathcal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\Pi}_{n}(\mathcal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{B}}
.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 4}

The square
4
is a homotopy push-out by
[
40
, Proposition 5.6]
. By
[
40
, Theorem 4.6]
, the following diagram is a homotopy push-out

\textstyle{\mathcal{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\bm{\Pi}_{n}(\mathcal{A})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\bm{\Pi}_{n}(\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{B}).}

Thus, the sequence

\bm{\Pi}_{n-1}(\mathcal{B})\rightarrow\bm{\Pi}_{n}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\rightarrow\bm{\Pi}_{n}(\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{B})

is a homotopy cofiber sequence in
\mathrm{dgcat}_{k}
.

∎

4.
Relative cluster categories

Let
f\colon\ B\to A
be a morphism (not necessarily unital) between differential graded (=dg)
k
-algebras. We consider the following assumptions.

Assumptions 1
.

Suppose that the morphism
f\colon B\to A
satisfies the following properties:

1)

A
and
B
are smooth,

2)

A
is connective, i.e. the cohomology of
A
vanishes in degrees
>0
,

3)

the morphism
f\colon B\to A
has a left
(n+1)
-Calabi–Yau structure,

4)

H^{0}(A)
is finite-dimensional.

Let
\mathrm{pvd}(A)
be the perfectly valued derived category of
A
, i.e.
\mathrm{pvd}(A)
is the full subcategory of
{\mathcal{D}}(A)
whose objects are the perfectly valued dg
A
-modules. Since
A
is homologically smooth,
\mathrm{pvd}(A)
is a full subcategory of
\mathrm{per}A
(see
[
39
, Lemma 4.1]
). We denote by
e
the idempotent
f(\boldmath{1}_{B})
and by
i\colon eAe\hookrightarrow A
the canonical inclusion of dg algebras.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
\mathrm{pvd}_{B}(A)
be the full triangulated subcategory of
\mathrm{pvd}(A)
defined as the kernel of the restriction functor
i_{*}\colon{\mathcal{D}}(A)\to{\mathcal{D}}(eAe)
. The
relative
n
-cluster category

\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
is defined as the following Verdier quotient

\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)=\mathrm{per}A/\mathrm{pvd}_{B}(A).

We denote by
\pi^{rel}
the canonical quotient functor
\mathrm{per}A\to\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
.

4.1.
Gluing
t
-structures

Let
G\colon\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
be a dg functor. Let
\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{B}
be the homotopy cofiber of
G
in
\mathrm{dgcat}_{k}
. Then the dg category
\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{B}
can be computed as the Drinfeld dg quotient of
\mathcal{A}
by its full dg subcategory
\mathrm{Im}(G)
, where
\mathrm{Im}(G)
is the full dg subcategory of
\mathcal{A}
whose objects are the
y\in\mathcal{A}
such that there exists
x\in\mathcal{B}
and an isomorphism
G(x)\cong y
in
\rm H^{0}(\mathcal{A})
. We denote by
i
the dg inclusion functor
\mathrm{Im}(G)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{A}
and by
p
the quotient functor
\mathcal{A}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{B}
.

Proposition 4.2
.

[
11
, Theorem 5.1.3]

We have the following recollement of derived categories

\textstyle{\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{B})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{*}=p_{!}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{*}=i_{!}}
\scriptstyle{p^{!}}
\scriptstyle{p^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}(\mathrm{Im}(G))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces.}
\scriptstyle{i^{!}}
\scriptstyle{i^{*}}

The respective triangle functors are explicitly given as follows:

\textstyle{p^{*}=?\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathbf{L}}\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{B}}
\textstyle{p_{*}=\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{B}}(\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{B},?)\simeq\ ?\otimes_{\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{B}}^{\mathbf{L}}\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{B}=p_{!}}
\textstyle{p^{!}=\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{B},?)}
\textstyle{i^{*}=?\otimes_{\rm Im(G)}^{\mathbf{L}}\mathcal{A}}
\textstyle{i_{*}=\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{A},?)\simeq\ ?\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathbf{L}}\mathcal{\mathcal{A}}=i_{!}}
\textstyle{i^{!}=\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Im}(G)}(\mathcal{A},?)}

Consequently, we have a triangle equivalence up to direct summands

\textstyle{\mathrm{per}(\mathcal{A})/\mathrm{per}(\mathrm{Im}(G))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{per}(\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{B}).}

Theorem 4.3
.

[
5
, Gluing
t
-structures]

Suppose that we have the following recollement of triangulated categories

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 6.75pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 30.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 30.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{V}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 51.1849pt\raise 21.59656pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.63542pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{j}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{\hbox{\kern 42.39615pt\raise 7.27614pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}\lx@xy@spline@}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 51.07756pt\raise-20.10698pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{r}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{\hbox{\kern 41.05078pt\raise-5.55382pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}\lx@xy@spline@}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Let
(\mathcal{U}^{\leqslant 0},\mathcal{U}^{\geqslant 0})
be a t-structure in
\mathcal{U}
and
(\mathcal{V}^{\leqslant 0},\mathcal{V}^{\geqslant 0})
be a t-structure in
\mathcal{V}
.
Then we have a canonical t-structure in
\mathcal{T}
defined as follows:

\textstyle{\mathcal{T}^{\leqslant n}=\{X\in\mathcal{T}|e(X)\in\mathcal{V}^{\leqslant n}\ and\ q(X)\in\mathcal{U}^{\leqslant n}\}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{T}^{\geqslant n}=\{X\in\mathcal{T}|e(X)\in\mathcal{V}^{\geqslant n}\ and\ p(X)\in\mathcal{U}^{\geqslant n}\}.}

We say that the t-structure
({\mathcal{T}}^{\leqslant n},{\mathcal{T}}^{\geqslant n})
on
{\mathcal{T}}
is
glued
from the given t-structure on
{\mathcal{U}}
and
{\mathcal{V}}
.

For any object
X
in
\mathcal{T}
, the canonical distinguished triangle for
X
with respect to the glued
t
-structure can be constructed as follows: Let
X
be an object in
\mathcal{T}
. We have a distinguished triangle in
\mathcal{V}
,

\tau^{\mathcal{V}}_{\leqslant 0}(e(X))\to e(X)\to\tau_{\mathcal{V}}^{\geqslant 1}(e(X))\to\Sigma\tau^{\mathcal{V}}_{\leqslant 0}(e(X)).

Hence we obtain a distinguished triangle

Y\xlongrightarrow{f}X\to r(\tau^{\mathcal{V}}_{\geqslant 1}e(X))\to\Sigma Y,

where
X\to r(\tau^{\mathcal{V}}_{\geqslant 1}e(X))
is the composition
X\to r(e(X))\to r(\tau^{\mathcal{V}}_{\geqslant 1}e(X)).

Similarly, we have a distinguished triangle in
\mathcal{U}
,

\tau^{\mathcal{U}}_{\leqslant 0}(q(Y))\to q(Y)\to\tau^{\mathcal{U}}_{\geqslant 1}(q(Y))\to\Sigma\tau^{\mathcal{U}}_{\leqslant 0}(q(Y)).

Hence we obtain a distinguished triangle

Z\xlongrightarrow{g}Y\to i(\tau^{\mathcal{U}}_{\geqslant 1}q(Y))\to\Sigma Z,

where
Y\to i(\tau_{\mathcal{U}}^{\geqslant 1}q(Y))
is the composition
Y\to i(q(Y))\to i(\tau^{\mathcal{U}}_{\geqslant 1}q(Y)).

Thus, we have the following octahedron

\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{i(\tau^{\mathcal{U}}_{\geqslant 1}q(Y))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f\circ g}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{U\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma g}
\textstyle{r(\tau^{\mathcal{V}}_{\geqslant 1}e(X))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{r(\tau^{\mathcal{V}}_{\geqslant 1}e(X))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma Y}
\textstyle{\Sigma Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma i(\tau^{\mathcal{U}}_{\geqslant 1}q(Y))\quad.}

Then one can show that we have
Z\in\mathcal{T}^{\leqslant 0}
and
U\in\mathcal{T}^{\geqslant 1}
. Thus, for any
X\in\mathcal{T}
, the canonical distinguished triangle for
X
with respect to the glued t-structure is given by

Z\to X\to U\to\Sigma Z.

Let
e=f(\boldmath{1}_{B}).
We denote by
\overline{A}
the homotopy cofiber of
f\colon B\rightarrow A
.
Consider the following homotopy cofiber sequence in
\mathrm{dgcat}_{k}

\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\overline{A}.}

Then we have the following immediate Proposition.

Proposition 4.4
.

[
8
, Corollary 7.1]

The homotopy cofiber
\overline{A}
is homologically smooth and it has a canonical
(n+1)
-Calabi–Yau structure.

Proposition 4.5
.

The homotopy cofiber
\overline{A}
is connective and
H^{0}(\overline{A})
is finite-dimensional.

Proof
.
By the construction of the Drinfeld dg quotient and the assumption that
A
is connective, the dg algebra
\overline{A}
is also connective. By
[
9
, Theorem 5.8]
, the 0-th cohomology
H^{0}(\overline{A})
is isomorphic to
H^{0}(A)/\langle e\rangle
. Thus, the algebra
H^{0}(\overline{A})
is finite-dimensional.

∎

Corollary 4.6
.

We have the following recollement

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 56.90964pt\raise 7.94609pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.24501pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{i^{!}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{\hbox{\kern 56.98694pt\raise-8.05135pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}\lx@xy@spline@}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

where the respective triangle functors are explicitly given as follows

\textstyle{p^{*}=?\otimes_{A}^{\mathbf{L}}\overline{A}}
\textstyle{p_{*}=\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{\overline{A}}(\overline{A},?)\simeq\ ?\otimes_{\overline{A}}^{\mathbf{L}}\overline{A}=p_{!}}
\textstyle{p^{!}=\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{A}(\overline{A},?)}
\textstyle{i^{*}=?\otimes_{eAe}^{\mathbf{L}}eA}
\textstyle{i_{*}=\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{A}(eA,?)\simeq\ ?\otimes_{A}^{\mathbf{L}}Ae=i_{!}}
\textstyle{i^{!}=\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{eAe}(Ae,?).}

Consequently, we have a triangle equivalence

\textstyle{i^{*}\colon\mathrm{per}(eAe)\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\langle eA\rangle_{\mathrm{per}A}}

and a triangle equivalence up to direct summands

\textstyle{p^{*}\colon\mathrm{per}(A)/\mathrm{per}(eAe)\rightarrow\mathrm{per}(\overline{A}),}

where
\langle eA\rangle_{\mathrm{per}A}
is the thick subcategory of
\mathrm{per}A
generated by
eA
.

Proof
.
This is a special case of Proposition
4.2
. For more details we refer the reader to
[
34
, Corollary 2.12]
.

∎

Definition 4.7
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an abelian
k
-category. For
i\in\mathbb{Z}
and for a complex
M
of objects in
\mathcal{A}
, we define the
standard truncations

\tau_{\leqslant i}M
and
\tau_{>i}M
by

(\tau_{\leqslant i}M)^{j}=\left\{\begin{aligned} M^{j}&&\text{if}&&j<i\\
\ker(d^{i}_{M})&&\text{if}&&j=i\\
0&&\text{if}&&j>i\end{aligned}\right.\qquad\qquad(\tau_{>i}M)^{j}=\left\{\begin{aligned} 0&&\text{if}&&j<i\\
\frac{M}{\ker(d^{i}_{M})}&&\text{if}&&j=i\\
M^{j}&&\text{if}&&j>i\end{aligned}\right.

Their respective differentials are inherited from
M
.
Notice that
\tau_{\leqslant i}(M)
is a subcomplex of
M
and
\tau_{>i}(M)
is the corresponding quotient complex. Thus we have a sequence, which is componentwise short exact,

0\to\tau_{\leqslant i}(M)\to M\to\tau_{>i}(M)\to 0.

Moreover, taking standard truncations behaves well with respect to cohomology, i.e. we have

H^{j}(\tau_{\leqslant i}M)=\left\{\begin{aligned} H^{j}(M)&&\text{if}&&j\leqslant i,\\
0&&\text{if}&&j>i.\end{aligned}\right.

4.2.
Relative
t
-structure

Let
f\colon B\rightarrow A
be a dg
k
-algebra morphism satisfying the Assumptions
1
. Then the map of complexes
\tau_{\leqslant 0}A\to A
is a quasi-isomorphism of dg algebras. Thus, we may assume that the components
A^{p}
vanish for all
p>0
. Then the canonical projection
A\rightarrow H^{0}(A)
is a homomorphism of dg algebras. We view a module over
H^{0}(A)
as a dg module over
A
via this homomorphism. This defines a natural functor
\mathrm{Mod}H^{0}(A)\to\mathcal{D}(A)
which induces an equivalence from
\mathrm{Mod}H^{0}(A)
onto the heart of the canonical t-structure on
{\mathcal{D}}(A)
whose left aisle (see
[
35
]
) is the full subcategory on the dg modules
M
such that
H^{p}M=0
for all
p>0
.

Let
\mathrm{Mod}_{B}H^{0}(A)
be the full subcategory of
\mathrm{Mod}H^{0}(A)
whose objects are the right
H^{0}(A)
-modules
X
such that the restriction of
X
to
H^{0}(eAe)
vanishes. Thus, we get a natural functor
i\colon\mathrm{Mod}_{B}H^{0}(A)\to\mathcal{D}(A)
.

On
\mathcal{D}(\overline{A})
we take the canonical t-structure with heart
\heartsuit=\mathrm{Mod}H^{0}(\overline{A})
and on
\mathcal{D}(eAe)
we take the trivial t-structure whose left aisle is
\mathcal{D}(eAe)
.
We deduce the following corollary from Theorem
4.3
.

Corollary 4.8
.

There is a t-structure on
\mathcal{D}(A)
obtained by gluing the canonical t-structure on
\mathcal{D}(\overline{A})
with the trivial t-structure on
\mathcal{D}(eAe)
through the recollement diagram (
4.6
). We denote by
(\mathcal{D}(A)_{rel}^{\leqslant 0},\mathcal{D}(A)_{rel}^{\geqslant 0})
the glued t-structure on
{\mathcal{D}}(A)
.
Here, for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}
,

\textstyle{\mathcal{D}(A)_{rel}^{\leqslant k}=\{X\in\mathcal{D}(A)|H^{l}(p^{*}X)=0,\,\forall l>k\},}

\textstyle{\mathcal{D}(A)_{rel}^{\geqslant k}=\{X\in\mathcal{D}(A)|i_{*}(X)=0,H^{l}(p^{!}X)\cong H^{l}(X)=0,\,\forall l<k\}.}

and so the heart
\heartsuit^{rel}
of this glued
t
-structure is equivalent to
\mathrm{Mod}_{B}H^{0}(A)
.

Proof
.
The functor
p_{*}\colon\mathcal{D}(\overline{A})\to\ker(i_{*})
is an equivalence of triangulated categories. So the restrictions of the adjoints
p^{*}
and
p^{!}
to
\ker(i_{*})
give quasi-inverses of
p_{*}\colon\mathcal{D}(\overline{A})\to\ker(i_{*})
. Thus, we have

(47)

\begin{split}\heartsuit^{rel}=\mathcal{D}(A)_{rel}^{\leqslant 0}\cap\mathcal{D}(A)_{rel}^{\geqslant 0}&=\{X\in\mathcal{D}(A)|i_{*}(X)=0,H^{l}(p^{!}X)=H^{l}(p^{*}X)=0,\ \forall l\neq 0\}\\
&=\{X\in\mathcal{D}(A)|i_{*}(X)=0,H^{l}(X)=0,\ \forall l\neq 0\}.\end{split}

The morphism of dg algebras
A\to H^{0}(A)
induces a natural functor
i\colon\mathrm{Mod}_{B}H^{0}(A)\to\heartsuit^{rel}
. Let
X
be an object in
\heartsuit^{rel}\subseteq\ker(i_{*})
. Then
X
is concentrated in degree 0 and
X
is isomorphic to an object
X^{\prime}
in
\mathrm{Mod}H^{0}(A)
. Since we know that
i_{*}(X)
is acyclic,
X^{\prime}
is also in
\mathrm{Mod}_{B}H^{0}(A)
. This shows the denseness of
i
. The fully faithfulness follows from the following commutative square with three fully faithful functors

\textstyle{\mathrm{Mod}_{B}H^{0}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Mod}H^{0}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\heartsuit^{rel}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\heartsuit.}

∎

We will call
(\mathcal{D}(A)_{rel}^{\leqslant 0},\mathcal{D}(A)_{rel}^{\geqslant 0})
the
relative t-structure
on
\mathcal{D}(A)
. We illustrate this glued t-structure in the following picture

where the blue region represents the subcategory

.

By Corollary
4.6
, the canonical triangle for an object
X\in\mathcal{D}(A)
with respect to the glued t-structure can be constructed as follows: Let
X
be an object in
\mathcal{D}(A)
. We have the following canonical triangle

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 19.46597pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-19.46597pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{i^{*}(i_{*}X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 43.46597pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 43.46597pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 82.5354pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 82.5354pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{p_{*}(p^{*}X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 148.63959pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 148.63959pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Sigma i^{*}(i_{*}X)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

For the object
p^{*}X\in\mathcal{D}(\overline{A})
, we have the following canonical triangle triangle

\textstyle{\tau_{\leqslant k}(p^{*}X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{p^{*}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\tau_{>k}(p^{*}X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma\tau_{\leqslant k}(p^{*}X).}

Then we get a triangle in
\mathcal{D}(A)

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 32.71185pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-32.71185pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{p_{*}(\tau_{\leqslant k}(p^{*}X))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 56.71185pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 56.71185pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{p_{*}(p^{*}X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 122.81604pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 122.81604pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{p_{*}(\tau_{>k}(p^{*}X))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 212.23975pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 212.23975pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Sigma p_{*}(\tau_{\leqslant k}(p^{*}X))}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Thus, by the octahedral axiom, there exists an object
\tau_{\leqslant k}^{rel}X
in
\mathcal{D}_{rel}^{\leqslant k}(A)
such that we have an isomorphism
p^{*}(\tau_{\leqslant k}^{rel}X)\cong\tau_{\leqslant k}(p^{*}X)
and the following morphism of distinguished triangles

\textstyle{p_{*}(\tau_{\leqslant k}(p^{*}X))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma p_{*}(\tau_{\leqslant k}(p^{*}X))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\tau_{\leqslant k}^{rel}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{p_{*}(\tau_{>k}(p^{*}X))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{i^{*}(i_{*}X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\boldmath{1_{X}}}
\textstyle{p_{*}(p^{*}X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{-1}p_{*}(\tau_{\leqslant k}(p^{*}X))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{p_{*}(\tau_{\leqslant k}(p^{*}X))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
.

Definition 4.9
.

We define the
relative truncation functor

\tau^{rel}_{>k}
to be the following composition

\textstyle{\tau^{rel}_{>k}\colon\mathcal{D}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}(\overline{A})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{>k}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}(\overline{A})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{*}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}(A).}

Thus, for any
X\in\mathcal{D}(A)
, we have a canonical triangle in
{\mathcal{D}}(A)

\tau_{\leqslant k}^{rel}X\to X\to\tau_{>k}^{rel}\to\Sigma\tau_{\leqslant k}^{rel}X

such that
\tau_{\leqslant k}^{rel}X
belongs to
\mathcal{D}(A)_{rel}^{\leqslant k}
and
\tau^{rel}_{>k}(X)=p_{*}(\tau_{>k}(p^{*}X))
belongs to
\mathcal{D}(A)_{rel}^{\geqslant k+1}
. Moreover, the object
\tau^{rel}_{>k}(X)
lies in
\mathrm{pvd}_{B}(A)
since it is the essential image of
p_{*}
.

4.3.
The restriction of the relative
t
-structure

Proposition 4.10
.

[
34
, Proposition 2.5]

For each
p\in\mathbb{Z}
, the space
H^{p}(A)
is finite dimensional. Consequently, the category
\mathrm{per}A
is Hom-finite.

Proposition 4.11
.

The relative t-structure on
\mathcal{D}(A)
restricts to
\mathrm{per}A
.

Proof
.
Let
X
be in
\mathrm{per}A
and look at the canonical triangle with respect to the relative t-structure on
\mathcal{D}(A)

\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant 0}X\rightarrow X\rightarrow\tau^{rel}_{>0}X\rightarrow\Sigma\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant 0}X,

where
\tau^{rel}_{>0}X=p_{*}(\tau_{>0}p^{*}(X))
. By Proposition
4.5
, the algebra
H^{0}(\overline{A})
is finite-dimensional. Then by
[
34
, Proposition 2.5]
, the category
\mathrm{per}(\overline{A})
is also Hom-finite. Thus, the space

H^{l}(\tau^{rel}_{>0}X)=\mathrm{Hom}_{{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{A}})}(A,\Sigma^{l}p_{*}\tau_{>0}p^{*}X)\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{{\mathcal{D}}(\overline{A})}(\overline{A},\Sigma^{l}\tau_{>0}p^{*}X)

equals zero or
H^{l}(\tau_{>0}p^{*}X)
which is finite-dimensional. Thus, the object
\tau^{rel}_{>0}X
is in
\mathrm{pvd}(A)
and so in
\mathrm{per}A
. Since
\mathrm{per}A
is a triangulated subcategory, it follows that
\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant 0}X
also lies in
\mathrm{per}A
.

∎

Proposition 4.12
.

Let
\mathrm{pvd}_{B}(A)_{rel}^{\leqslant 0}
be the full subcategory of
\mathcal{D}(A)_{rel}^{\leqslant 0}
whose objects are the
M\in\mathrm{pvd}(A)
whose restriction along
i\colon eAe\hookrightarrow A
is acyclic. Then
(\mathrm{pvd}_{B}(A)_{rel}^{\leqslant 0},\mathcal{D}(A)_{rel}^{\geqslant 0})
is a t-structure on
\mathrm{pvd}_{B}(A)
and the corresponding heart is equivalent to
\mathrm{mod}_{B}H^{0}(A)
, where
\mathrm{mod}_{B}H^{0}(A)
is the full subcategory of
\mathrm{Mod}_{B}H^{0}(A)
whose objects are the finite-dimensional
H^{0}(A)
-modules. Moreover, the triangulated category
\mathrm{pvd}_{B}(A)
is generated by its heart.

Proof
.
Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. For any object
X\in\mathrm{pvd}_{B}(A)
, we have the following triangle

\textstyle{\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant 0}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\tau^{rel}_{>0}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

with
\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant 0}X\in\mathcal{D}(A)_{rel}^{\leqslant 0}
and
\tau^{rel}_{>0}X\in\mathcal{D}(A)_{rel}^{>0}\subseteq\mathrm{pvd}_{B}(A)
. So the object
\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant 0}X
is also in
\mathrm{pvd}_{B}(A)
. This is the triangle required to show that
