(\mathrm{pvd}_{B}(A)_{rel}^{\leqslant 0},\mathcal{D}(A)_{rel}^{\geqslant 0})
is a t-structure.

To show the second statement, let
M
be an object in
\mathrm{pvd}_{B}(M)
. Let
k\leqslant m
be integers such that
H^{l}(M)\neq 0
only for
l\in[k,m]
. We use induction on
m-k
. If
m-k=0
, then a shift of
M
is in the heart. Now suppose
m-k>0
. Then the relative truncations yield a triangle in
\mathrm{pvd}_{B}(A)

\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant k}M\rightarrow M\rightarrow\tau^{rel}_{>k}M\rightarrow\Sigma\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant k}M.

The homology of
\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant k}M
is concentrated in degree
k
. Thus, the object
\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant k}M
belongs to a shifted copy of the heart. Moreover, the homology of
\tau^{rel}_{>k}M
is bounded between degrees
k+1
and
m
. By the induction hypothesis, the object
\tau^{rel}_{>k}M
is contained in the triangulated subcategory generated by the heart. Therefore the same holds for
M
.
∎

Recall that we have defined
{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(A,B)=\mathrm{per}A/\mathrm{pvd}_{B}(A)
.

Proposition 4.13
.

[
3
, Proposition 7.1.4]

Under the projection functor
\pi^{rel}\colon\mathrm{per}A\to\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
, for any
X
and
Y
in
\mathrm{per}A
, we have

\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}(\pi^{rel}X,\pi^{rel}Y)=\varinjlim_{k\leqslant 0}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(A)}(\tau_{\leqslant k}^{rel}X,\tau_{\leqslant k}^{rel}Y).}

Proof
.
Let
X
and
Y
be in
\mathrm{per}A
. An element of
\varinjlim_{k\leqslant 0}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(A)}(\tau_{\leqslant k}^{rel}X,\tau_{\leqslant k}^{rel}Y)
is an equivalence class of morphisms
\tau_{\leqslant k}^{rel}X\to\tau_{\leqslant k}^{rel}Y
. Two morphisms
f\colon\tau_{\leqslant k}^{rel}X\to\tau_{\leqslant k}^{rel}Y
and
g\colon\tau_{\leqslant m}^{rel}X\to\tau_{\leqslant m}^{rel}Y
with
m\geqslant k
are equivalent if there is a commutative square

\textstyle{\tau_{\leqslant k}^{rel}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\tau_{\leqslant k}^{rel}Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\tau_{\leqslant m}^{rel}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{\tau_{\leqslant m}^{rel}Y,}

where the vertical arrows are the canonical morphisms.

Suppose that
f
is a morphism
f\colon\tau_{\leqslant k}^{rel}X\to\tau_{\leqslant k}^{rel}Y
. We can form the following morphism from
X
to
Y
in
\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)

\textstyle{\tau_{\leqslant k}^{rel}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\tau_{\leqslant k}^{rel}Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{Y,}

where the morphisms
\tau_{\leqslant k}^{rel}X\to X
and
\tau_{\leqslant k}^{rel}Y\to Y
are the canonical morphisms. Here we use the fact that the cone
\tau^{rel}_{>k}X
of the morphism
\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant k}X\rightarrow X
lies in
\mathrm{pvd}_{B}(A)
. Hence the above diagram defines a morphism in
{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(A,B)
.

If
f\colon\tau_{\leqslant k}^{rel}X\to\tau_{\leqslant k}^{rel}Y
and
g\colon\tau_{\leqslant m}^{rel}X\to\tau_{\leqslant m}^{rel}Y
with
m\geqslant k
are equivalent, there is an equivalence of diagrams

\textstyle{\tau_{\leqslant k}^{rel}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\tau_{\leqslant k}^{rel}Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{Y}
\textstyle{\tau_{\leqslant m}^{rel}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{\tau_{\leqslant m}^{rel}Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\,.}

Thus, we have a well-defined map from
\varinjlim_{k\leqslant 0}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(A)}(\tau_{\leqslant k}^{rel}X,\tau_{\leqslant k}^{rel}Y)
to
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}(\pi^{rel}X,\pi^{rel}Y)
which is injective.

Let
h\colon X\to Y
be a morphism in
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}(\pi^{rel}X,\pi^{rel}Y)
. Suppose that
h
can be represented by the following right fraction

\textstyle{X^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s}
\scriptstyle{h^{\prime}}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{Y.}

Let
X^{\prime\prime}
be the cone of
s
. It is an object of
\mathrm{pvd}_{B}(A)
and therefore lies in
\mathcal{D}^{rel}_{>n}
for some
l\ll 0
. Therefore there are no morphisms from
\tau_{\leqslant l}^{rel}X
to
X^{\prime\prime}
and we have the following factorization

\textstyle{\tau_{\leqslant l}^{rel}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{0}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X^{\prime\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma X^{\prime}.}

We obtain an isomorphism of diagrams

\textstyle{X^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s}
\scriptstyle{h^{\prime}}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{Y}
\textstyle{\tau_{\leqslant l}^{rel}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}=h^{\prime}s^{\prime}}
.

Since
\tau_{\leqslant l}^{rel}X
is in
\mathcal{D}(A)_{rel}^{\leqslant l}
and
\tau_{>l}^{rel}Y
is in
\mathcal{D}(A)_{rel}^{>l}
, the morphism
f^{\prime}\colon\tau_{\leqslant l}^{rel}X\to Y
induces a morphism
f\colon\tau_{\leqslant l}^{rel}X\to\tau_{\leqslant l}^{rel}Y
which lifts the given morphism. Thus the map from
\varinjlim_{l\leqslant 0}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(A)}(\tau_{\leqslant l}^{rel}X,\tau_{\leqslant l}^{rel}Y)
to
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}(\pi^{rel}X,\pi^{rel}Y)
is surjective.

∎

4.4.
SMC reduction

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a Krull–Schmidt triangulated category and
\mathcal{S}
a subcategory of
\mathcal{T}
.

Definition 4.14
.

[
30
, Deﬁnition 2.4]

We call
\mathcal{S}
a
pre-simple-minded collection (pre-SMC)
if for any
X,Y\in\mathcal{S}
, the following conditions hold.

(1)

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{T}}(X,\Sigma^{<0}Y)=0
;

(2)

\mathrm{dim}_{k}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{T}}(X,Y)=\delta_{X,Y}.

We call
\mathcal{S}
a
simple-minded collection (SMC)
if
\mathcal{S}
is a pre-SMC and moreover,
\mathrm{thick}\,(\mathcal{S})=\mathcal{T}
.

Let
{\mathcal{S}}
be a pre-SMC. The
SMC reduction
of
\mathcal{T}
with respect to
\mathcal{S}
is defined as the following Verdier quotient
[
30
, Section 3.1]

\mathcal{U}\coloneqq\mathcal{T}/\mathrm{thick}(\mathcal{S}).

The subcategory
\mathrm{thick}\,(\mathcal{S})
admits a natural t-structure
(\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{S}},\mathcal{Y}_{\mathcal{S}})
, where
\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{S}}
is the smallest extension closed subcategory of
\mathcal{T}
containing any non-negative shift of
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathcal{S}}
is the smallest extension closed subcategory of
\mathcal{T}
containing any non-positive shift of
\mathcal{S}
(see
[
2
, Corollary 3 and Proposition 4]
,
[
45
, Proposition 5.4]
or
[
56
]
). Then the corresponding heart is denoted by
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{S}}
. It is equal to the smallest extension closed subcategory of
\mathcal{T}
containing
\mathcal{S}
.

Consider the following mild conditions:

(R1)

The heart
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{S}}
is contravariantly finite in the Hom-orthogonal subcategory
(\Sigma^{>0}\mathcal{S})^{\perp}
and covariantly finite in
{}^{\perp}(\Sigma^{<0}\mathcal{S})
.

(R2)

For any
X\in\mathcal{T}
, we have
\mathrm{Hom}_{{\mathcal{T}}}(X,\Sigma^{i}\mathcal{H}_{{\mathcal{S}}})=0=\mathrm{Hom}_{{\mathcal{T}}}(\mathcal{H}_{{\mathcal{S}}},\Sigma^{i}X)
for
i\ll 0
.

Proposition 4.15
.

[
30
, Proposition 3.2]

The following are equivalent.

(1)

(\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{S}},\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{S}}^{\perp})
and
({}^{\perp}\mathcal{Y}_{\mathcal{S}},\mathcal{Y}_{\mathcal{S}})
are t-structures on
\mathcal{T}
;

(2)

\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{S}}
satisfies the conditions
(R1)
and
(R2)
.

Let
\mathcal{W}
be the following subcategory of
\mathcal{T}

\mathcal{W}\coloneqq(\Sigma^{\geqslant 0}\mathcal{S})^{\perp}\cap{}^{\perp}(\Sigma^{\leqslant 0}\mathcal{S}).

Theorem 4.16
.

[
30
, Theorem 3.1]

Assume the assumptions
(R1)
and
(R2)
hold. Then the composition

\mathcal{W}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{T}\rightarrow\mathcal{U}

is a
k
-linear equivalence
\mathcal{W}\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathcal{U}
.

In our case, since the
k
-algebra
H^{0}(A)
is a finite dimensional
k
-algebra, we can suppose that
\boldmath{1}_{A}
has a decomposition

\boldmath{1}_{A}=e_{1}+\cdots+e_{n}

into primitive orthogonal idempotents
e_{i}
such that
e=f(\boldmath{1}_{B})=e_{1}+\cdots+e_{k}
for some
0\leqslant k\leqslant n
. Then
\mathrm{mod}_{B}H^{0}(A)
is generated by
\mathcal{S}=\{S_{k+1},S_{k+2},\cdots S_{n}\}
, where
S_{i}
is the simple
H^{0}(A)
module associated to the idempotent
e_{i}
.

Then it is easy to see that
\mathcal{S}
is a simple-minded collection of
\mathrm{pvd}_{B}(A)
and is a pre-simple-minded collection of
\mathrm{per}A
.

Corollary 4.17
.

The composition
\mathcal{W}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{per}A\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)=\mathrm{per}A/\mathrm{pvd}_{B}(A)
is a
k
-linear equivalence
\mathcal{W}\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
, where
\mathcal{W}
is the following subcategory of
\mathrm{per}A

\mathcal{W}=(\Sigma^{\geqslant 0}\mathcal{S})^{\perp}\cap\,^{\perp}(\Sigma^{\leqslant 0}\mathcal{S}).

In particular,
{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(A,B)
is idempotent complete.

Proof
.
It suffices to check the conditions (R1) and (R2). For any
X\in\mathrm{per}A
, it is easy to see that
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(X,\Sigma^{i}{\mathcal{H}}_{S})
vanishes for
i\ll 0
. By the relative Calabi–Yau duality (Corollary
3.13
), the space
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(\mathcal{H}_{{\mathcal{S}}},\Sigma^{i}X)
also vanishes for
i\ll 0
. Therefore
{\mathcal{H}}_{S}
satisfies the condition (R2). By Lemma
4.18
below, the category
\mathrm{mod}_{B}H^{0}(A)
is functorially finite in
\mathrm{per}A
. So
{\mathcal{H}}_{S}
satisfies the condition (R1). Then the claim follows from Theorem
4.16
.
∎

Lemma 4.18
.

Let
B\rightarrow A
be morphism between dg
k
-algebras which satisfies the assumptions
1
. Then
\mathrm{mod}_{B}H^{0}(A)
is functorially finite in
\mathrm{per}A
.

Proof
.
Let
P
be an object in
\mathrm{per}A
. Since
A
is connective, there is a canonical co-t-structure
((\mathrm{per}A)_{\geqslant 0},(\mathrm{per}A)_{\leqslant 0})
on
\mathrm{per}A
(
[
28
, Proposition 2.8]
), where

(\mathrm{per}A)_{\geqslant 0}\coloneqq\bigcup_{n\geqslant 0}\Sigma^{-n}\mathrm{add}A*\cdots*\Sigma^{-1}\mathrm{add}A*\mathrm{add}A\quad\text{and}\quad(\mathrm{per}A)_{\leqslant 0}\coloneqq\bigcup_{n\geqslant 0}\mathrm{add}A*\Sigma\mathrm{add}A*\cdots*\Sigma^{n}\mathrm{add}A.

Then we have a canonical triangle in
\mathrm{per}A

\sigma_{>0}P\rightarrow P\xrightarrow{t}\sigma_{\leqslant 0}P\rightarrow\Sigma\sigma_{>0}P

such that
\sigma_{>0}P\in(\mathrm{per}A)_{>0}
and
\sigma_{\leqslant 0}P\in(\mathrm{per}A)_{\leqslant 0}
. Consider the object
X=\tau_{\geqslant 0}(\sigma_{\leqslant 0}P)=H^{0}(\sigma_{\leqslant 0}P)
. It is easy to see that
\tau_{\geqslant 0}(\sigma_{\leqslant 0}P)
is in
\mathrm{mod}H^{0}(A)
and we have a canonical morphism
f\colon P\xrightarrow{t}\sigma_{\leqslant 0}P\rightarrow X
.

Let
M
be an object in
\mathrm{mod}H^{0}(A)
and
g\colon P\rightarrow M
a morphism. Since the space
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(\sigma_{>0}P,M)
vanishes, we have
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(P,M)\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(\sigma_{\leqslant 0}P,M)
. Then there exists a morphism
h\colon X\rightarrow M
such that the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{\leqslant 0}P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

This shows that
\mathrm{mod}H^{0}(A)
is covariantly finite in
\mathrm{per}A
. By
[
30
, Lemma 3.8]
, the subcategory
\mathrm{mod}_{B}H^{0}(A)
is functorially finite in
\mathrm{mod}H^{0}(A)
. Thus, the subcategory
\mathrm{mod}_{B}H^{0}(A)
is also covariantly finite in
\mathrm{per}A
. It remains to show
\mathrm{mod}_{B}H^{0}(A)
is contravariantly finite in
\mathrm{per}A
.

Let
N
be an object in
\mathrm{mod}_{B}H^{0}(A)
. Let
g^{\prime}\colon N\rightarrow P
be a morphism of dg
A
-modules. By the relative Calabi–Yau duality (see Corollary
3.13
), the spaces

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(N,\tau_{\leqslant-n-2}P)\simeq D\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(\tau_{\leqslant-n-2}P,\Sigma^{n+1}N)

and

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(N,\Sigma\tau_{\leqslant-n-2}P)\simeq D\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(\tau_{\leqslant-n-2}P,\Sigma^{n}N)

vanish. Thus, we have
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(N,P)\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(N,\tau_{\geqslant-n-1}P)
. We denote by
g^{\prime\prime}
the composition
N\xrightarrow{g^{\prime}}P\rightarrow\tau_{\geqslant-n-1}P
. Let
I_{P}
be a fibrant replacement of
\tau_{\geqslant-n-1}P
. Then we have
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(N,P)\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(N,\tau_{\geqslant-n-1}P)\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{{\mathcal{H}}(A)}(N,I_{P})
.

Since
\tau_{\geqslant-n-1}P
has finite dimensional total homology, the dg module
I_{P}
also has finite dimensional total homology. We write
I_{P}
as a
k
-complex and consider the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I^{-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{0}}

We put
L=\{x\in I^{0}\,|\,d^{0}(x)=0,xa=0,\forall a\in A^{p},p<0\}
. Then
L
is in
\mathrm{mod}H^{0}A
and
g^{\prime\prime}(N)
is contained in
L
. Thus, we have the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{P}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime\prime}}

Since the subcategory
\mathrm{mod}_{B}H^{0}(A)
is functorially finite in
\mathrm{mod}H^{0}(A)
, there exists an object
Y
in
\mathrm{mod}_{B}H^{0}(A)
with a right
\mathrm{mod}_{B}(H^{0}A)
-approximation
j\colon Y\rightarrow L
. Then there exists a morphism
k\colon N\rightarrow Y
such that the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{P}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}

This shows that
\mathrm{mod}_{B}H^{0}(A)
is contravariantly finite in
\mathrm{per}A
.

∎

Corollary 4.19
.

The relative cluster category
\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
is Hom-finite.

5.
Silting reduction and relative fundamental domain

5.1.
Silting reduction

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a triangulated category. A full subcategory
\mathcal{P}
of
\mathcal{T}
is
presilting
if
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{T}}(P,\Sigma^{i}P)=0
for any
i>0
. It is
silting
if in addition
\mathcal{T}=\mathrm{thick}\,\mathcal{P}
. An object
P
of
\mathcal{T}
is
presilting
if
\mathrm{add}P
is a presilting subcategory and
silting
if
\mathrm{add}P
is a silting subcategory.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a presilting subcategory of
\mathcal{T}
. Let
\mathcal{S}
be the thick subcategory
\mathrm{thick}\,\mathcal{P}
of
{\mathcal{T}}
and
\mathcal{U}
the quotient category
\mathcal{T}/\mathcal{S}
. We call
\mathcal{U}
the
silting reduction
of
\mathcal{T}
with respect to
\mathcal{P}
(see
[
1
]
). For an integer
l
, there is a bounded co-t-structure
(\mathcal{S}_{\geqslant l},\mathcal{S}_{\leqslant l})
on
\mathcal{S}
(see
[
28
, Proposition 2.8]
), where

\mathcal{S}_{\geqslant l}=\mathcal{S}_{>l-1}\coloneqq\bigcup_{i\geqslant 0}\Sigma^{-l-i}\mathcal{P}*\cdots*\Sigma^{-l-1}\mathcal{P}*\Sigma^{-l}\mathcal{P},

\mathcal{S}_{\leqslant l}=\mathcal{S}_{<l+1}\coloneqq\bigcup_{i\geqslant 0}\Sigma^{-l}\mathcal{P}*\Sigma^{-l+1}\mathcal{P}\cdots*\Sigma^{-l+i}\mathcal{P}.

Let
{\mathcal{Z}}
be the following subcategory of
{\mathcal{T}}

\mathcal{Z}={}^{\perp_{\mathcal{T}}}(\mathcal{S}_{<0})\cap(\mathcal{S}_{>0})^{\perp_{\mathcal{T}}}={}^{\perp_{\mathcal{T}}}(\Sigma^{>0}\mathcal{P})\cap(\Sigma^{<0}\mathcal{P})^{\perp_{\mathcal{T}}}.

Example 5.1
.

Let
{\mathcal{E}}
be a Frobenius category. Let
{\mathcal{T}}={\mathcal{D}}^{b}({\mathcal{E}})
be its bounded derived category and
{\mathcal{P}}
the projective-injective subcategory of
{\mathcal{E}}
. Then
{\mathcal{Z}}
is equal to
{\mathcal{E}}\subseteq{\mathcal{D}}^{b}({\mathcal{E}})
.

We consider the following mild technical conditions:

(P1)

\mathcal{P}
is covariantly finite in
{}^{\perp_{\mathcal{T}}}(\Sigma^{>0}\mathcal{P})
and contravariantly finite in
(\Sigma^{<0}\mathcal{P})^{\perp_{\mathcal{T}}}
.

(P2)

For any
X\in\mathcal{T}
, we have
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{T}}(X,\Sigma^{l}\mathcal{P})=0=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathcal{P},\Sigma^{l}X)
for
l\gg 0
.

Proposition 5.2
.

[
28
, Proposition 3.2]

The following conditions are equivalent.

(a)

The conditions (P1) and (P2) are satisfied.

(b)

The two pairs
({}^{\perp_{\mathcal{T}}}\mathcal{S}_{<0},\mathcal{S}_{\leqslant 0})
and
(\mathcal{S}_{\geqslant 0},\mathcal{S}_{>0}^{\perp_{\mathcal{T}}})
are co-t-structures on
\mathcal{T}
.

In this case, the co-hearts of these co-t-structures are
\mathcal{P}
.

Theorem 5.3
.

[
28
, Theorem 3.1]

Under the conditions
(P1)
and
(P2)
, the composition
\mathcal{Z}\subset\mathcal{T}\xrightarrow{\rho}\mathcal{U}
of natural functors induces an equivalence of additive categories:

\overline{\rho}\colon\mathcal{Z}/[\mathcal{P}]\longrightarrow\mathcal{U}.

Moreover, we have the following theorem.

Theorem 5.4
.

[
29
, Theorem 4.2]

The category
\mathcal{Z}/[\mathcal{P}]
has the structure of a triangulated category with respect to the following shift functor and triangles:

(a)

For
X\in\mathcal{Z}
, we take a triangle

X\xrightarrow{l_{X}}P_{X}\longrightarrow X\langle 1\rangle\longrightarrow\Sigma X

with a (fixed) left
\mathcal{P}
-approximation
l_{X}
. Then
\langle 1\rangle
gives a well-defined auto-equivalence of
\mathcal{Z}/[\mathcal{P}]
, which is the shift functor of
\mathcal{Z}/[\mathcal{P}]
.

(b)

For a triangle
X\longrightarrow Y\longrightarrow Z\longrightarrow\Sigma X
with
X,Y,Z\in\mathcal{Z}
, take the following commutative diagram of triangles

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{a}
\textstyle{\Sigma X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{l_{X}}
\textstyle{P_{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X\langle 1\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma X.}

Then we have a complex
X\xrightarrow{\overline{f}}Y\xrightarrow{\overline{g}}Z\xrightarrow{\overline{a}}X\langle 1\rangle.
We define triangles in
\mathcal{Z}/[\mathcal{P}]
as the complexes which are isomorphic to complexes obtained in this way.

Theorem 5.5
.

[
28
, Theorem 3.6]

The functor
\overline{\rho}\colon\mathcal{Z}/[\mathcal{P}]\longrightarrow\mathcal{U}
in Theorem
5.3
is a triangle equivalence where the triangulated structure of
\mathcal{Z}/[\mathcal{P}]
is given by Theorem
5.4
.

Remark 5.6
.

We remark that more general versions of Theorem
5.5
have been established in
[
49
]
.

In our case, we put
\mathcal{T}=\mathrm{per}A
,
\mathcal{P}=\mathrm{add}(eA)
, and
\mathcal{S}=\mathrm{thick}\,\mathcal{P}\cong\mathrm{per}(eAe)
. Then it is clear that the categories
\mathcal{T}
,
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{S}
satisfy the conditions
\rm(P1)
and
\rm(P2)
.

Corollary 5.7
.

We have the following equivalence of triangulated categories

p^{*}\colon\mathcal{Z}/[\mathcal{P}]\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathrm{per}A/\langle eA\rangle\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathrm{per}(\overline{A}),

where
\mathcal{Z}={}^{\perp_{\mathrm{per}A}}(\Sigma^{>0}\mathcal{P})\cap(\Sigma^{<0}\mathcal{P})^{\perp_{\mathrm{per}A}}
.

5.2.
The standard co-
t
-structure on
\mathrm{per}A

Proposition 5.8
.

[
28
, Proposition 2.8]

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a triangulated category and
\mathcal{M}
a silting subcategory of
\mathcal{T}
with
\mathcal{M}=\mathrm{add}\mathcal{M}
.

(a)

Then
(\mathcal{T}_{\geqslant 0},\mathcal{T}_{\leqslant 0})
is a bounded co-t-structure on
\mathcal{T}
, where

\mathcal{T}_{\geqslant 0}\coloneqq\bigcup_{k\geqslant 0}\Sigma^{-k}\mathcal{M}*\cdots*\Sigma^{-1}\mathcal{M}*\mathcal{M}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{T}_{\leqslant 0}\coloneqq\bigcup_{k\geqslant 0}\mathcal{M}*\Sigma\mathcal{M}*\cdots*\Sigma^{k}\mathcal{M}.

(b)

For any integers
m
and
l
, we have

\mathcal{T}_{\geqslant l}\cap\mathcal{T}_{\leqslant m}=\left\{\begin{aligned} \Sigma^{-m}\mathcal{M}*\Sigma^{-m+1}\mathcal{M}*\cdots*\Sigma^{-l}\mathcal{M}&&&&\text{if}\quad l\leqslant m,\\
0&&&&\text{if}\quad l>m.\end{aligned}\right.

Let
\Gamma
be a connective dg algebra. Then
\Gamma
is a silting object in
\mathrm{per}\Gamma
. By the above proposition, the pair
((\mathrm{per}\Gamma)_{\geqslant 0},(\mathrm{per}\Gamma)_{\leqslant 0})
is a co-t-structure on
\mathrm{per}\Gamma
, where

(\mathrm{per}\Gamma)_{\geqslant 0}\coloneqq\bigcup_{k\geqslant 0}\Sigma^{-k}\mathrm{add}\Gamma*\cdots*\Sigma^{-1}\mathrm{add}\Gamma*\mathrm{add}\Gamma\quad\text{and}\quad(\mathrm{per}\Gamma)_{\leqslant 0}\coloneqq\bigcup_{k\geqslant 0}\mathrm{add}\Gamma*\Sigma\mathrm{add}\Gamma*\cdots*\Sigma^{k}\mathrm{add}\Gamma.

The corresponding co-heart is
\mathrm{add}\Gamma
.

5.3.
Fundamental domain for generalized cluster categories

Let
\mathcal{F}
be the full subcategory
\mathcal{D}(\overline{A})^{\leqslant 0}\cap{}^{\perp}\mathcal{D}(\overline{A})^{\leqslant-n}\cap\mathrm{per}(\overline{A})
. In the paper
[
3
]
, it is called the
fundamental domain
of
\mathrm{per}\overline{A}
. We denote by
\pi\colon\mathrm{per}\overline{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{n}(\overline{A})
the canonical projection functor.

Lemma 5.9
.

[
18
, Lemma 3.2.8]

For each object
X
of
\mathcal{F}
, there exist
n-1
triangles (which are not unique in general)

P_{1}\to Q_{0}\to X\to\Sigma P_{1},

P_{2}\to Q_{1}\to P_{1}\to\Sigma P_{2},

\cdots

P_{n-1}\to Q_{n-2}\to P_{n-2}\to\Sigma P_{n-1},

where
Q_{0}
,
Q_{1}
,
\cdots
,
Q_{n-2}
and
P_{n-1}
are in
\mathrm{add}(\overline{A})
.

Remark 5.10
.

In fact, the fundamental domain
\mathcal{F}
is equal to

(\mathrm{per}\overline{A})_{\geqslant 1-n}\cap(\mathrm{per}\overline{A})_{\leqslant 0}=\mathrm{add}\overline{A}*\Sigma\mathrm{add}\overline{A}*\cdots*\Sigma^{n-1}\mathrm{add}\overline{A},

where
((\mathrm{per}\overline{A})_{\geqslant 0},(\mathrm{per}\overline{A})_{\leqslant 0})
is the canonical co-t-structure on
\mathrm{per}\overline{A}
.

Proposition 5.11
.

[
18
, Proposition 4.3.1]

The projection functor
\pi\colon\mathrm{per}\overline{A}\to\mathcal{C}_{n}(\overline{A})
induces a
k
-linear equivalence between
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{C}_{n}(\overline{A})
.

5.4.
Relative fundamental domain and Higgs category

Definition 5.12
.

We define the
relative fundamental domain
\mathcal{F}^{rel}
of
\mathrm{per}A
to be the following full subcategory

\mathcal{Z}\cap(\mathrm{per}A)_{\geqslant 1-n}\cap(\mathrm{per}A)_{\leqslant 0}=\mathcal{Z}\cap(\mathrm{add}A*\Sigma\mathrm{add}A*\cdots*\Sigma^{n-1}\mathrm{add}A),

where
((\mathrm{per}A)_{\geqslant 0},(\mathrm{per}A)_{\leqslant 0})
is the canonical co-t-structure on
\mathrm{per}A
and
\mathcal{Z}
is the subcategory

{}^{\perp_{\mathrm{per}A}}(\Sigma^{>0}\mathcal{P})\cap(\Sigma^{<0}\mathcal{P})^{\perp_{\mathrm{per}A}}

with
\mathcal{P}=\mathrm{add}(eA)
.

By the proof of
[
3
, Lemma 7.2.1]
(or
[
18
, Lemma 3.2.8]
), we can see that the subcategory
\mathrm{add}A*\Sigma\mathrm{add}A*\cdots*\Sigma^{n-1}\mathrm{add}A
is equal to
\mathcal{D}(A)^{\leqslant 0}\cap{}^{\perp}(\mathcal{D}(A)^{\leqslant-n})\cap\mathrm{per}(A)
. Thus, the relative fundamental domain
\mathcal{F}^{rel}
is also equal to
\mathcal{Z}\cap\mathcal{D}(A)^{\leqslant 0}\cap{}^{\perp}(\mathcal{D}(A)^{\leqslant-n})\cap\mathrm{per}(A)
.

Remark 5.13
.

The relative fundamental domain
\mathcal{F}^{rel}
is also equivalent to the full subcategory of
\mathcal{Z}\subseteq\mathrm{per}(A)
whose objects are the
X\in\mathcal{Z}
such that
X
fits into the following
n-1
triangles in
\mathrm{per}A

M_{1}\to N_{0}\to X\to\Sigma M_{1},

M_{2}\to N_{1}\to M_{1}\to\Sigma M_{2},

\cdots

M_{n-1}\to N_{n-2}\to M_{n-2}\to\Sigma M_{n-1}

with
N_{0}
,
N_{1}
,
\cdots
,
N_{n-2}
and
M_{n-1}
in
\mathrm{add}(A)
.

Proposition 5.14
.

The relative fundamental domain
\mathcal{F}^{rel}
is contained in

\mathcal{D}(A)_{rel}^{\leqslant 0}\cap{}^{\perp}(\mathcal{D}_{B}(A)_{rel}^{\leqslant-n})\cap\mathrm{per}(A),

where
\mathcal{D}_{B}(A)_{rel}^{\leqslant-n}
is the full subcategory of
\mathcal{D}(A)_{rel}^{\leqslant-n}
whose objects are the objects

X
in
\mathcal{D}(A)_{rel}^{\leqslant-n}
whose restriction
i_{*}(X)
to
eAe
is acyclic.

Proof
.
Let
X
be an object in
\mathcal{F}^{rel}=\mathcal{Z}\cap(\mathrm{add}A*\mathrm{add}A[1]*\cdots*\mathrm{add}A[n-1])
. Since
A,\Sigma A,\cdots,\Sigma^{n-1}A
are in
\mathcal{D}(A)_{rel}^{\leqslant 0}\cap{}^{\perp}(\mathcal{D}_{B}(A)_{rel}^{\leqslant-n})\cap\mathrm{per}(A)
, by using the triangles in Remark
5.13
, we see that
X
also lies in
\mathcal{D}(A)_{rel}^{\leqslant 0}\cap{}^{\perp}(\mathcal{D}_{B}(A)_{rel}^{\leqslant-n})\cap\mathrm{per}(A)
.

∎

We still denote by
p^{*}
the restriction of
p^{*}\colon\mathrm{per}(A)\to\mathrm{per}(\overline{A})
to
\mathcal{F}^{rel}
.

Proposition 5.15
.

The functor
p^{*}\colon\mathcal{F}^{rel}\to\mathcal{F}
is dense.

Proof
.
It is easy to see that
p^{*}
is well defined. Let
Y
be an object in
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathrm{per}\overline{A}
. By Lemma
5.9
, there exist
n-1
triangles in
\mathrm{per}\overline{A}

P_{1}\xrightarrow{b_{0}}Q_{0}\rightarrow Y\to\Sigma P_{1},

P_{2}\xrightarrow{b_{1}}Q_{1}\rightarrow P_{1}\to\Sigma P_{2},

\cdots

P_{n-2}\xrightarrow{b_{n-3}}Q_{n-3}\rightarrow P_{n-3}\to\Sigma P_{n-2},

P_{n-1}\xrightarrow{b_{n-2}}Q_{n-2}\rightarrow P_{n-2}\to\Sigma P_{n-1},

with
Q_{0}
,
Q_{1}
,
\cdots
,
Q_{n-2}
and
P_{n-1}
in
\mathrm{add}(\overline{A})
.

We start from the last triangle. Recall that
{\mathcal{Z}}
is the following subcategory of
\mathrm{per}A

{\mathcal{Z}}={}^{\perp}(\Sigma^{>0}\mathcal{P})\cap(\Sigma^{<0}\mathcal{P})^{\perp}

with
\mathcal{P}=\mathrm{add}(eA)
.

Since the functor
p^{*}\colon\mathrm{add}A\subseteq\mathcal{Z}\longrightarrow\mathrm{add}\overline{A}
is dense, there exist two objects
M^{\prime}_{n-1}
,
N^{\prime}_{n-2}
in
\mathrm{add}A
such that
p^{*}(M^{\prime}_{n-1})\cong P_{n-1}
and
p^{*}(N^{\prime}_{n-2})\cong Q_{n-2}
. We know that
p^{*}\colon\mathcal{Z}/[\mathcal{P}]\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathrm{per}A/\langle eA\rangle\rightarrow\mathrm{per}(\overline{A})
is fully faithful. Thus we have the following surjective map

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{Z}}(M^{\prime}_{n-1},N^{\prime}_{n-2})\twoheadrightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}(\overline{A})}(P_{n-1},Q_{n-2}).

We lift the map
b_{n-2}\colon P_{n-1}\rightarrow Q_{n-2}
from
\mathrm{add}(\overline{A})
to
\mathrm{add}(A)\subseteq\mathcal{Z}
. Then we get

g^{\prime}_{n-2}\colon M^{\prime}_{n-1}\rightarrow N^{\prime}_{n-1}
such that
p^{*}(g^{\prime}_{n-2})\cong b_{n-2}
.

Since
\mathcal{P}
is covariantly finite and contravariantly finite in
\mathcal{Z}
, we can find
h_{n-2}\colon M^{\prime}_{n-1}\rightarrow W_{n-2}
a left
\mathrm{add}({e}A)
-approximation of
M^{\prime}_{n-1}
. We define

(M_{n-1}\xrightarrow{g_{n-2}}N_{n-2})\coloneqq(M^{\prime}_{n-1}\xrightarrow{[g^{\prime}_{n-2},\ h_{n-2}]^{t}}N^{\prime}_{n-2}\oplus W_{n-2}).

Then we can see that
p^{*}(g_{n-2})\cong b_{n-2}
and the following map is surjective

g_{n-2}^{*}\colon\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(N_{n-2},eA)\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(M_{n-1},eA).

We form a triangle in
\mathrm{per}A

M_{n-1}\xrightarrow{g_{n-2}}N_{n-2}\rightarrow M_{n-2}\rightarrow\Sigma M_{n-1}.

Then
p^{*}(M_{n-2})
is isomorphic to
P_{n-2}
.

Since the map
g_{n-2}^{*}\colon\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(N_{n-2},eA)\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(M_{n-1},eA)
is surjective, we can see that
M_{n-2}
is an object in
\mathcal{Z}
.

Next, we consider the penultimate triangle. Repeating the above argument, we get a triangle in
\mathrm{per}A

M_{n-2}\xrightarrow{g_{n-3}}N_{n-3}\rightarrow M_{n-3}\rightarrow\Sigma M_{n-2}

such that
N_{n-3}\in\mathrm{add}A
,
p^{*}(N_{n-3})\cong Q_{n-3}
,
p^{*}(g_{n-3})\cong b_{n-3}
,
p^{*}(M_{n-3})\cong P_{n-3}
and

M_{n-3}\in\mathcal{Z}
.

Then, we keep repeating this argument until the first triangle. We get the following
n-1
triangles in
\mathrm{per}A

M_{1}\xrightarrow{g_{0}}N_{0}\rightarrow X\rightarrow\Sigma M_{1},

M_{2}\xrightarrow{g_{1}}N_{1}\rightarrow M_{1}\rightarrow\Sigma M_{2},

\cdots

M_{n-2}\xrightarrow{g_{n-3}}N_{n-3}\rightarrow M_{n-3}\rightarrow\Sigma M_{n-2},

M_{n-1}\xrightarrow{g_{n-2}}N_{n-2}\rightarrow M_{n-2}\rightarrow\Sigma M_{n-1}

such that
M_{n-1},N_{n-2},\cdots,N_{0}\in\mathrm{add}A
,
X\in\mathcal{Z}
and
p^{*}(X)\cong Y
.
Thus, the object
X
belongs to
\mathcal{F}^{rel}
and therefore
p^{*}\colon\mathcal{F}^{rel}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
is dense.

∎

Recall that the fundamental domain
\mathcal{F}
of
\mathrm{per}\overline{A}
is the full subcategory
\mathcal{D}(\overline{A})^{\leqslant 0}\cap

{}^{\perp}\mathcal{D}(\overline{A})^{\leqslant-n}\cap\mathrm{per}(\overline{A})
of
\mathrm{per}\overline{A}
. For any two objects in
{\mathcal{Z}}={}^{\perp}(\Sigma^{>0}\mathcal{P})\cap(\Sigma^{<0}\mathcal{P})^{\perp}
with
\mathcal{P}=\mathrm{add}(eA)
, we have the following useful lemma to compare their extension groups in
\mathrm{per}A
and
\mathrm{per}\overline{A}
.

Lemma 5.16
.

Let
X
and
Y
be two objects in
\mathcal{Z}
. Let
l>0
be an integer. Then we have

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(X,\Sigma^{l}Y)\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}(\overline{A})}(p^{*}(X),\Sigma^{l}p^{*}(Y)).

Proof
.
Let
X
and
Y
be two objects in
\mathcal{Z}
. For the object
Y
, we have the following triangle in
\mathrm{per}A

Y\xrightarrow{f_{1}}P_{Y_{1}}\rightarrow Y_{1}\rightarrow\Sigma Y,

where
f_{1}\colon Y\rightarrow P_{Y_{1}}
is a left
\mathrm{add}(eA)
-approximation. Since
f_{1}
is a a left
\mathrm{add}(eA)
-approximation, it is not hard to see that
Y_{1}
lies in
{\mathcal{Z}}
.

Similarly, for the object
Y_{1}
, we have the following triangle in
\mathrm{per}A

Y_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{2}}P_{Y_{2}}\rightarrow Y_{2}\rightarrow\Sigma Y_{1},

where
f_{2}\colon Y_{1}\rightarrow P_{Y_{2}}
is a left
\mathrm{add}(eA)
-approximation and
Y_{2}
lies in
{\mathcal{Z}}
.

Repeating this process, we can get the following
l
triangles in
\mathrm{per}A

Y\xrightarrow{f_{1}}P_{Y_{1}}\rightarrow Y_{1}\rightarrow\Sigma Y,

Y_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{2}}P_{Y_{2}}\rightarrow Y_{2}\rightarrow\Sigma Y_{1},

\cdots

Y_{l-1}\xrightarrow{f_{l}}P_{Y_{l}}\rightarrow Y_{l}\rightarrow\Sigma Y_{l-1},

where for each
1\leqslant i\leqslant l
,
f_{i}
is a left
\mathrm{add}(eA)
-approximation. Moreover each
Y_{i}
lies in
{\mathcal{Z}}
.

By the first triangle, we can see that

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(X,\Sigma^{l-1}Y_{1})\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(X,\Sigma^{l}Y).

Similarly, by the second triangle, we can see that

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(X,\Sigma^{l-2}Y_{2})\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(X,\Sigma^{l-1}Y_{1}).

Repeating this argument, we have

\begin{split}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(X,\Sigma^{l}Y)&\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(X,\Sigma^{l-1}Y_{1})\\
&\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(X,\Sigma^{l-2}Y_{2})\\
&\cdots\\
&\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(X,\Sigma Y_{l-1}).\end{split}

By the last triangle, it induces a long exact sequence

\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(X,P_{Y_{l}})\xrightarrow{\Phi}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(X,Y_{l})\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(X,\Sigma Y_{l-1})\rightarrow 0.

Thus we have

\begin{split}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(X,\Sigma^{l}Y)&\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(X,\Sigma Y_{l-1})\\
&\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(X,Y_{l})/\mathrm{Im}(\Phi)\\
&\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{Z}/[\mathcal{P}]}(X,Y\langle l\rangle)\\
&\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}(\overline{A})}(p^{*}(X),\Sigma^{l}p^{*}(Y)).\end{split}

∎

Proposition 5.17
.

The functor
p^{*}\colon\mathrm{per}A\rightarrow\mathrm{per}\overline{A}
is dense. Thus, we have equivalences

\mathcal{Z}/[\mathcal{P}]\simeq\mathrm{per}A/\langle eA\rangle\simeq\mathrm{per}\overline{A},

where
\mathcal{Z}={}^{\perp}(\Sigma^{>0}\mathcal{P})\cap(\Sigma^{<0}\mathcal{P})^{\perp}
with
\mathcal{P}=\mathrm{add}(eA)
.

Proof
.
There is a canonical co-t-structure
(\mathrm{per}(\overline{A})_{\geqslant 0},\mathrm{per}(\overline{A}))_{\leqslant 0}
on
\mathrm{per}(\overline{A})
, where

\mathrm{per}(\overline{A})_{\geqslant 0}=\bigcup_{n\geqslant 0}\Sigma^{-n}\mathrm{add}(\overline{A})*\cdots*\Sigma^{-1}\mathrm{add}(\overline{A})*\mathrm{add}(\overline{A}),

\mathrm{per}(\overline{A})_{\leqslant 0}=\bigcup_{n\geqslant 0}\mathrm{add}(\overline{A})*\Sigma\mathrm{add}(\overline{A})*\cdots*\Sigma^{n}\mathrm{add}(\overline{A}).

Let
Z
be an object in
\mathrm{per}(\overline{A})
. By using the canonical co-t-structure on
\mathrm{per}(\overline{A})
, we have a triangle in
\mathrm{per}(\overline{A})

X\rightarrow Z\rightarrow Y\xrightarrow{h}\Sigma X,

with
X\in\mathrm{per}(\overline{A})_{\geqslant 0}
and
Y\in\mathrm{per}(\overline{A})_{\leqslant 0}
.

We will find objects
U,V\in\mathcal{Z}\subseteq\mathrm{per}A
such that
p^{*}(U)\cong X
and
p^{*}(V)\cong Y
. Suppose that
X
is in
\Sigma^{-n_{0}}\mathrm{add}(\overline{A})*\cdots*\Sigma^{-1}\mathrm{add}(\overline{A})*\mathrm{add}(\overline{A})
and
Y
is in
\mathrm{add}(\overline{A})*\Sigma\mathrm{add}(\overline{A})*\cdots*\Sigma^{n_{1}}\mathrm{add}(\overline{A})
.

For the object
Y
, if
n_{1}=0
, it is easy to find such
V
. So we can assume that
n_{1}\geqslant 1
. Thus there are
n_{1}
triangles in
\mathrm{per}(\overline{A})

P_{1}\rightarrow Q_{0}\rightarrow Y\rightarrow\Sigma P_{1},

P_{2}\rightarrow Q_{1}\rightarrow P_{1}\rightarrow\Sigma P_{2},

\cdots

P_{n_{1}}\rightarrow Q_{n_{1}-1}\rightarrow P_{n_{1}-1}\rightarrow\Sigma P_{n_{1}},

with
P_{n_{1}},Q_{n_{1}-1},\cdots,Q_{0}\in\mathrm{add}(\overline{A})
.

It follows by a similar argument to that for Proposition
5.15
that there is an object
V\in\mathcal{Z}\subseteq\mathrm{per}A
such that
p^{*}(V)\cong Y
.

For the object
X\in\Sigma^{-n_{0}}\mathrm{add}(\overline{A})*\cdots*\Sigma^{-1}\mathrm{add}(\overline{A})*\mathrm{add}(\overline{A})
, we have
\Sigma^{n_{0}}X
in
\mathrm{add}(\overline{A})*\Sigma\mathrm{add}(\overline{A})*\cdots*\Sigma^{n_{0}}\mathrm{add}(\overline{A})
. Thus there exists an object
U^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}
such that
p^{*}(U^{\prime})\cong\Sigma^{n_{0}}X
.

Since
\mathcal{P}=\mathrm{add}(eA)
is covariantly finite and contravariantly finite in
\mathcal{Z}
, we can take the following
n_{0}+1
triangles in
\mathrm{per}A

U^{\prime}\langle-1\rangle\rightarrow R_{0}\xrightarrow{f_{0}}U^{\prime}\rightarrow\Sigma U^{\prime}\langle-1\rangle,

U^{\prime}\langle-2\rangle\rightarrow R_{-1}\xrightarrow{f_{-1}}U^{\prime}\langle-1\rangle\rightarrow\Sigma U^{\prime}\langle-2\rangle,

\cdots

U^{\prime}\langle-n_{0}-1\rangle\rightarrow R_{-n_{0}}\xrightarrow{f_{-n_{0}}}U^{\prime}\langle-n_{0}\rangle\rightarrow\Sigma U^{\prime}\langle-n_{0}-1\rangle

with
f_{i}
a right
\mathrm{add}(eA)
-approximation for any
-n_{0}\leqslant i\leqslant 0
. Then the object
p^{*}(U^{\prime})\cong\Sigma^{n_{0}}X
is isomorphic to
\Sigma^{n_{0}}p^{*}(U^{\prime}\langle-n_{0}\rangle)
. Let
U=U^{\prime}\langle-n_{0}\rangle
. Thus, we have
p^{*}(U)\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}X
.

Since
\mathcal{Z}/[\mathcal{P}]\cong\mathrm{per}A/\langle eA\rangle\rightarrow\mathrm{per}(\overline{A})
is fully faithful, the following map is a surjection (see Lemma
5.16
)

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{Z}}(V,\Sigma U\langle-n_{0}\rangle)\twoheadrightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{Z}/[\mathcal{P}]}(Y,\Sigma X)=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}(\overline{A})}(Y,\Sigma X).

We can lift the following triangle in
\mathrm{per}(\overline{A})

X\rightarrow Z\rightarrow Y\xrightarrow{h}\Sigma X,

to a triangle in
\mathrm{per}A

U\langle-n_{0}\rangle\rightarrow W\rightarrow V\xrightarrow{h^{\prime}}\Sigma U\langle-n_{0}\rangle.

Therefore, the object
p^{*}(W)
is isomorphic to
Z
. Hence the functor
p^{*}\colon\mathrm{per}A\rightarrow\mathrm{per}(\overline{A})
is dense.

∎

Corollary 5.18
.

We have a triangle equivalence

\textstyle{p^{*}\colon\mathrm{per}(A)/\mathrm{per}(eAe)\rightarrow\mathrm{per}(\overline{A}).}

Proof
.
It follows from Corollary
4.6
and Proposition
5.17
.
∎

Corollary 5.19
.

We have the following equivalence of
k
-categories

p^{*}\colon\mathcal{F}^{rel}/[\mathcal{P}]\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathcal{F}.

Proof
.
By Proposition
5.15
, we know that the quotient functor
\mathcal{F}^{rel}/[\mathcal{P}]\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
is dense. Since we have an equivalence
\mathcal{Z}/[\mathcal{P}]\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathrm{per}\overline{A}
, this quotient functor
\mathcal{F}^{rel}/[\mathcal{P}]\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
is also fully faithful. Thus the quotient functor
\mathcal{F}^{rel}/[\mathcal{P}]\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathcal{F}
is an equivalence of
k
-categories.
∎

Proposition 5.20
.

[
3
, proposition 7.2.1]

The restriction of the quotient functor
\pi^{rel}\colon\mathrm{per}A\to\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
to
\mathcal{F}^{rel}
is fully faithful.

Proof
.
Let
X
and
Y
be objects in
\mathcal{F}^{rel}\subseteq\mathcal{D}(A)_{rel}^{\leqslant 0}\cap{}^{\perp}(\mathcal{D}_{B}(A)_{rel}^{\leqslant-n})\cap\mathrm{per}(A)
. By Proposition
4.13
, the space
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}(\pi^{rel}X,\pi^{rel}Y)
is isomorphic to the direct limit
\varinjlim_{l<0}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(A)}(\tau_{\leqslant l}^{rel}X,\tau_{\leqslant l}^{rel}Y)
. A morphism between
X
and
Y
in
\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
is a diagram of the form

\textstyle{\tau_{\leqslant l}^{rel}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{Y.}

The canonical triangle

\Sigma^{-1}\tau_{>l}^{rel}X\to\tau_{\leqslant l}^{rel}X\to X\to\tau_{>l}^{rel}X

yields a long exact sequence:

\cdots\to\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(A)}(\tau_{>l}^{rel}X,Y)\to\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(A)}(X,Y)\to\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(A)}(\tau_{\leqslant l}^{rel}X,Y)\to\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(A)}(\Sigma^{-1}\tau_{>l}^{rel}X,Y)\to\cdots.

To remind the reader, the morphism
f\colon B\rightarrow A
has a left
(n+1)
-Calabi–Yau structure (see Assumption
1
). Since
i_{*}(\tau_{>l}^{rel}X)=0
, it satisfies the conditions of relative
(n+1)
-Calabi–Yau duality (see Corollary
3.13
), and so the space

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(A)}(\Sigma^{-1}\tau^{rel}_{>l}X,Y)

is isomorphic to the space
D\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(A)}(Y,\Sigma^{n}\tau^{rel}_{>l}X)
.

The object
X
is in
\mathcal{D}(A)_{rel}^{\leqslant 0}
, hence we have

\tau^{rel}_{>l}X\in\mathcal{D}_{B}(A)_{rel}^{\leqslant 0}
and then the space

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(A)}(Y,\Sigma^{n}\tau^{rel}_{>l}X)
vanishes.

For the same reasons, the space
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(A)}(\tau_{>l}^{rel}X,Y)
vanishes. Hence there are bijections

\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(A)}(\tau_{\leqslant l}^{rel}X,\tau_{\leqslant l}^{rel}Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(A)}(\tau_{\leqslant l}^{rel}X,Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(A)}(X,Y).}

Thus, the functor
\pi^{rel}:\mathcal{F}^{rel}\to\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)

is fully faithful.
∎

Corollary 5.21
.

We have an isomorphism

\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}(\pi^{rel}A)\simeq\mathrm{End}_{\mathrm{per}(A)}(A)=H^{0}(A)
.

Proof
.
This follows from Lemma
5.20
and the fact that
A
itself is in
\mathcal{F}^{rel}
.
∎

Definition 5.22
.

The
Higgs category

\mathcal{H}
is the image of
\mathcal{F}^{rel}
in
\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
under the quotient functor
\pi^{rel}\colon\mathrm{per}A\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
.

Remark 5.23
.

The reason for the name
“Higgs category”
is that this category generalizes the category of modules over the preprojective algebra of a Dynkin quiver and a module over the preprojective algebra can be called a
“Higgs module”
(in analogy with a
“Higgs bundle”
, which is the same object in a geometric context, see
[
22
,
57
]
).

5.5.
Relation with generalized cluster categories

In
[
18
]
, Lingyan Guo generalized Claire Amiot’s construction
[
3
]
of the generalized cluster categories to finite-dimensional algebras with global dimension at most
n
. She studied the category
\mathcal{C}_{n}(\Gamma)=\mathrm{per}\Gamma/\mathrm{pvd}(\Gamma)
associated with a dg algebra
\Gamma
under the following assumptions:

Assumptions 2
.

1)

\Gamma
is homologically smooth.

2)

\Gamma
is connective, i.e,
H^{p}(\Gamma)
is zero for each
p>0
.

3)

\Gamma
is
(n+1)
-Calabi–Yau as a bimodule, i.e. there is an isomorphism in
\mathcal{D}(\Gamma^{e})

\textstyle{\Sigma^{n+1}\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(\Gamma^{e})}(\Gamma,\Gamma^{e})\cong\Gamma.}

4)

The space
H^{0}(\Gamma)
is finite-dimensional.

Theorem 5.24
.

[
18
, Chapter 3]

Let
\Gamma
be a dg
k
-algebra with the four properties above. Then

(1)

the category
\mathcal{C}_{n}(\Gamma)=\mathrm{per}\,\Gamma/\mathrm{pvd}(\Gamma)
is Hom-finite and
n
-Calabi–Yau;

(2)

the object
T=\pi\Gamma
is an
n
-cluster tilting object in
\mathcal{C}_{n}(\Gamma)
where
\pi\colon\mathrm{per}\Gamma\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}_{n}(\Gamma)
is the canonical quotient functor, i.e. we have

\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(\Gamma)}(T,\Sigma^{r}T)=0\,\text{for}\quad r=1,\cdots,n-1,}

and for each object
L
in
\mathcal{C}_{n}(\Gamma)
, if the space
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(\Gamma)}(T,\Sigma^{r}L)
vanishes for each
r=1,\cdots,n-1
, then L belongs to
\mathrm{add}\,T
, the full subcategory of
\mathcal{C}_{n}(\Gamma)
consisting of direct summands of finite direct sums of copies of
T
;

(3)

the endomorphism algebra of
T
over
\mathcal{C}_{n}(\Gamma)
is isomorphic to
H^{0}(\Gamma)
.

By Proposition
4.4
and
4.5
, the dg algebra
\overline{A}
satisfies the assumptions
2
. We consider the associated generalized
n
-cluster category
\mathcal{C}_{n}(\overline{A})=\mathrm{per}\overline{A}/\mathrm{pvd}(\overline{A})
.

Proposition 5.25
.

We have the following equivalence of triangulated categories

p^{*}\colon\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)/\mathrm{per}(eAe)\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathcal{C}_{n}(\overline{A}).

Proof
.
By Corollary
5.18
, we have a triangle equivalence
p^{*}\colon\mathrm{per}(A)/\mathrm{per}(eAe)\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathrm{per}(\overline{A})
. Thus, it is enough to show that we have an equivalence of triangulated categories
p^{*}\colon\mathrm{pvd}_{B}(A)\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathrm{pvd}(\overline{A})
and the two subcategories
\mathrm{pvd}_{B}(A)
and
\mathrm{per}(eAe)
are left and right orthogonal to each other.

It is clear that the functor
p_{*}\colon\mathcal{D}(\overline{A})\to\ker(i_{*})
is an equivalence of triangulated categories. Then the restriction of
p^{*}
and
p^{!}
to
\ker(i_{*})
give a quasi-inverse of
p_{*}\colon\mathrm{pvd}(\overline{A})\to\mathrm{pvd}_{B}(A)
.

Let
X
be an object in
\mathrm{pvd}_{B}(A)
and let
Y
be an object in
\mathrm{per}(eAe)
. Then
i_{*}(X)
is acyclic. Thus, we have

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(A)}(X,i^{*}(Y))\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(eAe)}(i_{*}(X),Y)=0

and

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(A)}(i^{*}(Y),X)\cong D\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(A)}(X,\Sigma^{n+1}i^{*}(Y))=0,

where the second isomorphism is due to the relative Calabi–Yau property
3.13
. Thus, the categories
\mathrm{pvd}_{B}(A)
and
\mathrm{per}(eAe)
are left and right orthogonal to each other.

∎

Corollary 5.26
.

We have the following commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathrm{per}(eAe)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{per}(eAe)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{pvd}_{B}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\mathrm{per}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{pvd}(\overline{A})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{per}(\overline{A})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}_{n}(\overline{A})}

and the rows and columns are exact sequences of triangulated categories.

5.6.
Equivalence between the relative shifts of
\mathcal{F}^{rel}

Definition 5.27
.

Let
l\geqslant 0
be an integer. We define the
relative
l
-shifted fundamental domain
\mathcal{F}^{rel}\langle l\rangle
to be the following full subcategory of
\mathcal{Z}

\mathcal{F}^{rel}\langle l\rangle=\{X\in\mathcal{Z}\ |\ p^{*}(X)\in\Sigma^{l}\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathrm{per}(\overline{A})\},

where
\mathcal{Z}={}^{\perp_{\mathrm{per}A}}(\Sigma^{>0}\mathcal{P})\cap(\Sigma^{<0}\mathcal{P})^{\perp_{\mathrm{per}A}}
with
\mathcal{P}=\mathrm{add}(eA)
.

Remark 5.28
.

If
l=0
, then
\mathcal{F}^{rel}\langle 0\rangle=\{X\in\mathcal{Z}\ |\ p^{*}(X)\in\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathrm{per}(\overline{A})\}
is equal to
\mathcal{F}^{rel}
.

Our aim is to show (Proposition
5.35
) that the functor
\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-l}
induces an equivalence

{\mathcal{F}}^{rel}\langle l-1\rangle\rightarrow{\mathcal{F}}^{rel}\langle l\rangle.

Lemma 5.29
.

Let
l
be an integer. Then the subcategory
\mathcal{Z}
of
\mathrm{per}A
is stable under the relative truncation functors
\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant l},\tau^{rel}_{>l}\colon\mathrm{per}A\rightarrow\mathrm{per}A
, i.e.
\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant l}(\mathcal{Z})\subseteq\mathcal{Z}
and
\tau^{rel}_{>l}(\mathcal{Z})\subseteq\mathcal{Z}
.

Proof
.
Let
l
be an integer and let
X
be an object in
\mathcal{Z}
. We have a triangle in
\mathrm{per}A

\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant l}X\rightarrow X\rightarrow\tau^{rel}_{>l}X\rightarrow\Sigma\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant l}X.

By the relative Calabi–Yau property (see Corollary
3.13
) and
i_{*}(\tau^{rel}_{>l}X)=0
, we have

\begin{split}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(\tau^{rel}_{>l}X,\Sigma^{k}eA)&\simeq D\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(\Sigma^{k}eA,\Sigma^{n+1}\tau^{rel}_{>l}X)\\
&\simeq D\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(i^{*}(\Sigma^{k}eAe),\Sigma^{n+1}\tau^{rel}_{>l}X)\\
&\simeq D\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}(eAe)}(\Sigma^{k}eAe,\Sigma^{n+1}i_{*}(\tau^{rel}_{>l}X))\\
&=0\end{split}

for all
k
in
\mathbb{Z}
.
This implies that
\tau^{rel}_{>l}X
lies in
{}^{\perp}\Sigma^{>0}\mathcal{P}
. By the Calabi–Yau property again, the object
\tau^{rel}_{>l}X
lies
(\Sigma^{<0}\mathcal{P})^{\perp}
. Thus
\tau^{rel}_{>l}X
is in
\mathcal{Z}
.
For any
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have the following exact sequence

\cdots\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(X,\Sigma^{k}eA)\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant l}X,\Sigma^{k}eA)\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(\Sigma^{-1}\tau^{rel}_{>l}X,\Sigma^{k}eA)\rightarrow\cdots.

We see that
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant l}X,\Sigma^{k}eA)=0
, i.e.
\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant l}X
lies in
{}^{\perp}(\Sigma^{>0}\mathcal{P})
.

Since
X
is in
(\Sigma^{<0}\mathcal{P})^{\perp}
, the space
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(eA,\Sigma^{k}X)=H^{k}(Xe)
vanishes for any positive integer
k
. Then by the following computation

\begin{split}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(\Sigma^{<0}eA,\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant l}X)&\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}(eAe)}(\Sigma^{<0}eAe,i_{*}(\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant l}X))\\
&\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}(eAe)}(\Sigma^{<0}eAe,i_{*}(X))\\
&\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}(eAe)}(\Sigma^{<0}eAe,Xe),\end{split}

we see that
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(\Sigma^{<0}eA,\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant l}X)
vanishes, i.e.
\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant l}X
is in
(\Sigma^{<0}\mathcal{P})^{\perp}
. Thus
\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant l}X
is in
\mathcal{Z}
.

∎

Let
l
be a positive integer and
X
an object of
\mathcal{F}^{rel}\langle l\rangle
. Then the object
p^{*}(X)
lies in
\Sigma^{l}\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathrm{per}(\overline{A})
. Hence
\Sigma^{1-l}p^{*}(X)
is in
\Sigma\mathcal{F}
. By definition, there are
n-1
triangles related to the object
\Sigma^{1-l}p^{*}(X)
, i.e.
\Sigma^{1-l}p^{*}(X)
fits into the following
n-1
triangles in
\mathrm{per}(\overline{A})

P_{1}\rightarrow\Sigma Q_{0}\rightarrow\Sigma^{1-l}p^{*}(X)\xrightarrow{h_{0}}\Sigma P_{1},

P_{2}\rightarrow\Sigma Q_{1}\rightarrow P_{1}\xrightarrow{h_{1}}\Sigma P_{2},

\cdots

P_{n-2}\rightarrow\Sigma Q_{n-3}\rightarrow P_{n-3}\xrightarrow{h_{n-3}}\Sigma P_{n-2},

\Sigma P_{n-1}\rightarrow\Sigma Q_{n-2}\rightarrow P_{n-2}\xrightarrow{h_{n-2}}\Sigma^{2}P_{n-1},

where
Q_{0}
,
Q_{1}
,
\cdots
,
Q_{n-2}
and
P_{n-1}
are in
\mathrm{add}(\overline{A})
.

We denote by
\nu=?\otimes_{H^{0}(\overline{A})}D(H^{0}(\overline{A}))
the Nakayama functor on
\mathrm{mod}H^{0}(A)
. Then
\nu H^{0}(P_{n-1})
and
\nu H^{0}(Q_{n-2})
are injective
H^{0}(\overline{A})
-modules. Let
M^{\prime}
be the kernel of the morphism
\nu H^{0}(P_{n-1})\rightarrow\nu H^{0}(Q_{n-2})
. We define
M
to be
\Sigma^{l-1}p_{*}(M^{\prime})
. Then it is clear that
M
belongs to

\mathcal{D}(A)_{rel}^{\geqslant-l+1}=\{X\in\mathcal{D}(A)|\,i_{*}(X)=0,H^{i}(p^{!}X)\cong H^{i}(X)=0,\forall i<-l\}.

Lemma 5.30
.

The object
M=\Sigma^{l-1}p_{*}(M^{\prime})
is in
\mathcal{Z}
.

Proof
.
It is clear that
M
belongs to
\mathrm{pvd}_{B}(A)
. Then
M
is an object in
\mathcal{Z}
since
\mathrm{pvd}_{B}(A)
is a full subcategory of
\mathcal{Z}
.

∎

Lemma 5.31
.

[
18
, Lemma 3.2.9]

(1) There are isomorphisms of functors

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(\overline{A})}(?,\Sigma^{2-l}p^{*}(X))|_{\heartsuit(\overline{A})}\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(\overline{A})}(?,\Sigma^{2}P_{1})|_{\heartsuit(\overline{A})}\cong\cdots

\cdots\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(\overline{A})}(?,\Sigma^{n-1}P_{n-2})|_{\heartsuit(\overline{A})}\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\heartsuit(\overline{A})}(?,M^{\prime}).

(2) There is a monomorphism of functors

\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\heartsuit(\overline{A})}(?,M^{\prime})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(\overline{A})}(?,\Sigma^{n}P_{n-2})|_{\heartsuit(\overline{A})},
where
\heartsuit(\overline{A})=\mathrm{mod}H^{0}(\overline{A}).

By the above Lemma, the following two spaces are isomorphic

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}(\overline{A})}(M^{\prime},\Sigma^{2-l}p^{*}(X))\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}(\overline{A})}(M^{\prime},M^{\prime}).

By Lemma
5.16
, we have

\begin{split}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(M,\Sigma X)&\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}(\overline{A})}(p^{*}(M),\Sigma p^{*}(X))\\
&\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}(\overline{A})}(\Sigma^{l-1}M^{\prime},\Sigma p^{*}(X))\\
&\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}(\overline{A})}(M^{\prime},\Sigma^{2-l}p^{*}(X))\\
&\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}(\overline{A})}(M^{\prime},M^{\prime}).\end{split}

Let
\epsilon
be the preimage of the identity map on
M^{\prime}
under the isomorphism

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(A)}(M,\Sigma X)\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}(\overline{A})}(M^{\prime},M^{\prime}).

Then we form the corresponding triangle in
\mathrm{per}A

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}
\textstyle{\Sigma X.}

Similarly, let
\varepsilon^{\prime}
be the the preimage of the identity map on
M^{\prime}
under the isomorphism

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(\overline{A})}(M^{\prime},\Sigma^{2-l}p^{*}(X))\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\heartsuit(\overline{A})}(M^{\prime},M^{\prime}).

Then we form the corresponding triangle in
per(\overline{A})

\Sigma^{1-l}p^{*}(X)\rightarrow Y^{\prime}\rightarrow M^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon^{\prime}}\Sigma^{2-l}p^{*}(X).

We see that
p^{*}(Y)
is isomorphic to
\Sigma^{l-1}Y^{\prime}
.

Lemma 5.32
.

[
18
, Lemma 3.2.11]

The object
Y^{\prime}
is in the fundamental domain
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathrm{per}(\overline{A})
.

Lemma 5.33
.

The object
Y
is in
\mathcal{F}^{rel}\langle l-1\rangle
and
\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant l}Y
is isomorphic to
X
.

Proof
.

Step 1:
Y
is an object in
\mathcal{F}^{rel}\langle l-1\rangle
.

By Lemma
5.30
, the object
M
is in
\mathcal{Z}
. By the triangle (
5.6
), we can see that
Y
is in
\mathcal{Z}
. Then by Lemma
5.32
,
p^{*}(Y)\cong\Sigma^{l-1}Y^{\prime}
belongs to
\Sigma^{l-1}\mathcal{F}
. Thus, the object
Y
is in
\mathcal{F}^{rel}\langle l-1\rangle
.

Step 2:
\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-l}Y
is isomorphic to
X
.

Since
X\in\mathcal{D}(A)_{rel}^{\leqslant-l}
and
\tau^{rel}_{>-l}(Y)=p_{*}\Sigma^{l-1}H^{-l+1}(p^{*}(Y))\in\mathcal{D}(A)_{rel}^{\geqslant-l+1}
, the space
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(A)}(X,\tau^{rel}_{>-l}(Y))
is zero. Hence, we can obtain a commutative diagram of triangles

\textstyle{\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-l}Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\tau^{rel}_{>-l}(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-l}Y}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{2}}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{1}}
\textstyle{\Sigma X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\,.}

By the octahedral axiom, we have the following commutative diagram

\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{1}}
\textstyle{\Sigma X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{2}[1]}
\textstyle{\Sigma Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\tau^{rel}_{>-l}(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-l}Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma Y}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Cone}(\delta_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma\mathrm{Cone}(\delta_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{2}X}

and the object
\mathrm{Cone}(\delta_{1})
is isomorphic to
\Sigma\mathrm{Cone}(\delta_{2})
in
\mathrm{per}A
.

Since
\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-l}Y\in\mathcal{D}(A)^{\leqslant-l}_{rel}
and
X\in\mathcal{D}(A)^{\leqslant-l}_{rel}
,
\mathrm{Cone}(\delta_{2})
is also in
\mathcal{D}(A)^{\leqslant-l}_{rel}
. Thus
\Sigma\mathrm{Cone}(\delta_{2})
is in
\mathcal{D}(A)^{\leqslant-l-1}_{rel}
. On the other hand,
M
and
\tau^{rel}_{>-l}(Y)
are in
\mathcal{D}_{rel}^{\geqslant-l+1}(A)
. Thus
\mathrm{Cone}(\delta_{1})
is in
\mathcal{D}_{rel}^{\geqslant-l}
. Hence we can conclude that
\mathrm{Cone}(\delta_{1})\cong\Sigma\mathrm{Cone}(\delta_{2})
is zero. Thus, the relative truncation
\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-l}Y
of
Y
is isomorphic to
X
.
∎

Lemma 5.34
.

Let
l>0
be an integer. The image of the functor
\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-l}
restricted to
\mathcal{F}^{rel}\langle l-1\rangle
is in
\mathcal{F}^{rel}\langle l\rangle
and the functor
\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-l}\colon\mathcal{F}^{rel}\langle l-1\rangle\rightarrow\mathcal{F}^{rel}\langle l\rangle
is fully faithful.

Proof
.

Step 1: The image of the functor
\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-l}
restricted to
\mathcal{F}^{rel}\langle l-1\rangle
is in
\mathcal{F}^{rel}\langle l\rangle
.

Let
X
be an object in
\mathcal{F}^{rel}\langle l-1\rangle\subseteq\mathcal{Z}
. By Lemma
5.29
,
\tau_{\leqslant-l}^{rel}X
is still in
\mathcal{Z}
. It is clear that
p^{*}(\tau_{\leqslant-l}^{rel}X)\cong\tau_{\leqslant-l}(p^{*}(X))
is in
\mathcal{D}(\overline{A})^{\leqslant-l}
.

We have a triangle in
\mathrm{per}(\overline{A})

\Sigma^{-1}\tau_{>-l}(p^{*}(X))\rightarrow\tau_{\leqslant-l}(p^{*}(X))\rightarrow p^{*}(X)\rightarrow\tau_{>-l}(p^{*}(X)).

Let
W
be an object in
\mathcal{D}(\overline{A})^{\leqslant-n-l}
. The space
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(\overline{A})}(p^{*}(X),W)
is zero since
p^{*}(X)\in{}^{\perp}(\mathcal{D}(\overline{A})^{\leqslant-l-n+1})
. By the Calabi–Yau property, we have

\begin{split}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(\overline{A})}(\Sigma^{-1}\tau_{>-l}(p^{*}(X)),W)&\cong D\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(\overline{A})}(W,\Sigma^{n}\tau_{>-l}(p^{*}(X))).\end{split}

The space
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(\overline{A})}(W,\Sigma^{n}\tau_{>-l}(p^{*}(X)))
vanishes because
\Sigma^{n}\tau_{>-l}(p^{*}(X))\in\mathcal{D}(\overline{A})^{\geqslant-l-n+1}
. Thus
p^{*}(\tau_{\leqslant-l}^{rel}X)
is in
\Sigma^{l}\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathrm{per}(\overline{A})
and then

\tau_{\leqslant-l}^{rel}X
belongs to
\mathcal{F}^{rel}\langle l\rangle
.

Step 2: The functor
\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-l}\colon\mathcal{F}^{rel}\langle l-1\rangle\rightarrow\mathcal{F}^{rel}\langle l\rangle
is fully faithful.

Let
X
and
Y
be two objects in
\mathcal{F}^{rel}\langle l-1\rangle
and
f\colon\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-l}X\rightarrow\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-l}Y
be a morphism.

\textstyle{\Sigma^{-1}\tau^{rel}_{>-l}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-l}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\tau^{rel}_{>-l}X}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{-1}\tau^{rel}_{>-l}Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-l}Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\tau^{rel}_{>-l}Y.}

By the relative Calabi–Yau property, the space
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(A)}(\Sigma^{-1}\tau^{rel}_{>-l}X,Y)
is isomorphic to

D\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(A)}(Y,\Sigma^{n}\tau^{rel}_{>-l}X)
. Since
Y\in{}^{\perp}(\mathcal{D}(A)^{\leqslant-n-l+1})
and
\Sigma^{n}\tau^{rel}_{>-l}X\in\mathcal{D}(A)^{\leqslant-n-l+1}
, this space is zero. Then the composition
gf
factorizes through the canonical morphism
\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-l}X\rightarrow X
. Thus the functor
\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-l}\colon\mathcal{F}\langle l-1\rangle\rightarrow\mathcal{F}\langle l\rangle
is full.

Now let
X
and
Y
be objects of
\mathcal{F}^{rel}\langle l-1\rangle
and
f\colon X\rightarrow Y
a morphism satisfying
\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-l}f=0
. Then it induces a morphism of triangles:

\textstyle{\Sigma^{-1}\tau^{rel}_{>-l}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-l}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 8.19447pth}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\tau^{rel}_{>-l}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{-1}\tau^{rel}_{>-l}Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-l}Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\tau^{rel}_{>-l}Y.}

The composition
fh
vanishes, so
f
factorizes through
\tau^{rel}_{>-l}X
. By the relative Calabi–Yau property, the space
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{(}D)(A)}(\tau^{rel}_{>-l}X,Y)
is isomorphic to
D\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{(}D)(A)}(Y,\Sigma^{n+1}\tau^{rel}_{>-l}X)
which is zero because
Y
lies in
{}^{\perp}(\mathcal{D}(A)^{\leqslant-n-l+1})
and
\Sigma^{n+1}\tau^{rel}_{>-l}X\in\mathcal{D}(A)^{\leqslant-l-n}
. Thus
f=0
, i.e, the functor

\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-l}\colon\mathcal{F}^{rel}\langle l-1\rangle\rightarrow\mathcal{F}^{rel}\langle l\rangle

is faithful.

∎

Proposition 5.35
.

For any positive integer
l
, the functor
\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-l}
induces an equivalence from
\mathcal{F}^{rel}\langle l-1\rangle
to
\mathcal{F}^{rel}\langle l\rangle
.

Proof
.
This follows from Lemma
5.33
and Lemma
5.34
.
∎

Proposition 5.36
.

Let
X
and
Y
be two objects in the Higgs category
\mathcal{H}
. Let
l>0
be an integer. Then we have

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}(X,\Sigma^{l}Y)\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(\overline{A})}(p^{*}(X),\Sigma^{l}p^{*}(Y))

Proof
. For the object
Y
, we have the following triangle in
\mathrm{per}A

Y\xrightarrow{f_{1}}P_{Y_{1}}\xrightarrow{g_{1}}Y_{1}\rightarrow\Sigma Y,

where
f_{1}\colon Y\rightarrow P_{Y_{1}}
is a left
\mathrm{add}(eA)
-approximation and
Y_{1}\in\mathcal{F}^{rel}\langle 1\rangle
(see proof of Lemma
5.16
). Since
\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-1}\colon\mathcal{F}^{rel}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}^{rel}\langle 1\rangle
is an equivalence, there is an object
W_{1}\in\mathcal{F}^{rel}
such that
\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-1}W_{1}\cong Y_{1}
. Thus we get a triangle in
\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)

Y\xrightarrow{\pi^{rel}(f_{1})}P_{Y_{1}}\xrightarrow{\pi^{rel}(g_{1})}W_{1}\rightarrow\Sigma Y.

For the object
W_{1}
, we have the following triangle in
\mathrm{per}A

W_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{2}}P_{Y_{2}}\xrightarrow{g_{2}}Y_{2}\rightarrow\Sigma W_{1},

where
f_{2}\colon W_{1}\rightarrow P_{Y_{2}}
is a left
\mathrm{add}(eA)
-approximation and
Y_{2}\in\mathcal{F}^{rel}\langle 1\rangle
. For the same reason, there is an object
W_{2}\in\mathcal{F}^{rel}
such that
\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-1}W_{2}\cong Y_{2}
. Thus we get a triangle in
\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)

W_{1}\xrightarrow{\pi^{rel}(f_{2})}P_{Y_{2}}\xrightarrow{\pi^{rel}(g_{2})}W_{2}\rightarrow\Sigma W_{1}.

Repeating this process, we can get the following
l
triangles in
\mathrm{per}A

Y\xrightarrow{f_{1}}P_{Y_{1}}\xrightarrow{g_{1}}Y_{1}\rightarrow\Sigma Y,

W_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{2}}P_{Y_{2}}\xrightarrow{g_{1}}Y_{2}\rightarrow\Sigma W_{1},

\cdots

W_{l-2}\xrightarrow{f_{l-1}}P_{Y_{l-1}}\xrightarrow{g_{l-1}}Y_{l-1}\rightarrow\Sigma W_{l-2},

W_{l-1}\xrightarrow{f_{l}}P_{Y_{l}}\xrightarrow{g_{l}}Y_{l}\rightarrow\Sigma W_{l-1}

where for each
1\leqslant i\leqslant l
,
f_{i}
is a left
\mathrm{add}(eA)
-approximation,
Y_{i}
is in
\mathcal{F}^{rel}\langle 1\rangle
and
\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-1}W_{i}\cong Y_{i}
.

Thus we get
l
triangles in
\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)

Y\xrightarrow{\pi^{rel}(f_{1})}P_{Y_{1}}\xrightarrow{\pi^{rel}(g_{1})}W_{1}\rightarrow\Sigma Y,

W_{1}\xrightarrow{\pi^{rel}(f_{2})}P_{Y_{2}}\xrightarrow{\pi^{rel}(g_{1})}W_{2}\rightarrow\Sigma W_{1},

\cdots

W_{l-2}\xrightarrow{\pi^{rel}(f_{l-1})}P_{Y_{l-1}}\xrightarrow{\pi^{rel}(g_{l-1})}W_{l-1}\rightarrow\Sigma W_{l-2},

W_{l-1}\xrightarrow{\pi^{rel}(f_{l})}P_{Y_{l}}\xrightarrow{\pi^{rel}(g_{l})}W_{l}\rightarrow\Sigma W_{l-1}.

Then we have

\begin{split}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}(X,\Sigma^{l}Y)&\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}(X,\Sigma^{l-1}W_{1})\\
&\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}(X,\Sigma^{l-2}W_{2})\\
&\cdots\\
&\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}(X,\Sigma W_{l-1})\end{split}

By the last triangle, we have the following exact sequence

\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}(X,P_{Y_{l}})\xrightarrow{\Phi}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}(X,W_{l})\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}(X,\Sigma W_{l-1})\rightarrow 0.

Thus, we have

\begin{split}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}}(A,B)(X,\Sigma^{l}Y)&\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}(X,\Sigma W_{l-1})\\
&\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}(X,W_{l})/\mathrm{Im}(\Phi)\\
&\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(X,W_{l})/\mathrm{Im}(\Phi)\\
&\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}(\overline{A})}(p^{*}(X),p^{*}(W_{l}))\\
&\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(\overline{A})}(p^{*}(X),p^{*}(W_{l}))\\
&\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(\overline{A})}(p^{*}(X),\tau_{\leqslant-1}p^{*}(W_{l}))\\
&\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(\overline{A})}(p^{*}(X),p^{*}(\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-1}(W_{l})))\\
&\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(\overline{A})}(p^{*}(X),p^{*}(Y_{l}))\\
&\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(\overline{A})}(p^{*}(X),\Sigma^{l}p^{*}(Y)).\end{split}

∎

Proposition 5.37
.

[
18
, Proposition 4.8.1]

Suppose that
X
and
Y
are two objects in
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{n}(\overline{A})
. Then there is a long exact sequence

0\rightarrow\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathcal{D}(\overline{A})}^{1}(X,Y)\rightarrow\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(\overline{A})}^{1}(X,Y)\rightarrow D\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathcal{D}(\overline{A})}^{n-1}(X,Y)

\rightarrow\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathcal{D}(\overline{A})}^{2}(X,Y)\rightarrow\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(\overline{A})}^{2}(X,Y)\rightarrow D\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathcal{D}(\overline{A})}^{n-2}(X,Y)

\rightarrow\qquad\cdots\qquad\rightarrow

\rightarrow\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathcal{D}(\overline{A})}^{n-1}(X,Y)\rightarrow\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(\overline{A})}^{n-1}(X,Y)\rightarrow D\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathcal{D}(\overline{A})}^{1}(X,Y)\rightarrow 0.

Corollary 5.38
.

Suppose that
X
and
Y
are two objects in the Higgs category
\mathcal{H}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
. Then there is a long exact sequence

0\rightarrow\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathcal{D}(A)}^{1}(X,Y)\rightarrow\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}^{1}(X,Y)\rightarrow D\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathcal{D}(A)}^{n-1}(X,Y)

\rightarrow\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathcal{D}(A)}^{2}(X,Y)\rightarrow\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}^{2}(X,Y)\rightarrow D\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathcal{D}(\overline{A})}^{n-2}(X,Y)

\rightarrow\qquad\cdots\qquad\rightarrow

\rightarrow\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathcal{D}(A)}^{n-1}(X,Y)\rightarrow\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}^{n-1}(X,Y)\rightarrow D\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathcal{D}(A)}^{1}(X,Y)\rightarrow 0.

Proof
.
This follows from Proposition
5.16
, Proposition
5.36
and Proposition
5.37
.

∎

Proposition 5.39
.

The Higgs category
\mathcal{H}
is an extension closed subcategory of
\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
.

Proof
.
Let
X
and
Y
be two objects in
\mathcal{H}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
. For the object
Y
, we take a triangle in
\mathrm{per}A

Y\xrightarrow{f_{1}}P_{Y_{1}}\rightarrow Y_{1}\xrightarrow{\phi_{1}}\Sigma Y,

where
f_{1}\colon Y\rightarrow P_{Y_{1}}
is a fixed left
\mathrm{add}(eA)
-approximation and
Y_{1}\in\mathcal{F}^{rel}\langle 1\rangle
. Then we can get a triangle in
\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)

Y\xrightarrow{\pi^{rel}(f_{1})}P_{Y_{1}}\rightarrow Y_{1}\xrightarrow{\pi^{rel}(\phi_{1})}\Sigma Y.

This induces a long exact sequence

\cdots\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}(X,Y_{1})\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}(X,\Sigma Y)\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}(X,\Sigma P_{Y_{1}})\rightarrow\cdots.

Since
P_{Y_{1}}\in\mathrm{pvd}_{B}(A)^{\perp}
, we have

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}(X,\Sigma P_{Y_{1}})\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(X,\Sigma P_{Y_{1}})=0.

Thus we get the following surjective map

\cdots\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}(X,Y_{1})\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}(X,\Sigma Y)\rightarrow 0.

For the object
X
, we have a canonical triangle in
\mathrm{per}A

\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-1}X\rightarrow X\rightarrow\tau^{rel}_{\geqslant 0}X\rightarrow\Sigma\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-1}X.

Hence,
\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-1}X
is isomorphic to
X
in
\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
. Then we get the following exact sequence

\cdots\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}(\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-1}X,Y_{1})\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}(X,\Sigma Y)\rightarrow 0.

It is clear that
\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-1}X
and
Y_{1}
are in
\mathcal{F}^{rel}\langle 1\rangle
. Since
\pi^{rel}\colon\mathcal{F}^{rel}\cong\mathcal{F}^{rel}\langle 1\rangle\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
is also fully faithful, we have that the space
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}(\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-1}X,Y_{1})
is isomorphic to
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-1}X,Y_{1})
and the following sequence is exact

\cdots\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-1}X,Y_{1})\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}(X,\Sigma Y)\rightarrow 0.

Let
\epsilon
be an element in
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}(X,\Sigma Y)
. We suppose that the corresponding triangle in
\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
is given by

Y\rightarrow W\rightarrow X\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}\Sigma Y.

We need to show that
W
is also in
\mathcal{H}
.

Since the map
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-1}X,Y_{1})\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}(X,\Sigma Y)
is surjective, there is a morphism
\epsilon^{\prime}\colon\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-1}X\rightarrow Y_{1}
in
\mathrm{per}A
such that
\pi^{rel}(\phi_{1}\circ\varepsilon^{\prime})\cong\varepsilon
in
\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
.

We take a triangle in
\mathrm{per}A

Y\rightarrow W_{1}\rightarrow\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-1}X\xrightarrow{\phi_{1}\circ\varepsilon^{\prime}}\Sigma Y.

Then, the following morphism of triangles in
\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
is an isomorphism

\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\boldmath{1}_{Y}}
\textstyle{W_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-1}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{rel}(\phi_{1}\circ\varepsilon^{\prime})}
\textstyle{\Sigma Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\boldmath{1}_{Y}}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}
\textstyle{\Sigma Y.}

In particular,
W_{1}
is isomorphic to
W
in
\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
.

Since
Y
and
\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-1}X
are in
\mathcal{Z}\subseteq\mathrm{per}A
,
W_{1}
is also in
\mathcal{Z}
. It is easy to see that

p^{*}(Y)\in\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{D}(\overline{A})^{\leqslant 0}\cap{}^{\perp}(\mathcal{D}(\overline{A})^{\leqslant-n})\cap\mathrm{per}(\overline{A})

and

p^{*}(\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-1}X)\cong\tau_{\leqslant-1}(p^{*}(X))\in\Sigma\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{D}(\overline{A})^{\leqslant-1}\cap{}^{\perp}(\mathcal{D}(\overline{A})^{\leqslant-n-1})\cap\mathrm{per}(\overline{A}).

Then by the triangle in
\mathrm{per}A

Y\rightarrow W_{1}\rightarrow\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-1}X\xrightarrow{\phi_{1}\circ\varepsilon^{\prime}}\Sigma Y,

we can see that
p^{*}(W_{1})
is in
\mathcal{D}(\overline{A})^{\leqslant 0}\cap{}^{\perp}(\mathcal{D}(\overline{A})^{\leqslant-n-1})\cap\mathrm{per}(\overline{A})
.

Next we consider the object
\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-1}W_{1}\in\mathrm{per}A
. Since
W_{1}
is in
\mathcal{Z}
,
\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-1}W_{1}
is still in
\mathcal{Z}
. And we have a canonical triangle in
\mathrm{per}(\overline{A})

\tau_{\leqslant-1}(p^{*}(W_{1}))\rightarrow p^{*}(W_{1})\rightarrow\tau_{\geqslant 0}(p^{*}(W_{1}))\rightarrow\Sigma\tau_{\leqslant-1}(p^{*}(W_{1})).

Because
p^{*}(W_{1})
is in
\mathcal{D}(\overline{A})^{\leqslant 0}\cap{}^{\perp}(\mathcal{D}(\overline{A})^{\leqslant-n-1})\cap\mathrm{per}(\overline{A})
, we have

\tau_{\leqslant-1}(p^{*}(W_{1}))\in\mathcal{D}(\overline{A})^{\leqslant-1}\cap{}^{\perp}(\mathcal{D}(\overline{A})^{\leqslant-n-1})\cap\mathrm{per}(\overline{A})=\Sigma\mathcal{F}.

Thus the object
\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-1}W_{1}
is in
\mathcal{F}^{rel}\langle 1\rangle
. By the equivalence
\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-1}\colon\mathcal{F}^{rel}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}^{rel}\langle 1\rangle
, there exists an object
W_{2}\in\mathcal{F}^{rel}
such that
\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-1}W_{2}\cong\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-1}W_{1}
.

Since
W_{2}
and
W_{1}
are isomorphic in
\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
,
W_{2}
is isomorphic to
W
in
\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
. Thus
W
is an object in
\mathcal{H}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
. Therefore,
\mathcal{H}
is an extension closed subcategory of
\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
.

∎

Recall that a full subcategory
\mathcal{P}
of a triangulated category
\mathcal{T}
is presilting if
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathcal{P},\Sigma^{>0}\mathcal{P})=0
.

Proposition 5.40
.

(1)

\mathcal{P}=\mathrm{add}(eA)
is a presilting subcategory of
\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)=\mathrm{per}A/\mathrm{pvd}_{B}(A)
.

(2)

\mathcal{P}
is covariantly finite in
{}^{\perp_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}}(\Sigma^{>0}\mathcal{P})
and contravariantly finite in
(\Sigma^{<0}\mathcal{P})^{\perp_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}}
.

(3)

For any
X\in\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
, we have
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}(X,\Sigma^{l}P)=0=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}(P,\Sigma^{l}X)
for
l\gg 0
.

Proof
.
For
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
,
X\in\mathrm{per}A
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have isomorphisms

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(P,\Sigma^{m}X)\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathrm{Hom}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(A,B)}(P,\Sigma^{m}X)

and

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(X,\Sigma^{m}P)\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathrm{Hom}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(A,B)}(X,\Sigma^{m}P)

because
{\mathcal{P}}
is left orthogonal and right orthogonal to
\mathrm{pvd}_{B}(A)
. This implies
(1)
,
(2)
and
(3)
.
∎

Corollary 5.41
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be the following additive subcategory of
\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)

\mathcal{E}={}^{\perp_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}}(\Sigma^{>0}\mathcal{P})\cap(\Sigma^{<0}\mathcal{P})^{\perp_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}}.

Then the composition
\mathcal{E}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)\xrightarrow{p^{*}}\mathcal{C}_{n}(\overline{A})
induces a triangle equivalence

\mathcal{E}/[\mathcal{P}]\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{C}_{n}(\overline{A}).

Proof
.
This follows from Proposition
5.40
and Theorem
5.3
.
∎

Theorem 5.42
.

The Higgs category
\mathcal{H}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
is equal to
\mathcal{E}={}^{\perp_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}}(\Sigma^{>0}\mathcal{P})\cap(\Sigma^{<0}\mathcal{P})^{\perp_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}}
. In particular, the Higgs category
\mathcal{H}
is idempotent complete.

Proof
.
It is clear that we have the inclusion
\mathcal{H}\subseteq\mathcal{E}
. Let
X
be an object in
\mathcal{E}={}^{\perp_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}}(\Sigma^{>0}\mathcal{P})\cap(\Sigma^{<0}\mathcal{P})^{\perp_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}}
. We also view it as an object of
\mathrm{per}A
.

Since
\mathrm{per}(eAe)
and
\mathrm{pvd}_{B}(A)
are left orthogonal and right orthogonal to each other, we see that
X
is in
\mathcal{Z}={}^{\perp_{\mathrm{per}A}}(\Sigma^{>0}\mathcal{P})\cap(\Sigma^{<0}\mathcal{P})^{\perp_{\mathrm{per}A}}\subseteq\mathrm{per}A
.

For the object
p^{*}(X)\in\mathrm{per}(\overline{A})
, there exists a non-negative integer
r
such that
p^{*}(X)
is in
{}^{\perp}(\mathcal{D}(\overline{A})^{\leqslant-n-r})
. We consider the object
X^{\prime}=\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-r}X
. Then
X^{\prime}
becomes isomorphic to
X
in
\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
and
X^{\prime}
belongs to
\mathcal{F}^{rel}\langle r\rangle
. By Proposition
5.35
, there exists an object
Y
in
\mathcal{F}^{rel}
such that
Y
is isomorphic to
X^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
. Thus,
X
is in the image of
\mathcal{F}^{rel}
, i.e.
X
belongs to
\mathcal{H}
. Hence
\mathcal{H}
is equal to
\mathcal{E}
.

∎

Theorem 5.43
.

For any object
X\in\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
, there exists
l\in\mathbb{Z}
,
F\in\mathcal{H}
and
P\in\mathrm{per}(eAe)
, such that we have a triangle in
\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 12.05557pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-12.05557pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Sigma^{l}F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 36.05557pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 36.05557pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 75.125pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 75.125pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 112.93402pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 112.93402pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Sigma^{l+1}F}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Dually, there exist
m\in\mathbb{Z}
,
F^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}
and
P^{\prime}\in\mathrm{per}(eAe)
, such that we have a triangle in
\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
