\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 8.3073pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-8.3073pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{P^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 32.3073pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 32.3073pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 71.37672pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 71.37672pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Sigma^{m}F^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 126.82008pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 126.82008pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Sigma P^{\prime}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Proof
. We only show the first statement since the second statement can be shown dually. Let
X
be an object in
\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
. We view it as an object in
\mathrm{per}A
. There exists a positive integer
r_{1}
such that the object
X
is in
\mathcal{D}(A)^{\leqslant r_{1}}
. Consider the object
\Sigma^{r_{1}}X
. Then
\Sigma^{r_{1}}X
lies in
\mathcal{D}(A)^{\leqslant 0}
.

By Proposition
5.2
, the pairs
({}^{\perp_{\mathcal{T}}}\mathcal{S}_{<0},\mathcal{S}_{\leqslant 0})
and
(\mathcal{S}_{\geqslant 0},\mathcal{S}_{>0}^{\perp_{\mathcal{T}}})
are co-t-structures on
{\mathcal{T}}=\mathrm{per}A
, where

\mathcal{S}_{\geqslant l}=\mathcal{S}_{>l-1}\coloneqq\bigcup_{i\geqslant 0}\Sigma^{-l-i}\mathcal{P}*\cdots*\Sigma^{-l-1}\mathcal{P}*\Sigma^{-l}\mathcal{P},

\mathcal{S}_{\leqslant l}=\mathcal{S}_{<l+1}\coloneqq\bigcup_{i\geqslant 0}\Sigma^{-l}\mathcal{P}*\Sigma^{-l+1}\mathcal{P}\cdots*\Sigma^{-l+i}\mathcal{P}

and
\mathcal{P}=\mathrm{add}(eA)
. Hence we have a triangle

X^{\prime}\rightarrow\Sigma^{r_{1}}X\rightarrow S\rightarrow\Sigma X^{\prime},

where
X^{\prime}\in{}^{\perp}(\mathcal{S}_{<0})
and
S\in\mathcal{S}_{<0}\subseteq\mathcal{D}(A)^{\leqslant-1}
. We can see that
X^{\prime}
belongs to
\mathcal{D}(A)^{\leqslant 0}
.

Step 1: The object
X^{\prime}
is in
\mathcal{Z}={}^{\perp}(\Sigma^{>0}\mathcal{P})\cap(\Sigma^{<0}\mathcal{P})^{\perp}
.

Since
X^{\prime}\in{}^{\perp}(\mathcal{S}_{<0})
, it is enough to show that
X^{\prime}
is also in
(\Sigma^{<0}\mathcal{P})^{\perp}
. For any positive integer
k
, we have

\begin{split}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(A)}(eA,\Sigma^{k}X^{\prime})&\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(A)}(i^{*}(eAe),\Sigma^{k}X^{\prime})\\
&\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(eAe)}(eAe,\Sigma^{k}i_{*}(X^{\prime})).\end{split}

The space
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(A)}(eA,\Sigma^{k}X^{\prime})
vanishes for any positive integer
k
. Thus the object
X^{\prime}
is in
\mathcal{Z}
.

Step 2: There exists an object
W\in\mathcal{F}^{rel}
such that
W
is isomorphic to
X^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
.

By Step 1, the object
X^{\prime}
is in
\mathcal{Z}\subseteq\mathrm{per}A
. There is a non-negative integer
r_{2}
such that
p^{*}(X^{\prime})\in{}^{\perp}(\mathcal{D}(\overline{A})^{\leqslant-n-r_{2}})
. Consider the object
W^{\prime}=\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-r_{2}}X^{\prime}
. Then
W^{\prime}
is isomorphic to
X^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
.

By the definition of
\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-r_{2}}X^{\prime}
, we have the following triangle in
\mathrm{per}\overline{A}

p^{*}(\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-r_{2}}X^{\prime})\rightarrow p^{*}(X^{\prime})\rightarrow\tau_{>-r_{2}}(p^{*}(X^{\prime}))\rightarrow\Sigma p^{*}(\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-r_{2}}X^{\prime}).

Hence
p^{*}(\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-r_{2}}X^{\prime})
is isomorphic to
\tau_{\leqslant-r_{2}}(p^{*}(X^{\prime}))
. For any object
Y
in
\mathcal{D}(\overline{A})^{\leqslant-n-r_{2}}
, by the
(n+1)
-Calabi–Yau property of
\overline{A}
, the space
\mathrm{Hom}_{{\mathcal{D}}(\overline{A})}(\Sigma^{-1}\tau_{>-r_{2}}(p^{*}(X^{\prime})),Y)
is isomorphic to

D\mathrm{Hom}_{{\mathcal{D}}(\overline{A})}(Y,\Sigma^{n}\tau_{>-r_{2}}p^{*}(X^{\prime}))

and so vanishes. Therefore,
p^{*}(\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-r_{2}}X^{\prime})\cong\tau_{\leqslant-r_{2}}(p^{*}(X^{\prime}))
lies in
{}^{\perp}(\mathcal{D}(\overline{A})^{\leqslant-n-r_{2}})
. Thus, the object
p^{*}(\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-r_{2}}X^{\prime})
lies in
{}^{\perp}(\mathcal{D}(\overline{A})^{\leqslant-n-r_{2}})\cap{\mathcal{D}}(\overline{A})^{\leqslant-r_{2}}\cap\mathrm{per}(\overline{A})
which is equal to
\Sigma^{r_{2}}{\mathcal{F}}
. This shows that
W^{\prime}
belongs to
\mathcal{F}^{rel}\langle r_{2}\rangle
.

By Proposition
5.35
, there exists an object
W
in
\mathcal{F}^{rel}
such that
W
is isomorphic to
W^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
. Thus, we get the following triangle in
\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)

W\rightarrow\Sigma^{r_{1}}X\rightarrow S\rightarrow\Sigma W^{\prime\prime},

where
W
is in
\mathcal{H}
and
S
is in
\mathrm{per}(eAe)
.

∎

5.7.
Frobenius
m
-exangulated categories

In this subsection, we describe our results using the framework of
m
-exangulated categories. We refer to the readers to
[
50
]
,
[
20
]
,
[
21
]
and
[
46
]
for the relevant definitions and facts concerning
m
-exangulated categories.

Definition 5.44
.

[
46
, Deﬁnition 3.2]

Let
(\mathcal{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
be an
m
-exangulated category.

(1)

An object
P\in\mathcal{C}
is called
projective
if for any distinguished
m
-exangle

A_{0}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{0}}A_{1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow A_{m}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{m}}A_{m+1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\delta}}{{-\rightarrow}}

and any morphism
c
in
\mathcal{C}(P,A_{m+1})
, there exists a morphism
b\in\mathcal{C}(P,A_{m})
satisfying
\alpha_{m}b=c
. We denote the full subcategory of projective objects in
\mathcal{C}
by
\mathcal{P}
. Dually, the full subcategory of injective objects in
\mathcal{C}
is denoted by
\mathcal{I}
.

(2)

We say that
{\mathcal{C}}
has
enough projectives
if for any object
C\in\mathcal{C}
, there exists a distinguished
m
-exangle

B\xrightarrow{\alpha_{0}}P_{1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow P_{m}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{m}}C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\delta}}{{-\rightarrow}}

satisfying
P_{1},P_{2},\cdots,P_{m}\in\mathcal{P}
. We can define the notion of
having enough injectives
dually.

(3)

\mathcal{C}
is said to be
Frobenius
if
\mathcal{C}
has enough projectives and enough injectives and if moreover the projectives coincide with the injectives.

Remark 5.45
.

In the case
m=1
, these agree with the usual definitions of extriangulated categories (see
[
50
, Definition 3.23, Definition 3.25 and Definition 7.1]
).

Theorem 5.46
.

The Higgs category
\mathcal{H}
carries a canonical structure of Frobenius extriangulated category with projective-injective objects
\mathcal{P}=\mathrm{add}(eA)
. The functor
p^{*}\colon\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{n}(\overline{A})
induces an equivalence of triangulated categories

\mathcal{H}/[\mathcal{P}]\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{C}_{n}(\overline{A}).

Proof
.

Step 1:
\mathcal{H}
is an extriangulated category.

By Proposition
5.39
, the Higgs category
\mathcal{H}
is an extension closed subcategory of
\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
. Then by
[
50
, Remark 2.18]
,
\mathcal{H}
is an extriangulated category and
(\mathcal{H},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
can be described as follows:

(1)

For any two objects
X,Y\in\!\mathcal{H}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
, the
\mathbb{E}
-extension space
\mathbb{E}(X,Y)
is given by

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}(X,\Sigma Y).

(2)

For any
\delta\in\mathbb{E}(X,Y)=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}(Z,\Sigma X)
, take a distinguished triangle

X\xrightarrow{f}Y\xrightarrow{g}Z\xrightarrow{\delta}\Sigma X

and define
\mathfrak{s}(\delta)=[X\xrightarrow{f}Y\xrightarrow{g}Z]
. This
\mathfrak{s}(\delta)
does not depend on the choice of the distinguished triangle above.

Step 2:
\mathcal{H}
has has enough injectives and the full subcategory of injective objects in
\mathcal{H}
is
\mathcal{P}=\mathrm{add}(eA)
.

Let
I
be an object in
\mathrm{add}(eA)
. For any distinguished triangle in
\mathcal{H}

X\rightarrow Y\rightarrow Z\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\delta}}{{-\rightarrow}},

the space
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B}(\Sigma^{-1}Z,I)\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{per}A}(Z,\Sigma I)
vanishes since
Z\in\mathcal{Z}={}^{\perp}(\Sigma^{>0}\mathcal{P})\cap(\Sigma^{<0}\mathcal{P})^{\perp}\subseteq\mathrm{per}A
. Thus, we have the following exact sequence

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}(Y,I)\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}(X,I)\rightarrow 0.

Thus, any object in
\mathrm{add}(eA)
is injective.

Now let
X
be an object in
\mathcal{H}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
. Then
X
is an object in
\mathcal{Z}\subseteq\mathrm{per}A
. We take a triangle in
\mathrm{per}A

X\xrightarrow{l_{X}}P_{X}\rightarrow X_{1}\rightarrow\Sigma X

with a left
\mathcal{P}=\mathrm{add}(eA)
-approximation
l_{X}
and
X_{1}\in\mathcal{Z}
. It is easy to see that
X_{1}
is in
\mathcal{F}^{rel}\langle 1\rangle
. By Proposition
5.35
, there is an object
X_{2}\in\mathcal{F}^{rel}
such that
\tau^{rel}_{\leqslant-1}X_{2}\cong X_{1}
. Thus, we have a triangle in
\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)

X\xrightarrow{l_{X}}P_{X}\rightarrow X_{2}\rightarrow\Sigma X

with
P_{X}
in
\mathrm{add}(eA)
and
l_{X}
an inflation. Therefore,
\mathcal{H}
has has enough injectives.

It remains to show that any injective object is in
\mathrm{add}(eA)
. Let
J
be an injective object in
\mathcal{H}
. We take a triangle in
\mathrm{per}A

J\xrightarrow{l_{J}}P_{J}\rightarrow J_{1}\rightarrow\Sigma J

with a left
\mathcal{P}=\mathrm{add}(eA)
-approximation
l_{J}
and
J_{1}\in\mathcal{Z}
. Since
J
is injective, the morphism
l_{J}\colon J\rightarrow P_{J}
is split in
\mathcal{H}\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{rel}_{n}(A,B)
. Thus
l_{J}
is also split in
\mathcal{F}^{rel}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}\subseteq\mathrm{per}A
.

Therefore,
J
belongs to
\mathrm{add}(eA)
and the subcategory of injective objects in
\mathcal{H}
is
\mathcal{P}=\mathrm{add}(eA)
.

Step 3:
\mathcal{H}
has has enough projectives and the full subcategory of projective objects in
\mathcal{H}
is
\mathcal{P}=\mathrm{add}(eA)
.

This follows from the dual of the argument in Step 2.

Step 4:
\mathcal{H}
is a Frobenius extriangulated category.

By Steps 1, 2, and 3, the Higgs category
\mathcal{H}
is a Frobenius extriangulated category with projective-injective objects
\mathcal{P}=\mathrm{add}(eA)
. By Corollary
5.19
, we have the equivalence between triangulated categories

\mathcal{H}/[\mathcal{P}]\cong\mathcal{F}\cong\mathcal{C}_{n}(\overline{A}).

∎

5.8.
Higher extensions in an extriangulated category

Let
(\mathcal{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
be an extriangulated category. Assume that it has enough projectives and injectives, and let
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
(respectively,
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
) denote the full subcategory of projectives (resp. injectives). We denote the ideal quotients
\mathcal{C}/[\mathcal{P}]
and
\mathcal{C}/[\mathcal{I}]
by
\underline{\mathcal{C}}
and
\overline{\mathcal{C}}
, respectively. The extension group bifunctor
\mathbb{E}\colon\mathcal{C}^{op}\times\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathrm{Ab}
induces
\mathbb{E}\colon\underline{\mathcal{C}^{op}}\times\overline{\mathcal{C}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Ab}
, which we denote by the same symbol. To define the higher extension groups, we need the following assumptions

Assumptions 3
.

Each object
A\in\mathcal{C}
is assigned the following data (i) and (ii).

(i)

A pair
(\Sigma A,l^{A})
of an object
\Sigma A\in\mathcal{C}
and an extension
l^{A}\in\mathbb{E}(\Sigma A,A)
, for
which
\mathfrak{s}(l^{A})=[A\rightarrow I\rightarrow\Sigma A]
satisfies
I\in\mathcal{I}
.

(ii)

A pair
(\Omega A,\omega^{A})
of an object
\Omega A\in\mathcal{C}
and an extension
\omega^{A}\in\mathbb{E}(A,\Omega A)
, for
which
\mathfrak{s}(\omega^{A})=[\Omega A\rightarrow P\rightarrow A]
satisfies
P\in\mathcal{P}
.

Definition 5.47
.

[
21
, Definition 3.6]

Let
i\geqslant 1
be any integer. Define a biadditive functor
\mathbb{E}^{i}\colon\mathcal{C}^{op}\times\mathcal{C}\rightarrow Ab
to be the composition of

\mathcal{C}^{op}\times\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\underline{\mathcal{C}^{op}}\times\overline{\mathcal{C}}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Id}\times\Sigma^{i-1}}\underline{\mathcal{C}^{op}}\times\overline{\mathcal{C}}\xrightarrow{\mathbb{E}}\mathrm{Ab},

where
\Sigma^{i-1}
is the
(i-1)
-times iteration of the endfunctor
\Sigma
.

By Theorem
5.46
, the Higgs category
\mathcal{H}
is a Frobenius extriangulated category (or Frobenius 1-exangulated category) with projective-injective objects
\mathcal{P}=\mathrm{add}eA
. Thus the higher extension can be computed as follows:

Let
X
and
Y
be two objects in
\mathcal{H}
. Let
l>0
be an integer. We have

\mathbb{E}^{l}(X,Y)=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{Z}/[\mathcal{P}]}(X,Y\langle l\rangle)\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(\overline{A})}(p^{*}(A),\Sigma^{l}p^{*}(Y))\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}(X,\Sigma^{l}Y).

Definition 5.48
.

[
21
, Definition 3.21]

Let
(\mathcal{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
be a Frobenius extriangulated category. Let
\mathcal{T}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
be a full additive subcategory closed under isomorphisms and direct summands. Then
\mathcal{T}
is called an
n-cluster tilting subcategory
of
\mathcal{C}
, if it satisfies the following conditions.

(1)

\mathcal{T}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
is functorially finite.

(2)

For any
C\in\mathcal{C}
, the following are equivalent.

(i)
C\in\mathcal{T}
,

(ii)
\mathbb{E}^{i}(C,\mathcal{T})=0
for any
1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1
,

(iii)
\mathbb{E}^{i}(\mathcal{T},C)=0
for any
1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1
.

Proposition 5.49
.

The category
\mathrm{add}(\pi^{rel}A)
is an
n
-cluster-tilting subcategory of
\mathcal{H}
.

Proof
.
Since
{\mathcal{H}}
is Hom-finite, it is clear that
\mathrm{add}(\pi^{rel}A)
is functorially finite in
\mathcal{H}
.

Step 1:
\pi^{rel}(A)
is an
n
-rigid object in
\mathcal{H}
.

By Proposition
5.36
,
[
3
, Theorem 7.1]
and
[
18
, Theorem 3.2.2]
, we have that

\begin{split}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}(\pi^{rel}(A),\Sigma^{i}\pi^{rel}(A))&\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(\overline{A})}(\overline{A},\Sigma^{i}\overline{A})\\
&\simeq 0\end{split}

for any
1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1
.

Step 2: Let
X
be an object in
\mathcal{H}
satisfying
\mathbb{E}^{i}(X,\mathrm{add}A)=0
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1
. Then
X
is in
\mathrm{add}A
.

Since
\mathbb{E}^{i}(X,\mathrm{add}A)=0
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1
, we have
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(\overline{A})}(p^{*}(X),\mathrm{add}(\overline{A}))=0
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1
. We know that
\mathrm{add}(\overline{A})
is an
n
-cluster tilting subcategory of
\mathcal{F}\cong\mathcal{C}_{n}(\overline{A})
(see
[
3
,
18
]
). Hence
p^{*}(X)
is in
\mathrm{add}(\overline{A})
. By the equivalence
p^{*}\colon\mathcal{Z}/[\mathcal{P}]\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathcal{F}\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathcal{C}_{n}(\overline{A})
, the object
X
is in
\mathrm{add}A
.

Step 3: Let
X
be an object in
\mathcal{H}
satisfying
\mathbb{E}^{i}(\mathrm{add}A,X)=0
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1
. Then
X
is in
\mathrm{add}A
.

This follows by a similar argument to that in Step 2.

Thus, the category
\mathrm{add}A
is an
n
-cluster tilting subcategory of
\mathcal{H}
.
∎

Proposition 5.50
.

Suppose that the
n
-cluster tilting category
\mathrm{add}\overline{A}
of
{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\overline{A})
satisfies
\Sigma^{n}(\mathrm{add}\overline{A})=\mathrm{add}\overline{A}
. Then we have:

(1)

If
X\in\mathcal{H}
satisfies
\mathbb{E}^{n-1}(\mathrm{add}A,X)=0
, then there is
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle

Y\xrightarrow{f}P\rightarrow X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{-\rightarrow}}\quad(P\in\mathcal{P}=\mathrm{add}(eA))

for which

\mathcal{H}(T,f)\colon\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{H}}(T,Y)\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{H}}(T,P)

is injective for any
T\in\mathrm{add}(A)
.

(2)

Dually, if
Z\in\mathcal{H}
satisfies
\mathbb{E}^{n-1}(Z,\mathrm{add}A)=0
, then there is
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle

Z\rightarrow I\xrightarrow{g}W\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{-\rightarrow}}\quad(I\in\mathcal{P}=\mathrm{add}(eA))

for which

\mathcal{H}(g,T)\colon\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{H}}(W,T)\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{H}}(I,T)

is injective for any
T\in\mathrm{add}(A)
.

Proof
.
We only show the first statement since the second statement can be shown dually. Let
X
be an object in
\mathcal{H}
which satisfies

\mathbb{E}^{n-1}(\mathrm{add}A,X)=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)}(\mathrm{add}(A),\Sigma^{n-1}X)\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}(\overline{A})}(\mathrm{add}(\overline{A}),\Sigma^{n-1}p^{*}X)=0.

Since
{\mathcal{H}}
is a Frobenius extriangulated category, there is an
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle

Y\xrightarrow{f}P\rightarrow X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{-\rightarrow}}\quad(P\in\mathcal{P}=\mathrm{add}(eA))

with
Y
in
{\mathcal{H}}
and
P
in
{\mathcal{P}}=\mathrm{add}(eA)
. Then it is enough to show that
\mathrm{Hom}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(A,B)}(\Sigma\mathrm{add}(A),X)=0
.

By Proposition
5.36
, we have

\begin{split}\mathrm{Hom}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(A,B)}(\Sigma\mathrm{add}(A),X)\simeq&\mathrm{Hom}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\overline{A})}(\Sigma\mathrm{add}(\overline{A}),p^{*}X)\\
\simeq&\mathrm{Hom}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\overline{A})}(\Sigma^{n}\mathrm{add}(\overline{A}),\Sigma^{n-1}p^{*}X)\\
\simeq&\mathrm{Hom}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\overline{A})}(\mathrm{add}(\overline{A}),\Sigma^{n-1}p^{*}X)\\
=&0.\end{split}

Thus, the space
\mathrm{Hom}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(A,B)}(\Sigma\mathrm{add}(A),X)
vanishes.

∎

Proposition 5.51
.

By Theorem
5.46
,
(\mathcal{H},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
is an extriangulated category. Let
f\in\mathcal{H}(X,Y)
,
g\in\mathcal{H}(Y,Z)
be any pair of morphisms. We have

(1)

If
g\circ f
is an
\mathfrak{s}
-inflation (
[
20
, Definition 2.23]
), then so is
f
.

(2)

If
g\circ f
is an
\mathfrak{s}
-deflation (
[
20
, Definition 2.23]
), then so is
g
.

Proof
.
We only show the first statement since the second statement can be shown dually. Suppose that
g\circ f
is an
\mathfrak{s}
-inflation, i.e. there is a triangle in
\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)

X\xrightarrow{g\circ f}Z\rightarrow W\rightarrow\Sigma X

such that
W
is also in
\mathcal{H}
. By the octahedral axiom, we have the following commutative diagram in
\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{U\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{gf}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma X}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma U}

and the upper middle commutative diagram is a homotopy bi-cartesian square. Thus, there is a triangle in
\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)

Y\rightarrow U\oplus Z\rightarrow W\rightarrow\Sigma Y.

Since
\mathcal{H}
is an extension closed subcategory of
\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
, the sum
U\oplus Z
is in
\mathcal{H}
. By Theorem
5.42
,
\mathcal{H}
is closed under taking direct summands. Thus
U
is in
\mathcal{H}
. We conclude that
f\colon X\rightarrow Y
is an
\mathfrak{s}
-inflation.

∎

Remark 5.52
.

The Proposition above shows that
{\mathcal{H}}
satisfies Nakaoka–Palu’s WIC condition (see
[
50
, Condition 5.8]
), which is equivalent to
{\mathcal{H}}
being weakly idempotent complete in the usual sense (see
[
44
, Proposition 2.7]
).

Theorem 5.53
.

Suppose that the
n
-cluster tilting category
\mathrm{add}\overline{A}
of
{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\overline{A})
satisfies

\Sigma^{n}\mathrm{add}\overline{A}=\mathrm{add}\overline{A}.

Then the
n
-cluster-tilting subcategory
\mathrm{add}\overline{A}
of
{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\overline{A})
carries a canonical
(n+2)
-angulated structure. Moreover, the
n
-cluster-tilting subcategory
\mathrm{add}A
of
{\mathcal{H}}
carries a canonical structure of Frobenius
n
-exangulated category with projective-injective objects
\mathcal{P}=\mathrm{add}(eA)
. The quotient functor
p^{*}\colon\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{n}(\overline{A})
induces an equivalence of
(n+2)
-angulated categories

\mathrm{add}A/[\mathcal{P}]\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathrm{add}(\overline{A}).

Proof
.

1. The canonical
(n+2)
-angulated structure on
\mathrm{add}\overline{A}
.

Since
\mathrm{add}\overline{A}
is closed under the
n
-th power of the shift functor in
\mathcal{C}_{n}(\overline{A})
, by
[
16
, Theorem 1]
, the
n
-cluster-tilting subcategory
\mathrm{add}\overline{A}
carries a canonical
(n+2)
-angulated structure
(\mathrm{add}\overline{A},\Sigma^{n},\pentagon)
, where
\pentagon
is the class of all
(n+2)
-sequences in
\mathrm{add}\overline{A}

M\xrightarrow{\alpha_{0}}X_{1}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{1}}X_{2}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{2}}\cdots\xrightarrow{\alpha_{n-1}}X_{n}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{n}}N\xrightarrow{\delta}\Sigma^{n}M

such that there exists a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1.5}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{2.5}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{n-0.5}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\qquad\qquad\qquad|}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\qquad\qquad\qquad|}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\qquad\qquad\qquad|}

with
X_{i}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\overline{A})
for
i\notin\mathbb{Z}
, such that all oriented triangles are triangles in
{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\overline{A})
, all non-oriented triangles commute, and
\delta
is the composition along the lower edge of the diagram.

For any two objects
M,N
in
\mathrm{add}A
, the category
\mathbf{T}^{n+2}_{M,N}
(see
[
20
, Definition 2.17]
) is defined as follows
\colon

(a)

An object in
\mathbf{T}^{n+2}_{M,N}
is a complex
X^{\bullet}=({X^{i},d_{X}^{i}})
of the form

X^{0}\xrightarrow{d_{X}^{0}}X^{1}\xrightarrow{d_{X}^{1}}\cdots\xrightarrow{d_{X}^{n-1}}X^{n}\xrightarrow{d_{X}^{n}}X^{n+1}

with all
X^{i}
in
\mathrm{add}A
and
X^{0}=M
,
X^{n+1}=N
.

(b)

For any
X^{\bullet},Y^{\bullet}\in\mathbf{T}^{n+2}_{M,N}
, a morphism
f
between
X^{\bullet}
and
Y^{\bullet}
is a chain map
f=(f^{0},\cdots,f^{n+1})
such that
f^{0}=\boldmath{1}_{M}
and
f^{n+1}=\boldmath{1}_{N}
. Two morphisms
f^{\bullet}
and
g^{\bullet}\in\mathbf{T}^{n+2}_{M,N}(X^{\bullet},Y^{\bullet})
are
homotopic
if there is a sequence of morphisms
h^{\bullet}=(h^{1},\cdots,h^{n})
satisfying

\begin{split}0=&h^{1}\circ d_{X}^{0},\\
g^{i}-f^{i}=&d_{Y}^{i-1}\circ h^{i}+h^{i+1}\circ d_{X}^{i}\quad(1\leqslant i\leqslant n),\\
0=&d_{Y}^{n}\circ h^{n+1}.\end{split}

2. The canonical Frobenius
n
-exangulated structure on
\mathrm{add}A
.

By Propositions
5.50
and
5.51
, the
n
-cluster-tilting subcategory
\mathrm{add}A\subseteq{\mathcal{H}}
satisfies the conditions in
[
21
, Theorem 3.41]
. Thus, it carries a canonical
n
-exangulated structure
(\mathrm{add}A,\mathbb{E}^{n},\mathfrak{s}^{n})
which is given by

(1)

For any two objects
M,N
in
\mathrm{add}A
, the group
\mathbb{E}^{n}(M,N)
is the higher extension group defined in Definition
5.47
, i.e.
\mathbb{E}^{n}(N,M)=\mathrm{Hom}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(A,B)}(N,\Sigma^{n}M)\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\overline{A})}(p^{*}(N),\Sigma^{n}p^{*}(M))
;

(2)

For any
M,N
in
\mathrm{add}A
and any
\delta\in\mathbb{E}^{n}(N,M)
, define

\mathfrak{s}^{n}(\delta)=[X^{\bullet}]

to be the homotopy equivalence class of
X^{\bullet}
in
\mathbf{T}^{n+2}_{M,N}
, where
X^{\bullet}
is given by an
(n+2)
-sequence in
\mathrm{add}A

M\xrightarrow{\alpha_{0}}X_{1}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{1}}X_{2}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{2}}\cdots\xrightarrow{\alpha_{n-1}}X_{n}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{n}}N\xrightarrow{\delta}\Sigma^{n}M

such that there exists a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1.5}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{2.5}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{n-0.5}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\qquad\qquad\qquad|}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\qquad\qquad\qquad|}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\qquad\qquad\qquad|}

with
X_{i}\in{\mathcal{H}}
for
i\notin\mathbb{Z}
, such that all oriented triangles are triangles in
{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(A,B)
, all non-oriented triangles commute, and
\delta
is the composition along the lower edge of the diagram.

Next, we will show that
\mathrm{add}A
carries a canonical structure of Frobenius
n
-exangulated category with projective-injective objects
\mathcal{P}=\mathrm{add}(eA)
.

Firstly, we show that
{\mathcal{P}}=\mathrm{add}(eA)
consists of projective-injective objects in
\mathrm{add}A
. Let
P
be an object in
\mathrm{add}(eA)
. We take a distinguished
n
-exangle in
\mathrm{add}A

Y_{0}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{0}}Y_{1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow Y_{n}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{n}}Y_{n+1}\xrightarrow{\delta}\Sigma^{n}Y_{0}.

Then we have a distinguished triangle in
{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(A,B)

X\rightarrow Y_{n}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{n}}Y_{n+1}\rightarrow\Sigma X

such that
X
is in
{\mathcal{H}}
. Let
c\colon P\rightarrow Y_{n+1}
be a morphism in
\mathrm{add}A
. It induces the following long exact sequence

\cdots\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(A,B)}(P,Y_{n})\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(A,B)}(P,Y_{n+1})\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(A,B)}(P,\Sigma X)\rightarrow\cdots.

Since
X
is in
{\mathcal{H}}
and
{\mathcal{P}}
is projective in
{\mathcal{H}}
, the space
\mathrm{Hom}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(A,B)}(P,\Sigma X)
vanishes. Thus, there exists a morphism
b\colon P\rightarrow Y_{n}
in
\mathrm{add}A
satisfying
\alpha_{n}\circ b=c
. This shows that
P
is projective. Dually, we can show that
P
is injective.

Let
N
be an object in
\mathrm{add}A
. Since
\mathrm{add}(eA)
is functorially finite in
{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(A,B)
, there exists a distinguished triangle in
{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(A,B)

Q_{n}\xrightarrow{a_{n}}P_{n}\xrightarrow{b_{n}}N\xrightarrow{c_{n}}\Sigma Q_{n}

with
P_{n}
in
\mathrm{add}(eA)
. We see that
p^{*}(N)\simeq\Sigma p^{*}(Q_{n})\in\mathrm{add}(\overline{A})
in
{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\overline{A})
.

For the object
Q_{n}
, we also have a distinguished triangle in
{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(A,B)

Q_{n-1}\xrightarrow{a_{n-1}}P_{n-1}\xrightarrow{b_{n-1}}Q_{n}\xrightarrow{c_{n-1}}\Sigma Q_{n-1}

with
P_{n-1}
in
\mathrm{add}(eA)
. We see that
p^{*}(N)\simeq\Sigma^{2}p^{*}(Q_{n-1})\in\mathrm{add}(\overline{A})
in
{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\overline{A})
.

Repeating the process, we get the following triangles in
{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(A,B)

Q_{n}\xrightarrow{a_{n}}P_{n}\xrightarrow{b_{n}}N\xrightarrow{c_{n}}\Sigma Q_{n},

Q_{n-1}\xrightarrow{a_{n-1}}P_{n-1}\xrightarrow{b_{n-1}}Q_{n}\xrightarrow{c_{n-1}}\Sigma Q_{n-1},

\cdots

Q_{0}\xrightarrow{a_{0}}P_{0}\xrightarrow{b_{0}}Q_{1}\xrightarrow{c_{0}}\Sigma Q_{0}

such that all
P_{i}\,,\,0\leqslant i\leqslant n
, are in
\mathrm{add}(eA)
and
p^{*}(N)\simeq\Sigma^{n}p^{*}(Q_{0})\in\mathrm{add}(\overline{A})
.

By our assumption
\Sigma^{n}\mathrm{add}\overline{A}=\mathrm{add}\overline{A}
, we see that
p^{*}(Q_{0})
is in
\mathrm{add}\overline{A}
. Thus, the object
Q_{0}
is in
\mathrm{add}A
. Then we get a distinguished
n
-exangle in
\mathrm{add}A

Q_{0}\xrightarrow{a_{0}}P_{0}\xrightarrow{a_{1}\circ b_{0}}P_{1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow P_{n}\xrightarrow{b_{n}}N\xrightarrow{\delta}\Sigma^{n}Q_{0},

where
\delta
is the composition

N\xrightarrow{c_{n}}\Sigma Q_{n}\xrightarrow{\Sigma c_{n-1}}\Sigma^{2}Q_{n-1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow Q_{1}\xrightarrow{\Sigma^{n-1}c_{0}}\Sigma^{n}Q_{0}.

Thus, this shows that
\mathrm{add}A
has enough projectives. Dually, we can show that
\mathrm{add}A
has enough injectives. Moreover, projective-injective objects form exactly the subcategory
{\mathcal{P}}=\mathrm{add}(eA)
. Therefore, we have shown that
\mathrm{add}A
carries a canonical structure of Frobenius
n
-exangulated category with projective-injective objects
\mathcal{P}=\mathrm{add}(eA)
.

3. The canonical
(n+2)
-angulated structure on
\mathrm{add}A/[{\mathcal{P}}]
.

The stable category
\mathrm{add}A/[{\mathcal{P}}]
has the same objects as
\mathrm{add}A
. For any two objects
M
and
N
, the morphism space is given by the quotient group

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{add}A}(M,N)/[{\mathcal{P}}](M,N),

where
[{\mathcal{P}}](M,N)
is the subgroup of
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{add}A}(M,N)
consisting of those morphisms which factor through an object in
{\mathcal{P}}=\mathrm{add}eA
.

For any object
M
in
\mathrm{add}A
, we have the following triangles in
{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(A,B)

M\xrightarrow{a_{0}}I_{0}\xrightarrow{b_{0}}Q_{0}\xrightarrow{c_{0}}\Sigma M,

Q_{0}\xrightarrow{a_{1}}I_{1}\xrightarrow{b_{1}}Q_{1}\xrightarrow{c_{1}}\Sigma Q_{0},

\cdots

Q_{n-1}\xrightarrow{a_{n}}I_{n}\xrightarrow{b_{n}}Q_{n}\xrightarrow{c_{n}}\Sigma Q_{n-1}

such that all
I_{i}
,
0\leqslant i\leqslant n
, are in
\mathrm{add}(eA)
and
Q_{n}
is in
\mathrm{add}A
. Those triangles induce a distinguished
n
-exangle in
\mathrm{add}A

M\xrightarrow{a_{0}}I_{0}\xrightarrow{a_{1}\circ b_{0}}I_{1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow I_{n}\xrightarrow{c_{n}}Q_{n}\xrightarrow{\delta}\Sigma^{n}M,

where
\delta
is the composition

Q_{n}\xrightarrow{c_{n}}\Sigma Q_{n-1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\Sigma^{n-1}Q_{0}\xrightarrow{\Sigma^{n-1}c_{0}}\Sigma^{n}M.

We define the functor
S\colon\mathrm{add}A/[{\mathcal{P}}]\rightarrow\mathrm{add}A/[{\mathcal{P}}]
such that it takes
M
to
Q_{n}
. By
[
46
, Proposition 3.7]
, the
S
functor is well defined and it is an auto-equivalence. It is easy to see that
S(M)
is isomorphic to
\Sigma^{n}p^{*}(M)
in
{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(\overline{A})
.

Thus, by
[
46
, Theorem 3.13]
, the stable category
\mathrm{add}A/[{\mathcal{P}}]
carries a canonical
(n+2)
-angulated structure
(\mathrm{add}A/[{\mathcal{P}}],S,\square_{S})
which is given by

(1)

The functor
S
defined as above.

(2)

For any two objects
M,N
in
\mathrm{add}A
, there is a one-to-one correspondence between
\mathbb{E}^{n}(N,M)=\mathrm{Hom}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(A,B)}(N,\Sigma^{n}M)
and
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{add}/[{\mathcal{P}}]}(N,S(M))\simeq\mathbb{E}^{n}(N,M)
(see
[
46
, Lemma 3.12]
). Any distinguished
n
-exangle

M\xrightarrow{\alpha_{0}}X_{1}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{1}}X_{2}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{2}}\cdots\xrightarrow{\alpha_{n-1}}X_{n}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{n}}N\xrightarrow{\delta}\Sigma^{n}M

in
\mathrm{add}A
induces an
(n+2)
-sequence

M\xrightarrow{\overline{\alpha}_{0}}X_{1}\xrightarrow{\overline{\alpha}_{1}}X_{2}\xrightarrow{\alpha_{2}}\cdots\xrightarrow{\overline{\alpha}_{n-1}}X_{n}\xrightarrow{\overline{\alpha}_{n}}N\xrightarrow{\overline{\delta}}S(M)

in
\mathrm{add}A/[{\mathcal{P}}]
. We call such sequence an
(n+2)
-
S
-sequence
. We denote by
\square_{S}
the class of
(n+2)
-
S
-sequences.

For any object
M
of
\mathrm{add}A/[\mathcal{P}]
, we have
S(M)\cong p^{*}(\Sigma^{n}M)\cong\Sigma^{n}(p^{*}(M))
. Moreover, the
k
-equivalence
p^{*}\colon\mathrm{add}A/[\mathcal{P}]\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathrm{add}(\overline{A})
maps
\square_{S}
to
\pentagon
.
Thus
p^{*}
induces an equivalence of
(n+2)
-angulated categories

(\mathrm{add}A/[\mathcal{P}],S,\square_{S})\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}(\mathrm{add}(\overline{A}),\Sigma^{n},\pentagon).

∎

Remark 5.54
.

If
\mathrm{add}A
is stable under
\Sigma^{n}
in
{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(A,B)
, then the algebra
B
is zero. So the
n
-cluster-tilting subcategory
\mathrm{add}A\subseteq{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(A,B)
can only carry an
n
-angulated structure with higher suspension
\Sigma^{n}
if
B=0
(see
[
16
, Theorem 1]
).

6.
The case when
A
is concentrated in degree 0

Let
f\colon B\to A
be a morphism (not necessarily preserving the identity element) between two differential graded (=dg)
k
-algebras. We assume that
f
satisfies the assumptions
1
and moreover,
A
is concentrated in degree 0. In particular,
f
carries a relative
(n+1)
-Calabi–Yau structure.

Proposition 6.1
.

Under the assumption above, the
k
-algebra
H^{0}(A)
is a finite-dimensional algebra with
\mathrm{gldim}\,H^{0}(A)\leqslant n+1
.

Proof
.
By assumptions
1
, the algebra
H^{0}(A)
is finite-dimensional. Suppose that
\boldmath{1}_{H^{0}(A)}
has decomposition

\boldmath{1}_{H^{0}(A)}=e_{1}+e_{2}+\cdots+e_{n}

into primitive orthogonal idempotents such that

e=f(\boldmath{1}_{B})=e_{1}+\cdots+e_{k}

for an integer
0\leqslant k\leqslant n
. Here we regard
e
as an element of
H^{0}(A)
. By Proposition
3.12
,
\mathrm{pvd}_{B}(A)
is an
(n+1)
-Calabi–Yau triangulated category. Thus for each simple module
S_{i}
,
k+1\leqslant i\leqslant n
, we have
\mathrm{pdim}\,S_{i}\leqslant n+1
.

Let
M
be a finite-dimensional
H^{0}(A)
-module. For each simple module
S_{i}
,
1\leqslant i\leqslant k
, by Proposition
3.12
, we have the following isomorphism of triangles

\textstyle{\mathcal{C}(S_{i},\Sigma^{-1}M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{A}(S_{i},M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{B}(S_{i}|_{B},M|_{B})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{D\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{A}(M,\Sigma^{n+1}S_{i})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{D\mathcal{C}(M,\Sigma^{n}S_{i})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{D\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{B}(M|_{B},\Sigma^{n}S_{i}|_{B})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
.

Recall that
\mathcal{C}(S_{i},M)
is defined as
\mathrm{Cone}(\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{A}(S_{i},M)\rightarrow\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{B}(S_{i}|_{B},M|_{B}))
. Hence we have
\mathcal{C}(S_{i},\Sigma^{k}M)=\Sigma^{k}\mathcal{C}(S_{i},M)
for any
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.

For each integer
p\geqslant n+2
, we have

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(B)}(S_{i}|_{B},\Sigma^{p}(M|_{B}))=0

because
B
is
n
-Calabi–Yau.

Thus, we have

\textstyle{H^{0}(\mathcal{C}(S_{i},\Sigma^{p-1}M))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Ext}_{H^{0}(A)}^{p}(S_{i},M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0=D\mathrm{Ext}_{H^{0}(A)}^{n+1-p}(M,S_{i})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{DH^{0}(\mathcal{C}(M,\Sigma^{n-1-p}S_{i}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

We see that the space
\mathrm{Ext}_{H^{0}(A)}^{p}(S_{i},M)
vanishes for each
p\geqslant n+2
.
Then
\mathrm{pdim}\,S_{i}\leqslant n+1
for each
1\leqslant i\leqslant k
. Therefore, we have
\mathrm{gldim}\,H^{0}(A)\leqslant n+1
.
∎

Let
{\mathcal{E}}
be a Frobenius category and
{\mathcal{M}}
a full subcategory of
{\mathcal{E}}
which contains the full subcategory
{\mathcal{P}}
of
{\mathcal{E}}
formed by the projective-injective objects. We denote by
{\mathcal{K}}^{b}({\mathcal{E}})
and
{\mathcal{D}}^{b}({\mathcal{E}})
respectively the bounded homotopy category and the bounded derived category of
{\mathcal{E}}
.

We say that a complex
X\colon\cdots\rightarrow X^{i-1}\rightarrow X^{i}\rightarrow X^{i+1}\rightarrow\cdots
in
{\mathcal{K}}^{b}({\mathcal{E}})
is
{\mathcal{E}}
-acyclic
if there are conflations
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{i+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l^{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{i}}
such that
d_{X}^{i}=l^{i+1}\circ\pi^{i}
for each
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.

We also denote by
{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathcal{E}}-ac}^{b}({\mathcal{E}})
,
{\mathcal{K}}^{b}({\mathcal{P}})
,
{\mathcal{K}}^{b}({\mathcal{M}})
and
{\mathcal{K}}^{b}_{{\mathcal{E}}-ac}({\mathcal{M}})
the full subcategory of
{\mathcal{K}}^{b}({\mathcal{E}})
whose objects are the
{\mathcal{E}}
-acyclic complexes, the complexes of projective objects in
{\mathcal{E}}
, the complexes of objects of
{\mathcal{M}}
and the
{\mathcal{E}}
-acyclic complexes of objects of
{\mathcal{M}}
respectively.

Theorem 6.2
.

Let
f\colon B\rightarrow A
be a dg algebra morphism which satisfies the assumptions
1
. Let
e=f(\boldmath{1}_{B})
. Moreover, we assume that
A
is concentrated in degree
0
. Then we have

a)

The algebra
B^{\prime}=eH^{0}(A)e
is Iwanaga-Gorenstein of injective dimension at most
n+1
as a
B^{\prime}
-module.

b)

Under the equivalence
\mathcal{D}^{b}(\mathrm{mod}H^{0}A)\simeq\mathrm{per}A
, the subcategory
\mathcal{F}^{rel}
corresponds to the subcategory
\mathrm{mod}_{n-1}(H^{0}A)
of
H^{0}A
-modules of projective dimension at most
n-1
.

c)

Via the equivalence
\mathrm{res}\colon\mathcal{D}^{b}(\mathrm{mod}H^{0}A)\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathrm{per}A
, the localization
\pi^{rel}\colon\mathrm{per}A\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
identifies with the restriction functor
\mathcal{D}^{b}(\mathrm{mod}H^{0}A)\rightarrow\mathcal{D}^{b}(\mathrm{mod}B^{\prime})
, i.e. we have a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}^{b}(\mathrm{mod}H^{0}A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}^{b}(\mathrm{mod}B^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{per}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B).}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle\sim

d)

Under the equivalence
\mathcal{D}^{b}(\mathrm{mod}B^{\prime})\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
, the Higgs category
\mathcal{H}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)
corresponds to the subcategory
\mathrm{gpr}B^{\prime}
of Gorenstein projective modules over
B^{\prime}=eH^{0}(A)e
. In particular, when
B^{\prime}
is self injective, we have
\mathcal{H}\cong\mathrm{mod}B^{\prime}
.

e)

Let
{\mathcal{M}}=\mathrm{add}A\subset{\mathcal{H}}
. Then the exact sequence of triangulated categories

0\rightarrow\mathrm{pvd}_{B}(A)\rightarrow\mathrm{per}A\rightarrow{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(A,B)\rightarrow 0

is equivalent to

0\rightarrow{\mathcal{K}}^{b}_{{\mathcal{H}}-ac}({\mathcal{M}})\rightarrow{\mathcal{K}}^{b}({\mathcal{M}})\rightarrow{\mathcal{D}}^{b}({\mathcal{H}})\rightarrow 0.

In particular, the relative cluster category
{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(A,B)
is equivalent to the bounded derived category
{\mathcal{D}}^{b}({\mathcal{H}})
of
{\mathcal{H}}
.

Proof
.
Since
H^{0}(A)
is of finite global dimension, the restriction along the quasi isomorphism

A\rightarrow H^{0}(A)

induces a triangle equivalence

\mathcal{D}^{b}(\mathrm{mod}H^{0}(A))\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathrm{per}A.

Under this equivalence,
\mathrm{pvd}_{B}(A)
identifies with

\begin{split}\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{N}}^{b}(\mathrm{mod}H^{0}(A))=&\{X\in\mathcal{D}^{b}(\mathrm{mod}H^{0}(A))|\ H^{l}(X)|_{B^{\prime}}=0,\forall l\in\mathbb{Z}\}\\
=&\{X\in\mathcal{D}^{b}(\mathrm{mod}H^{0}(A))|H^{l}(A)\in\mathcal{N},\forall l\in\mathbb{Z}\},\end{split}

where
\mathcal{N}=\{M\in\mathrm{mod}H^{0}(A)\ |\ M|_{B^{\prime}}=0\}
. Clearly, the category
\mathcal{N}
is a Serre subcategory of
\mathrm{mod}H^{0}(A)
and the restriction
\mathrm{mod}H^{0}(A)\rightarrow\mathrm{mod}B^{\prime}
induces an exact sequence of abelian categories

0\rightarrow\mathcal{N}\rightarrow\mathrm{mod}H^{0}(A)\rightarrow\mathrm{mod}B^{\prime}\rightarrow 0.

This exact sequence induces an exact sequence of triangulated categories

0\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{N}}^{b}(\mathrm{mod}H^{0}A)\rightarrow\mathcal{D}^{b}(\mathrm{mod}H^{0}(A))\rightarrow\mathcal{D}^{b}(\mathrm{mod}B^{\prime})\rightarrow 0.

Thus, the restriction
\mathrm{per}A\cong\mathcal{D}^{b}(\mathrm{mod}H^{0}(A))\rightarrow\mathcal{D}^{b}(\mathrm{mod}B^{\prime})
induces an equivalence

\mathcal{C}_{n}(A,B)\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathcal{D}^{b}(\mathrm{mod}B^{\prime}).

By inspecting the definition of
\mathcal{F}^{rel}
, it is equivalent to the following subcategory

\mathrm{mod}_{n-1}(H^{0}A)=\{M\in\mathrm{mod}H^{0}(A)\ |\ \mathrm{pdim}M\leqslant n-1\}.

The Higgs category
{\mathcal{H}}
is contained in
\mathrm{mod}B^{\prime}
and stable under extensions in
{\mathcal{C}}_{n}(A,B)\simeq{\mathcal{D}}(B^{\prime})
. Thus, it is a fully exact subcategory of
\mathrm{mod}B^{\prime}
with the induced exact structure. Moreover,
\mathcal{H}
is a Frobenius exact category with projective-injective objects
\mathcal{P}=\mathrm{proj}(B^{\prime})
and
\mathcal{H}
contains an
n
-cluster-tilting object
T
, namely the image of
A
, such that
\mathrm{End}_{\mathcal{H}}(T)\cong H^{0}(A)
with
\mathrm{gldim}\,H^{0}(A)\leqslant n+1
.

By Theorem
5.42
, the Higgs category is idempotent complete. Thus, we can apply Iyama–Kalck–Wemyss–Yang’s structure theorem for Frobenius exact categories with an
n
-cluster-tilting object (see
[
33
, Theorem 2.7]
), to conclude that
B^{\prime}
is Iwanaga-Gorenstein of injective dimension at most
n+1
as a
B^{\prime}
-module and that restriction to
B^{\prime}
is an equivalence from
{\mathcal{H}}\subseteq\mathrm{mod}H^{0}(A)
to the category
\mathrm{gpr}(B^{\prime})
of Gorenstein projective
B^{\prime}
-modules, i.e. we have an equivalence

\mathcal{H}\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathrm{gpr}(B^{\prime})=\{M\in\mathrm{mod}B^{\prime}\ |\ \mathrm{Ext}_{B^{\prime}}^{i}(M,B^{\prime})=0,\ \forall i>0\}.

This shows
a)
,
b)
,
c)
and
d)
. It remains to show
e)
.

Let
\mathrm{per}{\mathcal{M}}
be the full subcategory of the derived category of modules over
{\mathcal{M}}
generated by all representable functors and let
\mathrm{per}_{\underline{{\mathcal{M}}}}
be its full subcategory consisting of complexes whose cohomologies are in
\mathrm{mod}\underline{{\mathcal{M}}}\cong\mathrm{mod}\overline{A}
. By
[
51
, Lemma 7]
, the Yoneda equivalence of triangulated categories
{\mathcal{K}}^{b}({\mathcal{M}})\rightarrow\mathrm{per}{\mathcal{M}}\cong\mathrm{per}A
induces a triangle equivalence

{\mathcal{K}}^{b}_{{\mathcal{H}}-ac}({\mathcal{M}})\rightarrow\mathrm{per}_{\underline{{\mathcal{M}}}}{\mathcal{M}}\cong\mathrm{pvd}_{B}(A).

Thus, we finish the proof.
∎

6.1.
Relation with Pressland’s works

Definition 6.3
.

[
54
]

Let
A
be a
k
-algebra,
e
an idempotent of
A
, and
d
a non-negative integer. We say that
A
is
internally
d
-Calabi–Yau
with respect to
e
if

(1)

\mathrm{gldim}A\leqslant d
, and

(2)

for each
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, there is a functorial duality

\textstyle{D\mathrm{Ext}^{i}_{A}(M,N)\cong\mathrm{Ext}^{d-i}_{A}(N,M)}

where
M
and
N
are quasi-isomorphic to a bounded complex of finitely generated projective
A
-modules such that
M
is also a finite-dimensional

A/AeA
-module.

Let
A
be an algebra and
e
an idempotent of
A
. We denote the corresponding quotient algebra by
\overline{A}\coloneqq A/\langle e\rangle
. Let
\mathcal{D}(A)
be the unbounded derived category of
A
,
\mathcal{D}_{e}(A)
the full subcategory of
\mathcal{D}(A)

consisting of complexes with homology groups in
\mathrm{Mod}(\overline{A})
, and
\mathrm{pvd}_{e}(A)
the full subcategory of
\mathcal{D}_{e}(A)
consisting of objects with finite dimensional total cohomology.

Recall that the inverse dualizing bimodule of
A
is defined as
A^{\vee}=\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{A^{e}}(A,A^{e})
.

Definition 6.4
.

[
54
]

An algebra
A
is
bimodule internally
n
-Calabi–Yau
with respect to an idempotent
e\in A
if

•

\mathrm{pdim}_{A^{e}}A\leqslant n
,

•

A\in\mathrm{per}A^{e}
, and

•

there exists a triangle

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{n}A^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma A}

in
\mathcal{D}(A^{e})
, such that
\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{A}(C,M)=0=\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{A^{op}}(C,N)
for any
M\in\mathrm{pvd}_{e}(A)
and
N\in\mathrm{pvd}_{e}(A^{op})
.

Proposition 6.5
.

[
53
, Corollary 5.12]

If
A
is internally bimodule
n
-Calabi–Yau with respect to an idempotent
e
of
A
, then it is internally
n
-Calabi–Yau with respect to
e
.

Proposition 6.6
.

Let
f\colon B\to A
be a morphism between dg
k
-algebras. Suppose that
f
satisfies the assumptions
1
and moreover,
A
is concentrated in degree
0
. Then
A
and
A^{op}
are internally bimodule
(n+1)
-Calabi–Yau with respect to
e=f(\boldmath{1}_{B})
. Hence, the algebras
A
and
A^{op}
are internally
(n+1)
-Calabi–Yau with respect to
e=f(\boldmath{1}_{B})
.

Proof
.
By the definition of a relative
(n+1)
-Calabi–Yau structure, we have the following triangle in
\mathcal{D}(A^{e})

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{n+1}A^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{n+1}\textbf{L}f^{*}(B^{\vee})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Sigma A,}

where
A^{\vee}=\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{A^{e}}(A,A^{e})
,
B^{\vee}=\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{B^{e}}(B,B^{e})
and
\mathbf{L}f^{*}(B^{\vee})\cong A\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{B}B^{\vee}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{B}A
.

Let
M
be an object in
\mathrm{pvd}_{e}(A)
. We have

\begin{split}\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{A}(\mathbf{L}f^{*}(B^{\vee}),M)&=\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{A}(A\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{B}B^{\vee}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{B}A,M)\\
&\simeq\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{A}(A\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{B}B^{\vee},\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{B}(A,M|_{B}))\\
&=0\end{split}

Similarly, we have
\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{A^{op}}(\mathbf{L}f^{*}(B^{\vee}),N)=0
for any
N\in\mathrm{pvd}_{e}(A^{op})
. Thus, the algebra
A
is bimodule internally
(n+1)
-Calabi–Yau with respect to the idempotent
e=f(\boldmath{1}_{B})
. In the same way, we can show that
A^{op}
is bimodule internally
(n+1)
-Calabi–Yau with respect to the idempotent
e
.

∎

7.
Relative cluster categories for Jacobi-finite ice quivers with potential

7.1.
Ice quivers with potential

Definition 7.1
.

A
quiver
is a tuple
Q=(Q_{0},Q_{1},s,t)
, where
Q_{0}
and
Q_{1}
are sets, and
s,t\colon Q_{1}\to Q_{0}
are functions. Each
\alpha\in Q_{1}
is realised as an arrow
\alpha\colon s(\alpha)\to t(\alpha)
. We call
Q
finite if
Q_{0}
and
Q_{1}
are finite sets.

Definition 7.2
.

Let
Q
be a quiver. A quiver
F=(F_{0},F_{1},s^{\prime},t^{\prime})
is called a
subquiver
of
Q
if
F_{0}\subseteq Q_{0}
,
F_{1}\subseteq Q_{1}
and
s^{\prime},t^{\prime}
are the restrictions of
s,t
to
F_{1}
. We call
F
a
full subquiver
of
Q
if
F
is a subquiver and
F_{1}=\{\alpha\in Q_{1}\colon s(\alpha),t(\alpha)\in F_{0}\}
.

Definition 7.3
.

An
ice quiver
is a pair
(Q,F)
, where
Q
is a quiver, and
F
is a subquiver of
Q
.

Let
Q
be a finite quiver. For each arrow
a
of
Q
, we define the cyclic derivative with respect to
a
as the unique linear map

\textstyle{\partial_{a}\colon kQ/[kQ,kQ]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{kQ}

which takes the class of a path
p
to the sum
\sum_{p=uav}vu
taken over all decompositions of the path
p
.

Definition 7.4
.

An element of
kQ/[kQ,kQ]
is called a
potential
on
Q
. It is given by a linear combination of cycles in
Q
. An ice quiver with potential is a tuple
(Q,F,W)
in which
(Q,F)
is a finite ice quiver without loops, and
W
is a potential on
Q
. If
F
is the empty quiver
\emptyset
, then
(Q,\emptyset,W)\coloneqq(Q,W)
is simply called a quiver with potential.

7.2.
Relative Ginzburg algebras and relative Jacobian algebras

Definition 7.5
.

Let
(Q,F,W)
be a finite ice quiver with potential. Let
\widetilde{Q}
be the graded quiver with the same vertices as
Q
and whose arrows are

•

the arrows of
Q
,

•

an arrow
a^{*}\colon j\to i
of degree
-1
for each arrow
a
of
Q
not belonging to
F
,

•

a loop
t_{i}\colon i\to i
of degree
-2
for each vertex
i
of
Q
not belonging to
F
.

The
relative Ginzburg dg algebra

\bm{\Gamma}_{rel}(Q,F,W)
is the dg algebra whose underlying graded vector space is the graded path algebra
k\widetilde{Q}
. Its differential is the unique linear endomorphism of degree 1 which satisfies the Leibniz rule

\textstyle{d(u\circ v)=d(u)\circ v+(-1)^{p}u\circ d(v)}

for all homogeneous
u
of degree
p
and all
v
, and takes the following values on the arrows of
\widetilde{Q}
:

•

d(a)=0
for each arrow
a
of
Q
,

•

d(a^{*})=\partial_{a}W
for each arrow
a
of
Q
not belonging to
F
,

•

d(t_{i})=e_{i}(\sum_{a\in Q_{1}}[a,a^{*}])e_{i}
for each vertex
i
of
Q
not belonging to
F
, where
e_{i}
is the lazy path corresponding to the vertex
i
.

Definition 7.6
.

Let
(Q,F,W)
be a finite ice quiver with potential. The
relative (or frozen) Jacobian algebra

J(Q,F,W)
is the zeroth cohomology of the relative Ginzburg algebra
\bm{\Gamma}_{rel}(Q,F,W)
. It is the quotient algebra

\displaystyle kQ/\langle\partial_{a}W,a\in Q_{1}\setminus F_{1}\rangle

where
\langle\partial_{a}W,a\in Q_{1}\setminus F_{1}\rangle
is the two-sided ideal generated by
\partial_{a}W
with
a\in Q_{1}\setminus F_{1}
.

Let
(Q,F,W)
be a finite ice quiver with potential. Since
W
can be viewed as an element in
HC_{0}(kQ)
,
c=B(W)
is an element in
HH_{1}(kQ)
, where
B
is the Connes connecting map (see
[
40
, Section 6.1]
)

B\colon H\!C_{n}(kQ)\rightarrow H\!H_{n+1}(kQ).

Then
\xi=(0,c)
is an element of
H\!H_{0}(G)
which provides the deformation parameter for the relative 3-Calabi–Yau completion of
G\colon kF\hookrightarrow kQ
, namely the functor

\bm{G}_{rel}\colon\bm{\Pi}_{2}(F)\rightarrow\bm{\Pi}^{red}_{3}(Q,F,\xi)

defined in Proposition
3.18
. An easy check shows that the dg algebra
\bm{\Pi}^{red}_{3}(kQ,kF,\xi)
is isomorphic to
\bm{\Gamma}_{rel}(Q,F,W)
and that the dg functor
\bm{G}_{rel}
takes the following values as follows:

•

\bm{G}_{rel}(i)=i
for each frozen vertex
i\in F_{0}
,

•

\bm{G}_{rel}(a)=a
for each arrow
a\in F_{1}
,

•

\bm{G}_{rel}(\tilde{a})=-\partial_{a}W
for each arrow
a\in F_{1}
,

•

\bm{G}_{rel}(r_{i})=e_{i}(\sum_{a\in Q_{1}\setminus F_{1}}[a,a^{*}])e_{i}
for each frozen vertex
i\in F_{0}
.

We call
\bm{G}_{rel}
the
Ginzburg functor
associated with
G\colon kF\hookrightarrow kQ
and
W
.

Remark 7.7
.

If we apply
H^{0}
to
\bm{G}_{rel}
, we recover Proposition 8.1 of
[
55
]
.

Proposition 7.8
.

Let
(Q,F,W)
be a finite ice quiver with potential. Let
\overline{Q}
be the quiver obtained from
Q
by deleting all vertices in
F
and all arrows incident with vertices in
F
. Let
\overline{W}
be the potential on
\overline{Q}
obtaining by deleting all cycles passing through vertices of
F
in
W
. Then

\bm{\Pi}_{2}(F)\xrightarrow{\bm{G}_{rel}}\bm{\Gamma}_{rel}(Q,F,W)\to\bm{\Gamma}(\overline{Q},\overline{W})

is a
homotopy cofiber sequence of dg categories, where
\bm{\Gamma}(\overline{Q},\overline{W})
is the Ginzburg algebra (see
[
40
]
) associated with quiver with potential
(\overline{Q},\overline{W})
.

Proof
.
By Proposition
3.18
, the homotopy cofiber of
\bm{G}_{rel}
is isomorphic to that of
\tilde{G}
. Since
\tilde{G}
is a cofibration, the dg quotient identifies with the quotient of
\bm{\Pi}_{3}(kQ,kF,\xi)
by the 2-sided ideal generated by the image of
\tilde{G}
. This quotient is isomorphic to
\bm{\Gamma}(\overline{Q},\overline{W})
as a dg category.
∎

7.3.
Jacobi-finite ice quivers with potential

An ice quiver with potential
(Q,F,W)
is called
Jacobi-finite
if the relative Jacobian algebra
J(Q,F,W)
is finite-dimensional.

Definition 7.9
.

Let
(Q,F,W)
be a Jacobi-finite ice quiver with potential. Denote by
\bm{\Gamma}_{rel}
the relative Ginzburg dg algebra
\bm{\Gamma}_{rel}(Q,F,W)
. Let
e=\sum_{i\in F}e_{i}
be the idempotent associated with all frozen vertices. Let
\mathrm{pvd}_{e}(\bm{\Gamma}_{rel})
be the full subcategory of
\mathrm{pvd}(\bm{\Gamma}_{rel})
consisting of the dg
\bm{\Gamma}_{rel}
-modules whose restriction to frozen vertices is acyclic.

Then the
relative cluster category

\mathcal{C}(Q,F,W)
associated to
(Q,F,W)
is defined as the Verdier quotient of triangulated categories

\mathrm{per}(\bm{\Gamma}_{rel})/\mathrm{pvd}_{e}(\bm{\Gamma}_{rel}).

The relative fundamental domain
\mathcal{F}^{rel}
associated to
(Q,F,W)
is defined as the following subcategory of
\mathrm{per}\,\bm{\Gamma}_{rel}

\mathcal{F}^{rel}\coloneqq\{\mathrm{Cone}(X_{1}\xrightarrow{f}X_{0})\ |\ X_{i}\in\mathrm{add}(\bm{\Gamma}_{rel})\,\,\text{and}\,\,\mathrm{Hom}(f,I)\ \text{is surjective},\ \forall\ I\in\mathcal{P}=\mathrm{add}(e\bm{\Gamma}_{rel})\}.

We have a fully faithful embedding
\pi^{rel}\colon\mathcal{F}^{rel}\subseteq\mathrm{per}\,\bm{\Gamma}_{rel}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}(Q,F,W)
. Then the Higgs category
\mathcal{H}
associated to
(Q,F,W)
is the image of
\mathcal{F}^{rel}
in
\mathcal{C}(Q,F,W)
under the functor
\pi^{rel}
.

Combining Theorem
5.46
and Proposition
5.49
, we get the result.

Theorem 7.10
.

Let
(Q,F,W)
be a Jacobi-finite ice quiver with potential. Then the relative cluster category
\mathcal{C}(Q,F,W)
is Hom-finite, and the Higgs category
\mathcal{H}
is a Frobenius 2-Calabi–Yau extriangulated category with projective-injective objects
\mathcal{P}=\mathrm{add}(e\bm{\Gamma}_{rel})
. The free module
\bm{\Gamma}_{rel}
in
\mathcal{H}
is a cluster-tilting object. Its endomorphism algebra is isomorphic to the relative Jacobian algebra
J(Q,F,W)
.

Moreover, the stable category of
\mathcal{H}
is equivalent to the usual cluster category

\underline{\mathcal{H}}=\mathcal{H}/[\mathcal{P}]\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{C}(\overline{Q},\overline{W})=\mathrm{per}({\bm{\Gamma}}(\overline{Q},\overline{W}))/\mathrm{pvd}({\bm{\Gamma}}(\overline{Q},\overline{W}))

and the following diagram commutes

\textstyle{\mathrm{per}(e\bm{\Gamma}_{rel}e)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{per}(e\bm{\Gamma}_{rel}e)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{pvd}_{e}(\bm{\Gamma}_{rel})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\mathrm{per}(\bm{\Gamma}_{rel})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}(Q,F,W)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{pvd}({\bm{\Gamma}}(\overline{Q},\overline{W}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{per}({\bm{\Gamma}}(\overline{Q},\overline{W}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}(\overline{Q},\overline{W})}

where the rows and columns are exact sequences of triangulated categories.

Let
(Q,F,W)
be an ice quiver with potential. For simplicity of notation, we write
J_{rel}
and
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{rel}
respectively instead of
J(Q,F,W)
and
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{rel}(Q,F,W)
. Let
Q_{0}^{m}=Q_{0}\setminus F_{0}
and
Q_{1}^{m}=Q_{1}\setminus F_{1}
. Let
S
be the semisimple
k
-algebra
\prod_{i\in Q_{0}}ke_{i}
. We denote by
S^{m}
,
V
and
V^{m}
the
S
-bimodules generated by
Q_{0}^{m}
,
Q_{1}
and
Q_{1}^{m}
respectively. Let
V^{m*}
be the dual bimodule
\mathrm{Hom}_{S^{e}}(V^{m},S^{e})
.

There is a canonical short exact sequence of
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{rel}
-bimodules

\textstyle{0\rightarrow\ker(m)\xrightarrow{\rho}\mathbf{\Gamma}_{rel}\otimes_{S}\mathbf{\Gamma}_{rel}\xrightarrow{m}\mathbf{\Gamma}_{rel}\rightarrow 0,}

where the map
m
is induced by the multiplication of
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{rel}
. The mapping cone
\mathrm{Cone}(\rho)
of
\rho
is a cofibrant resolution of
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{rel}
as a bimodule over itself.

Then
P(J_{rel})=J_{rel}\otimes_{\bm{\Gamma}_{rel}}\mathrm{Cone}(\rho)\otimes_{\bm{\Gamma}_{rel}}J_{rel}
is the following complex

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{J_{rel}\otimes_{S}\otimes S^{m}\otimes_{S}J_{rel}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m_{3}}
\textstyle{J_{rel}\otimes_{S}\otimes V^{m*}\otimes_{S}J_{rel}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m_{2}}
\textstyle{J_{rel}\otimes_{S}\otimes V\otimes_{S}J_{rel}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m_{1}}
\textstyle{J_{rel}\otimes_{S}J_{rel}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}

where
m_{3}
,
m_{2}
and
m_{1}
are as follows:

m_{1}(x\otimes a\otimes y)=xa\otimes y-x\otimes ay

and

m_{3}(x\otimes t_{i}\otimes y)=\sum_{a,t(a)=t_{i}}xa\otimes a^{*}\otimes y-\sum_{b,s(b)=t_{i}}x\otimes b^{*}\otimes by.

For any path
p=a_{m}\cdots a_{1}
of
kQ
, we define

\displaystyle\triangle_{a}(p)=\sum_{a_{i}=a}a_{m\cdots}a_{i+1}\otimes a_{i}\otimes a_{i-1}\cdots a_{1},

and extend by linearity to obtain a map
\triangle_{a}\colon kQ\to J_{rel}\otimes_{S}kQ_{1}\otimes_{S}J_{rel}
. Then
m_{2}
is given by:

m_{2}(x\otimes a^{*}\otimes y)=\sum_{b\in Q_{1}}x\triangle_{b}(\partial_{a}W)y.

There is a canonical morphism
P(J_{rel})\to J_{rel}
, which is induced by the multiplication map
m
in
J_{rel}

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{J_{rel}\otimes_{S}\otimes S^{m}\otimes_{S}J_{rel}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m_{3}}
\textstyle{J_{rel}\otimes_{S}\otimes V^{m*}\otimes_{S}J_{rel}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m_{2}}
\textstyle{J_{rel}\otimes_{S}\otimes V\otimes_{S}J_{rel}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{J_{rel}\otimes_{S}J_{rel}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{J_{rel}.}

Remark 7.11
.

When
F=\emptyset
, the complex (
7.3
) defined above is the complex associated to
(Q,W)
defined by Ginzburg in
[
17
, Section 5]
. In general, it is exactly the complex
P(J_{rel})
defined by Pressland in
[
53
, Deﬁnition 5.21]
. Moreover, it has already appeared in the work of Amiot–Reiten–Todorov (see
[
4
, Propostion 2.2]
). By a result of Butler–King
[
10
, 1.2]
, the vertical maps
\alpha
and
m
induce isomorphisms on cohomology. Thus, the canonical morphism
P(J_{rel})\rightarrow J_{rel}
is a quasi-isomorphism if and only if the cohomology of
P(J_{rel})
vanishes at
J_{rel}\otimes_{S}V^{m*}\otimes_{S}J_{rel}
and
J_{rel}\otimes_{S}R^{m}\otimes_{S}J_{rel}
.

Applying the functor
S\otimes_{J_{rel}}?
to the complex (
7.3
), we get another complex

0\rightarrow S\otimes_{J_{rel}}P(J_{rel})\rightarrow S\rightarrow 0.

The complex above decomposes along with
S
, so its exactness is equivalent to the exactness of the following complex

\textstyle{0\rightarrow S_{v}\otimes_{J_{rel}}P(J_{rel})\rightarrow S_{v}\rightarrow 0}

for each
v\in Q_{0}
, where
S_{v}
is the simple
J_{rel}
-module at the vertex
v
. If
v
is an unfrozen vertex, the complex (
7.3
) is the following complex

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{e_{v}J_{rel}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(b)}
\textstyle{\displaystyle\bigoplus_{b\in Q_{1},s(b)=v}e_{t(b)}J_{rel}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(a^{-1}(\partial_{b}W))}
\textstyle{\displaystyle\bigoplus_{a\in Q_{1},t(a)=v}e_{s(a)}J_{rel}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(a)}
\textstyle{e_{v}J_{rel}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S_{v}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

If
v
is a frozen vertex, the complex (
7.3
) is the following complex

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\displaystyle\bigoplus_{b\in Q_{1}\setminus F_{1},s(b)=v}e_{t(b)}J_{rel}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(a^{-1}(\partial_{b}W))}
\textstyle{\displaystyle\bigoplus_{a\in Q_{1},t(a)=v}e_{s(a)}J_{rel}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(a)}
\textstyle{e_{v}J_{rel}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S_{v}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

When
c
is a path in
Q
, we write
a^{-1}c=b
if
b=ac
in
kQ
and
a^{-1}c=0
otherwise.

Theorem 7.12
.

Let
(Q,F,W)
be an ice quiver with potential. Suppose that
F
is a full subquiver of
Q
. Let
\bm{\Gamma}_{rel}(Q,F,W)
be the complete relative Ginzburg algebra (see
[
61
, Definition 4.20]
). The following statements are equivalent.

(i)

\bm{\Gamma}_{rel}
is a stalk algebra, i.e. its homology is concentrated in degree zero.

(ii)

The canonical morphism (
7.3
) is a quasi-isomorphism.

(iii)

For each vertex
v\in Q_{0}
, the corresponding complex (
7.3
) or (
7.3
) is exact.

(iv)

The canonical map
g_{rel}\colon\bm{\Pi}_{2}(F)\xrightarrow{\bm{G}_{rel}}\bm{\Gamma}_{rel}(Q,F,W)\rightarrow J_{rel}
has a left 3-Calabi–Yau structure which is induced by the canonical left 3-Calabi–Yau structure on
\bm{G}_{rel}
.

Moreover, if
(Q,F,W)
is Jacobi-finite, then the above statements are also equivalent to

(v)

The extriangulated structure on
{\mathcal{H}}
is exact (see Theorem
7.10
) and for every frozen vertex
v\in F_{0}
, the module
\mathrm{rad}(e_{v}J_{rel})
is of the form
\mathrm{Hom}_{{\mathcal{C}}(Q,F,W)}(\bm{\Gamma}_{rel},T_{v})
for some
T_{v}
in
{\mathcal{H}}
.

Proof
.

(i)\Rightarrow(ii)\Rightarrow(iii)
and
(i)\Rightarrow(iv)
are clear. To prove that
(iii)\Rightarrow(i)
, let
{\mathcal{D}}_{pc}(\bm{\Gamma}_{rel})
be the pseudocompact derived category of
\bm{\Gamma}_{rel}
(see
[
42
, Section 7.11]
). By
[
42
, Proposition 7.14]
,
({\mathcal{D}}_{pc}(\bm{\Gamma}_{rel}))^{op}
is compactly generated by
\{S_{v}\,|\,v\in Q_{0}\}
and similarly for
({\mathcal{D}}_{pc}(J_{rel}))^{op}
. The restriction functor

\theta\colon{\mathcal{D}}_{pc}(J_{rel})\rightarrow{\mathcal{D}}_{pc}(\bm{\Gamma}_{rel})

takes
S_{v}
to
S_{v}
. We can conclude that
\theta
is an equivalence if it induces isomorphisms

\mathrm{Ext}^{*}_{J_{rel}}(S_{i},S_{j})\xrightarrow{{}_{\sim}}\mathrm{Ext}^{*}_{\bm{\Gamma}_{rel}}(S_{i},S_{j})\,\forall i,j\in Q_{0}.

The complexes (
7.3
), (
7.3
) are exact, i.e.
S_{i}\otimes_{J_{rel}}\mathrm{Cone}(\rho)\otimes_{J_{rel}}J_{rel}\rightarrow S_{i}
is exact for each vertex
i
(see (
7.3
)). So we can use it to compute
\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}
:

\begin{split}\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{J_{rel}}(S_{i},S_{j})&=\mathrm{Hom}_{J_{rel}}(S_{i}\otimes_{J_{rel}}\mathrm{Cone}(\rho)\otimes_{J_{rel}}J_{rel},S_{j})\\
&=\mathrm{Hom}_{\bm{\Gamma}_{rel}}(S_{i}\otimes_{J_{rel}}\mathrm{Cone}(\rho),\mathrm{Hom}_{J_{rel}}(J_{rel},S_{j}))\\
&=\mathrm{Hom}_{\bm{\Gamma}_{rel}}(S_{i}\otimes_{\bm{\Gamma}_{rel}}\mathrm{Cone}(\rho),S_{j})\\
&=\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{\bm{\Gamma}_{rel}}(S_{i},S_{j}).\end{split}

Thus, the restriction functor
\theta\colon{\mathcal{D}}_{pc}(J_{rel})\rightarrow{\mathcal{D}}_{pc}(\bm{\Gamma}_{rel})
is an equivalence. It follows that
\bm{\Gamma}_{rel}\rightarrow J_{rel}
is a quasi-isomorphism. Thus, the complete relative Ginzburg algebra
\bm{\Gamma}_{rel}
is concentrated in degree 0.

To prove that
(iv)\Rightarrow(iii)
, suppose that the canonical map

g_{rel}\colon\bm{\Pi}_{2}(F)\xrightarrow{\bm{G}_{rel}}\bm{\Gamma}_{rel}(Q,F,W)\rightarrow J_{rel}

has a canonical left 3-Calabi–Yau structure. Let
k
be an unfrozen vertex. By the proof of Proposition
6.1
, we see that the simple
J_{rel}
-module
S_{k}
has projective dimension at most 3. Therefore, by Remark
7.11
, there is a projective resolution of length 3

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\bigoplus_{j\in Q_{0}}(e_{j}J_{rel})^{m_{kj}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\bigoplus_{b\in Q_{1},s(b)=k}e_{t(b)}J_{rel}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\bigoplus_{a\in Q_{1},t(a)=k}e_{s(a)}J_{rel}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{k}J_{rel}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{k}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(a^{-1}(\partial_{b}(W)))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(a)}

Using the relative Calabi–Yau property (see Corollary
3.13
) and comparing dimensions, we conclude that

m_{kj}=\dim\mathrm{Ext}^{3}_{J_{rel}}(S_{k},S_{j})=\dim\mathrm{Hom}_{J_{rel}}(S_{j},S_{k})=\delta_{jk}.

Thus, the above resolution is exactly the complex (
7.3
).

Let
v
be a frozen vertex. Using the same proof as for Proposition
6.1
, we show that the frozen simple
J_{rel}
-module
S_{v}
has projective dimension at most 2. Therefore, there is a projective resolution of length 2

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\bigoplus_{j\in Q_{0}}(e_{j}J_{rel})^{m_{vj}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\bigoplus_{a,t(a)=v}e_{s(a)}J_{rel}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{v}J_{rel}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{v}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(a)}

Similarly, using the relative Calabi–Yau property (see Corollary
3.13
) and comparing dimensions, we conclude that

•

For any unfrozen vertex
k
, we have

\begin{split}\dim\mathrm{Ext}^{2}_{J_{rel}}(S_{v},S_{k})&=\dim\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{J_{rel}}(S_{k},S_{v})\\
&=\sharp\{a\in Q_{1}\colon v\to k\}\\
&=\sharp\{a\in Q_{1}^{m}\colon v\to k\}.\end{split}

•

For any frozen vertex
w\neq v
, by Proposition
3.12
, we have an exact sequence

D\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\bm{\Pi}_{2}(F)}(S_{w},S_{v})\rightarrow D\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{J_{rel}}(S_{w},S_{v})\rightarrow\mathrm{Ext}^{2}_{J_{rel}}(S_{v},S_{w})\rightarrow 0.

Since
F
is a full subquiver of
Q
, the space
\mathrm{Ext}^{2}_{J_{rel}}(S_{v},S_{w})
vanishes. Thus, we can conclude that

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{j\in Q_{0}}(e_{j}J_{rel})^{m_{vj}}=\displaystyle\bigoplus_{b\in Q_{1}^{m},s(b)=v}e_{t(b)}J_{rel}.

Hence the complex (
7.3
) is exact. This finishes the proof of
(iv)\Rightarrow(iii)
.

Now, suppose that
(Q,F,W)
is Jacobi-finite. By Theorem
6.2
,
(i)\Rightarrow(v)
is clear. To prove that
(v)\Rightarrow(iii)
, suppose that the extriangulated structure on
{\mathcal{H}}
is exact. All inflations in
{\mathcal{H}}
are monomorphisms and all deflations in
{\mathcal{H}}
are epimorphisms. Let
j
be an unfrozen vertex. We have exchange conflations in
{\mathcal{H}}
(
[
4
, Proposition 1.1 and 1.2]
)

e_{j}\bm{\Gamma}_{rel}\rightarrowtail\bigoplus_{j\to k\in Q_{1}}e_{k}\bm{\Gamma}_{rel}\twoheadrightarrow T_{j}^{*}\quad\text{and}\quad T_{j}^{*}\rightarrowtail\bigoplus_{i\to j\in Q_{1}}e_{i}\bm{\Gamma}_{rel}\twoheadrightarrow e_{j}\bm{\Gamma}_{rel}.

Thus, we get a 2-almost-split sequence

\textstyle{0\rightarrow e_{j}\bm{\Gamma}_{rel}\rightarrow\displaystyle\bigoplus_{j\to k\in Q_{1}}e_{k}\bm{\Gamma}_{rel}\rightarrow\displaystyle\bigoplus_{i\to j\in Q_{1}}e_{i}\bm{\Gamma}_{rel}\rightarrow e_{j}\bm{\Gamma}_{rel}\rightarrow 0.}

Applying the functor
\mathrm{Hom}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\bm{\Gamma}_{rel},?)
to the above 2-almost-split sequence, we get a projective resolution of
S_{j}
which is exactly the complex
7.3
. Similarly, applying the functor
D\mathrm{Hom}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(?,\bm{\Gamma}_{rel})
to the 2-almost split sequence, we get an injective resolution of
S_{j}

S_{j}\hookrightarrow D(J_{rel}e_{j})\xrightarrow{(b)}\displaystyle\bigoplus_{b\in Q_{1},s(b)=j}D(J_{rel}e_{t(b)})\rightarrow\displaystyle\bigoplus_{a\in Q_{1},t(a)=j}D(J_{rel}e_{s(a)})\twoheadrightarrow D(J_{rel}e_{j}).

Suppose that for every frozen vertex
v\in F_{0}
, the functor

\mathrm{rad}(?,e_{v}\bm{\Gamma}_{rel})|_{{\mathcal{H}}}\colon{\mathcal{H}}\rightarrow\mathrm{mod}J_{rel}

is representable in
{\mathcal{H}}
.
Let
v
be a frozen vertex. By the assumption, there exists an object
T_{1}\in\mathrm{add}\bm{\Gamma}_{rel}
such that we have a conflation in
{\mathcal{H}}

T_{1}\rightarrowtail\displaystyle\bigoplus_{a,t(a)=v}e_{s(a)}\bm{\Gamma}_{rel}\twoheadrightarrow e_{v}\bm{\Gamma}_{rel}.

This shows that
\mathrm{pdim}S_{v}=2
. Let

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\bigoplus_{j\in Q_{0}}(e_{j}J_{rel})^{m_{vj}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\bigoplus_{a,t(a)=v}e_{s(a)}J_{rel}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{v}J_{rel}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{v}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(a)}

be a minimal projective resolution of
S_{v}
. Since
F
is full, there are no direct summands
\{e_{v}J_{rel}\,|\,v\in F_{0}\}
in
\displaystyle\bigoplus_{j\in Q_{0}}(e_{j}J_{rel})^{m_{kj}}
. For each unfrozen vertex
j
, we have

\begin{split}\dim\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{J_{rel}}(S_{v},S_{j})&=\dim\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\bm{\Gamma}_{rel}}(S_{v},S_{j})\\
&=\sharp\{a\in Q_{1}\colon j\to v\}\end{split}

and

\begin{split}m_{vj}&=\dim\mathrm{Ext}^{2}_{J_{rel}}(S_{v},S_{j})\\
&=\dim(\displaystyle\bigoplus_{v\to j}(S_{v},D(J_{rel}e_{j})))\\
&=\sharp\{b\in Q_{1}^{m}\colon v\to j\}.\end{split}

Thus, we conclude that

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{j\in Q_{0}}(e_{j}J_{rel})^{m_{vj}}=\displaystyle\bigoplus_{b\in Q_{1}^{m},s(b)=v}e_{t(b)}J_{rel}.

Hence we get the resolution complex (
7.3
).

∎

8.
Relative Calabi–Yau structures in higher Auslander–Reiten theory

8.1.
For algebras of finite global dimension

Let
n
be a non-negative integer. Let
B_{0}
be a finite dimensional algebra with global dimension at most
n
. Let
\mathbb{S}_{B_{0}}=?\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{B_{0}}DB_{0}
be the Serre functor of
\mathcal{D}^{b}(\mathrm{mod}B_{0})
. The corresponding inverse Serre functor is given by
\mathbb{S}^{-1}_{B_{0}}=?\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{B_{0}}\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{B^{e}}(B_{0},B_{0}^{e})
. Moreover, the
Nakayama functor

\nu_{B_{0}}
for
\mathrm{mod}B_{0}
is given by
\nu_{B_{0}}=D\mathrm{Hom}_{B_{0}}(?,B_{0})
.

Definition 8.1
.

[
24
]

The
higher inverse Auslander-Reiten translation

\tau^{-1}_{n}
of
\mathrm{mod}B_{0}
is defined to be the following composition

\textstyle{\tau^{-1}_{n}\colon\mathrm{mod}B_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}^{b}(B_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma^{n}\mathbb{S}^{-1}_{B_{0}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}^{b}(B_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{H^{0}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{mod}B_{0}.}

Definition 8.2
.

Let
f\colon\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
be a dg functor. The
relative inverse Serre functor
for
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})
is defined as

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 65.01782pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-65.01782pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}^{-1}=?\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{A}}}\Theta_{f}\colon\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

where
\Theta_{f}=\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}^{e}}(\mathrm{Cone}(\mathcal{A}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}}\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}),\mathcal{A}^{e})\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}^{e})
.

Remark 8.3
.

It is clear that we have an isomorphism
\bm{\Pi}_{n+2}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\simeq\bigoplus_{i\geqslant 0}(\Sigma^{n+1}\Theta_{f})^{\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{i}}
in
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})
.

Definition 8.4
.

[
27
]

Let
B_{0}
be an algebra of global dimension at most
n
. Then the
(n+1)
-
preprojective algebra
of
B_{0}
is defined as

\widetilde{B_{0}}=T_{B_{0}}(\mathrm{Ext}_{B_{0}}^{n}(DB_{0},B_{0})),

i.e, the tensor algebra of the
B_{0}
-bimodule
\mathrm{Ext}_{B_{0}}^{n}(DB_{0},B_{0})
over
B_{0}
. Then

\widetilde{B_{0}}
is isomorphic to
\bigoplus_{i\geqslant 0}\tau_{n}^{-i}B_{0}
as a
B_{0}
-module.

Remark 8.5
.

In
[
40
, Section 4]
, Keller introduced the notion of the derived
(n+1)
-preprojective algebra
\bm{\Pi}_{n+1}(B_{0})
(also called
(n+1)
-Calabi–Yau completion of
B_{0}
). The
(n+1)
-preprojective algebras are the
0
-th homology of his derived
(n+1)
-preprojective algebras.

We denote by
\mathcal{B}\coloneqq\mathrm{proj}B_{0}\subseteq\mathrm{mod}B_{0}
the projective modules. Let
\mathcal{A}
be a subcategory of
\mathrm{mod}B_{0}
which contains
\mathcal{B}
as a full subcategory. Then there is a natural dg inclusion functor

f_{0}\colon\mathcal{B}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{A}.

For any
X\in\mathcal{A}
, we put
X^{\wedge}\coloneqq\mathrm{Hom}_{B_{0}}(?,X)|_{\mathcal{A}}\in\mathrm{proj}\mathcal{A}
. For simplicity of notation, let
\Theta_{{\mathcal{B}}}
(respectively
\Theta_{{\mathcal{A}}}
) stand for
{\mathcal{B}}^{\vee}=\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{B}^{e}}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{B}^{e})
(respectively
{\mathcal{A}}^{\vee}
) in the next Proposition.

Proposition 8.6
.

Assume that
\mathcal{A}
is homologically smooth and is an
n
-rigid subcategory of
\mathrm{mod}B_{0}
, i.e.
\mathrm{Ext}^{k}_{B_{0}}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A})=0
for
1\leqslant k\leqslant n-1
. Then for
X\in\mathcal{A}
, we have a functorial isomorphism
X^{\wedge}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{A}}}\Sigma^{n+1}\Theta_{f_{0}}\cong(\tau_{n}^{-1}X)^{\wedge}
.

Proof
.
Let
X
be an object in
{\mathcal{A}}
. We will show that

X^{\wedge}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{A}}}\Sigma^{n+1}\Theta_{f_{0}}\cong(\tau_{n}^{-1}X)^{\wedge},

where
\Theta_{f_{0}}=\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}^{e}}(\mathrm{Cone}(\mathcal{A}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}}\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}),\mathcal{A}^{e})
.

Step 1.
We compute the image of
X^{\wedge}
under the functor
?\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{A}}}\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{{\mathcal{A}}^{e}}(\mathcal{A}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}}\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}^{e})\colon{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{A}})\rightarrow{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{A}}).

Since
\mathcal{B}
is smooth, we have

\begin{split}\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}^{e}}(\mathcal{A}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}}\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}^{e})\cong&\ \mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}^{e}}(\mathcal{A}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}}\mathcal{B}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}}\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}^{e})\\
\cong&\mathrm{\mathbf{R}Hom}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}({\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{A}}^{e})\\
\cong&\Theta_{{\mathcal{B}}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{e}}{\mathcal{A}}^{e}\\
\cong&\mathcal{A}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}}\Theta_{{\mathcal{B}}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}}\mathcal{A},\end{split}

where
\Theta_{{\mathcal{B}}}=\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{B}^{e}}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{B}^{e})
. Here we use the smoothness of
{\mathcal{B}}
in the third isomorphism.

Then we have

\begin{split}X^{\wedge}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{A}}}\Sigma^{n+1}(\mathcal{A}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}}\Theta_{{\mathcal{B}}}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}}\mathcal{A})&\cong(X^{\wedge}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}}\Theta_{{\mathcal{B}}})\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}}\Sigma^{n+1}\mathcal{A}\\
&\cong\mathbb{S}_{B_{0}}^{-1}(\Sigma^{n+1}X)^{\wedge}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}}\mathcal{A}.\end{split}

Fix a minimal injective resolution of
X

0\to X\to I^{0}\to I^{1}\cdots\to I^{n}\to 0.

Then
\mathbb{S}_{B_{0}}^{-1}(\Sigma^{n+1}X)=\nu_{B_{0}}^{-1}(I^{\bullet})
is the following complex

0\to P_{0}\to P_{1}\cdots\to P_{n}\to 0,

where
P_{i}
is in degree
i-n-1
and
P_{i}=\nu_{B_{0}}^{-1}(I^{i})\in\mathrm{proj}B_{0}
. After applying the functor

?\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}}\mathcal{A}\colon\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{B})\to\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}),

we get

0\to P_{0}^{\wedge}\to P_{1}^{\wedge}\to\cdots\to P_{n}^{\wedge}\to 0,

where
P_{i}^{\wedge}=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{mod}B_{0}}(?,P_{i})|_{\mathcal{A}}\in\mathrm{proj}(\mathcal{A})
.

Thus the image of
X^{\wedge}
under the functor
?\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{A}}}\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}^{e}}(\mathcal{A}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{B}}}\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}^{e})\colon\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})
is

0\to P_{0}^{\wedge}\to P_{1}^{\wedge}\to\cdots\to P_{n}^{\wedge}\to 0,

where
P_{i}^{\wedge}=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{mod}B_{0}}(?,P_{i})|_{\mathcal{A}}\in\mathrm{proj}(\mathcal{A})
.

Step 2. We compute the image of
X^{\wedge}
under the functor
?\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{A}}}\Sigma^{n+1}\Theta_{{\mathcal{A}}}=\Sigma^{n+1}\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{A}}^{-1}\colon\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})
.

We have the minimal injective resolution in Step 1

0\to X\to I^{0}\to I^{1}\cdots\to I^{n}\to 0.

Then
\Sigma^{n+1}\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{A}}^{-1}(X^{\wedge})
is the following complex

0\to(?,X)\to(?,I^{0})\to(?,I^{1})\cdots\to(?,I^{n}).

For each
1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1
, the cohomology at
(?,I^{i})
is
\mathrm{Ext}^{i}_{B_{0}}(?,X)=0
because
\mathcal{A}
is an
n
-rigid subcategory of
\mathrm{mod}B_{0}
. The cohomology at
(?,I^{n})
is
\mathrm{Ext}^{n}_{B_{0}}(?,X)
. For any object
L
in
\mathrm{mod}B_{0}
, we have

\begin{split}D\mathrm{Ext}^{n}_{B_{0}}(L,X)&\simeq D\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(B_{0})}(L,\Sigma^{n}X)\\
&\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(B_{0})}(\Sigma^{n}X,\mathbb{S}_{B_{0}}(L))\\
&\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(B_{0})}(\Sigma^{n}(\mathbb{S}_{B_{0}}^{-1}X),L)\\
&\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(B_{0})}(H^{0}(\Sigma^{n}(\mathbb{S}_{B_{0}}^{-1}X)),L)\\
&\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(B_{0})}(\tau_{n}^{-1}X,L)\\
&\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{B_{0}}(\tau_{n}^{-1}X,L),\end{split}

where the fourth equivalence follows from
\mathbb{S}_{B_{0}}^{-1}=\mathbf{R}\mathrm{Hom}_{B_{0}}(DB_{0},?)
and
\mathrm{gldim}(B_{0})\leqslant n
. Then the cohomology at
(?,I^{n})
is isomorphic to
D\mathrm{Hom}_{B_{0}}(\tau_{n}^{-1}X,?)
.

Since we have isomorphisms
(?,I^{i})\simeq D(P_{i},?)
for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1
, we get the following injective resolution of
(?,X)

0\to(?,X)\to D(P_{0},?)\to D(P_{1},?)\cdots\to D(P_{n},?)\to D(\tau_{n}^{-1}X,?)\to 0.

Applying the functor
\Sigma^{n+1}\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{A}}^{-1}\colon{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{A}})\rightarrow{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{A}})
to the above complex, we get a complex

0\to(?,P_{0})\to(?,P_{1})\to\cdots\to(?,P_{n})\to(?,\tau_{n}^{-1}X)\to 0,

where
(?,P_{i})
is in degree
i-n-1
and
(?,\tau_{n}^{-1}X)
is in degree 0. This is because
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{A}}^{-1}(D(M,?))=\nu_{\mathrm{mod}\mathcal{A}}^{-1}(D(M,?))=(?,M)
for any object
M
in
\mathcal{A}
.

Step 3.
From the computations in step 1 and step 2, the object
X^{\wedge}\overset{\rm\textbf{L}}{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{A}}}\Sigma^{n+1}\Theta_{f_{0}}
is equal to the homotopy fiber of the following morphism of complexes

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(?,P_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
